Moduli space of optimization algorithms
Dmitry Pasechnyuk-Vilensky, Martin Takáč

Abstract
We introduce a geometric and operator–theoretic formalism viewing optimization algorithms as discrete connections on a space of update operators. Each iterative method is encoded by two coupled channels—drift and diffusion—whose algebraic curvature measures the deviation from ideal reversibility and determines the attainable order of accuracy. Flat connections correspond to methods whose updates commute up to higher order and thus achieve minimal numerical dissipation while preserving stability. The formalism recovers classical gradient, proximal, and momentum schemes as first–order flat cases and extends naturally to stochastic, preconditioned, and adaptive algorithms through perturbations controlled by curvature order. Explicit gauge corrections yield higher–order variants with guaranteed energy monotonicity and noise stability. The associated determinantal and isomonodromic formulations yield exact nonasymptotic bounds and describe acceleration effects via Painlevé-type invariants and Stokes corrections.



Introduction

Optimization algorithms are typically described either by their update rules or by their convergence rates. Between these two levels—local algebraic structure and global analytic behavior—there has long been a conceptual gap. Step–size schedules, preconditioners, or momentum terms are often justified empirically, while rigorous analyses rely on method–specific Lyapunov arguments. As a result, the common geometric or spectral principles underlying stability and acceleration have remained opaque.

We aim to provide a consistent mathematical framework that allows comparing and classifying iterative methods within a shared geometric setting.

Objectives.

Our study is organized around six complementary objectives.

1.

Local–to–global correspondence.

We establish a precise relation between the local structure of an iteration and its global convergence behavior.
The order to which elementary updates commute determines the attainable accuracy and the minimal energy dissipation of the method.
This provides a rigorous criterion linking discrete approximation order to actual stability and rate.

2.

Universal minimax principle.

We identify a general optimization principle valid for all first– and higher–order discrete schemes:
minimizing the total trajectory error is equivalent to an extremal approximation of the exponential map.
The resulting optimal policies coincide with the classical Chebyshev and Zolotarev filters, giving acceleration a precise variational meaning.

3.

Spectral–energetic equivalence.

We connect the analytic energy of an iteration to its spectral invariants.
The variation of the energy functional is shown to coincide with that of a regularized determinant of the underlying evolution operator, ensuring that stability and convergence can be expressed in purely spectral terms.

4.

Isomonodromic structure of discrete optimization.

We show that families of iterative methods admit an isomonodromic form:
their energy potential is a
\tau
–function satisfying a Painlevé–type differential identity.
This structure explains why accelerated and stabilized schemes exhibit phase–preserving dynamics and yields nonasymptotic bounds exact up to Stokes–type corrections.

5.

Geometry of adaptive and stochastic methods.

Adaptive, stochastic, and preconditioned algorithms are described as controlled geometric deformations of the same flat structure that governs deterministic schemes.
All admissible perturbations preserve flatness up to a prescribed order and share the same spectral class, providing a unified explanation of robustness and variance control.

6.

Continuous–discrete correspondence.

We establish a spectral–variational correspondence between continuous and discrete formulations.
The discrete curvature functional introduced here converges, as the step size tends to zero, to its continuous Yang–Mills counterpart, linking iterative algorithms and continuous gradient flows within a single analytic framework.

Together, these results outline a coherent picture of optimization as a geometric theory of discrete dynamics:
each algorithm corresponds to a point in a moduli space whose curvature encodes its accuracy, energy, and stability.
Flatness—vanishing curvature—marks the ideal regime where updates are reversible, dissipation is minimal, and convergence is optimal.

1
Definitions

Let
\mathbb{R}^{n}
be the state space,
X\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
be a feasible manifold,
K\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
be a closed convex feasible set. Let
f:X\to\mathbb{R}
be the function.

Let
\{U\}\subset\End(X)
be the set of elementary operators to update the state. Let the exact algorithm be defined as
x_{k+1}=S(h_{k},\{U\})x_{k}
, where
S(h,\{U\})\in\End(X)
and satisfies the following assumptions:

1.

S(0,\{U\})=\mathrm{id}

2.

S(h_{1}+h_{2},\{U\})=S(h_{1},\{U\})\circ S(h_{2},\{U\})

3.

\exists\Omega(h,\{U\})\in\mathfrak{L}\langle U\rangle:\;S(h,\{U\})=\exp(\Omega(h,\{U\}))
,
\Omega(h,\{U\})=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}h^{k}\Omega_{k}(\{U\})

4.

S(\cdot,\{U\})
is continuously differentiable at
h=0
.
A(\{U\})=\partial_{h}S(\cdot,\{U\})|_{h=0}
is the generator of evolution,

where
\mathfrak{L}\langle U\rangle
is a free Lie algebra
[
99
]
.

Example 1
.

S
has closed form if family
\{U\}
is simple:

1.

If
\{U\}=\{A\}
, where
A
is a monotone operator,
S(h,\{U\})=(\mathrm{id}+hA)^{-1}

2.

If
\{U\}=\{H\}
, where
H
is a linear operator,
S(h,\{U\})=e^{-hH}

[
57
]

Let
\mathfrak{U}=\langle U\rangle
be the algebra of operators to update the state with the following axioms:

1.

\forall U_{1},U_{2}\in\mathfrak{U}:\;U_{1}\circ U_{2}\in\mathfrak{U}

2.

\forall U_{1},U_{2}\in\mathfrak{U}\;\forall a,b\in\mathbb{R}:\;aU_{1}+bU_{2}\in\mathfrak{U}

3.

\forall U\in\mathfrak{U}\;\forall a\in\mathbb{R}:
if
(\mathrm{id}+aU)
is invertible in
\End(X)
, then
(\mathrm{id}+aU_{1})^{-1}\in\mathfrak{U}

4.

for any Lipschitz
\theta\mapsto U_{\theta}
and probability distribution
\pi(\theta)
,
\int U_{\theta}d\pi(\theta)\in\mathfrak{U}

5.

if
U_{k}\to U
and
U_{k}\in\mathfrak{U}
, then
U\in\mathfrak{U}

Let the feasible spectrum
\Sigma\subset\mathbb{R}
be compact. For
t\in\mathcal{T}
(which will be defined in the next section),
r_{t}(h,U)\in\mathfrak{U}
is the drift update, for
s\in\mathcal{S}
,
m_{s}(h,U)\in\mathfrak{U}
is the diffusion update. Let
U_{s,t}=m_{s}\circ r_{t}=r_{t}\circ m_{s}
(the correctness will be further proven) be the update and define the practical algorithm as
x_{k+1}=U_{t,s}(h_{k},U)x_{k}
, where
U=U(x_{k})
serves as the oracle.

1.1
Hierarchies

Let the feasible spectrum
\Sigma\subset\mathbb{C}
be compact, and
\tilde{\mathfrak{U}}\subset\mathfrak{U}
is subalgebra of implementable update operators. The drift hierarchy
\mathcal{T}
is a set of
t=(r,\alpha)
, where
r\in\tilde{\mathfrak{U}}
, and
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
is such that
r(0,U)=\mathrm{id}
and

\sup_{\lambda\in\Sigma}|e^{-h\lambda}-r(h,\lambda)|\leq Ch^{\alpha}

Example 2
.

Implementable update operators are approximation methods

1.

truncated Taylor series
[
57
,
111
]
:

r_{p}(h,\lambda)=\sum_{k=0}^{p}\frac{(-h\lambda)^{k}}{k!},\ \alpha=p+1,\ \sum_{\lambda\in\Sigma}|e^{-h\lambda}-r_{p}(h,\lambda)|=O(h^{p+1})
.

2.

Padé [1/1] (Cayley step)
[
8
,
57
,
54
,
56
]
:

r_{[1/1]}(h,\lambda)=\frac{1-\tfrac{h\lambda}{2}}{1+\tfrac{h\lambda}{2}},\ \alpha=3,\ \sum_{\lambda\in\Sigma}|e^{-h\lambda}-r_{[1/1]}(h,\lambda)|=O(h^{3})
.

3.

Padé [n/n]
[
8
,
57
]
:

r_{[n/n]}(h,\lambda)=\dfrac{P_{n}(h\lambda)}{Q_{n}(h\lambda)},\ \alpha=2n+1,\ \sum_{\lambda\in\Sigma}|e^{-h\lambda}-r_{[n/n]}(h,\lambda)|=O(h^{2n+1})
.

4.

Chebyshev polynomial of degree
p

[
100
,
111
]
:

r^{(Ch)}_{p}(h,\lambda)=\sum_{k=0}^{p}c_{k}(h)\lambda^{k},\ \alpha=p+1,\ \sum_{\lambda\in\Sigma}|e^{-h\lambda}-r^{(Ch)}_{p}(h,\lambda)|=O(h^{p+1})
.

\mathcal{T}
is equipped with partial order: if
r_{1}
and
r_{2}
are of the same type,
t_{1}=(r_{1},\alpha_{1})\leq t_{2}=(r_{2},\alpha_{2})\Leftrightarrow\alpha_{1}\leq\alpha_{2}
.

Let obstruction channel be
\sigma=(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{E},\Gamma)
, where
\mathcal{U}
is a convex set of admissible perturbations
u:\mathbb{R}_{+}\to X
,
\mathcal{E}
is a convex set of correction maps
E:X\to X
modelling systematic deviations, and
\Gamma
is a nonnegative gauge on pairs
(u,E)
specifying admissibility (we only use
(u,E)
with
\Gamma(u,E)\leq 1
). We associate the following update operator with each
\sigma
:

d_{\sigma}(h,U)=\mathbb{E}_{u\in\mathcal{U}}\big[(\mathrm{id}+hE)^{-1}\circ\Phi(h,U;u)\big]\ \in\ \mathfrak{U},\quad\Gamma(u,E)\leq 1,

where
\Phi(h,U;u)\in\mathfrak{U}
is the transfer operator satisfying
\Phi(h,U;0)=U
and
\Phi(h,U;u)
locally Lipschitz in
(h,U,u)
.

Let
I
be the index set, with some
\sigma
-algebra, which indexes channels
\{\sigma_{i}\}_{i\in I}
.
The diffusion hierarchy
\mathcal{S}
is a set of
s=(\nu,\beta)
, where
\nu
is a nonnegative measure on
I
and

\beta:I\to[0,\infty)
is density such that
\int_{I}\beta(i)\,d\nu(i)=1
.
The associated update operator is the mixture

d_{s}(h,U)\ :=\ \int_{I}\beta(i)\,d_{\sigma_{i}}(h,U)\,d\nu(i)\ \in\ \mathfrak{U},

such that
d_{s}(0,U)=\mathrm{id}

[
115
]
.

Example 3
.

\mathcal{S}
usually describes noise models:

1.

Square–integrable noise
[
115
,
104
]
:

\mathcal{U}=\{u:\mathbb{E}u=0,\ \mathbb{E}\|u\|^{2}<\infty\}
,

\mathcal{E}=\{0\}
,

\Gamma(u,0)=\mathbb{E}\|u\|^{2}
,

\Phi(h,U;u)=U\circ(\mathrm{id}+h\,u)
.

2.

Heavy–tail noise (
1<\beta<2
)
[
115
,
104
]
:

\mathcal{U}=\{u:\|u\|_{\psi_{\beta}}\leq 1\}
,

\mathcal{E}=\{0\}
,

\Gamma(u,0)=\|u\|_{\psi_{\beta}}
,

\Phi(h,U;u)=U\circ(\mathrm{id}+h\,u)
.

3.

Solver inexactness:

\mathcal{U}=\{\delta_{0}\}
,

\mathcal{E}=\{E:\|(\mathrm{id}+hE)^{-1}-(\mathrm{id}+hH)^{-1}\|\leq Ch^{\alpha}\}
,

\Gamma(\delta_{0},E)=\|E-H\|
,

\Phi(h,U;0)=U
.

4.

Linear distortion (calibration error):

\mathcal{U}=\{\delta_{0}\}
,

\mathcal{E}=\{E_{\Delta}:x\mapsto\Delta x,\ \|\Delta\|\leq\rho\}
,

\Gamma(\delta_{0},E_{\Delta})=\|\Delta\|/\rho
,

\Phi(h,U;0)=U
.

5.

Biased observation:

\mathcal{U}=\{u:\|\mathbb{E}u\|\leq\varepsilon,\ \Var(u)\leq\sigma^{2}\}
,

\mathcal{E}=\{0\}
,

\Gamma(u,0)=\|\mathbb{E}u\|+\Var(u)
,

\Phi(h,U;u)=U\circ(\mathrm{id}+h\,u)
.

6.

Model–homotopy:

\mathcal{U}=\{\delta_{0}\}
,

\mathcal{E}=\{0\}
,

\Gamma(\delta_{0},0)=1
,

\Phi(h,U)=U_{\varepsilon}
, where
U_{\varepsilon}
is the same update rule
constructed for the deformed model
F_{\varepsilon}=\varepsilon F+(1-\varepsilon)F_{0}
,
\varepsilon\in[0,1]
.

Let
\mathfrak{U}^{\mathrm{fil}}\subset\mathfrak{U}
be the smallest subalgebra
(closed under composition and inversion) that contains all coefficients

r_{t,k}(U)
and
d_{s,k}(U)
from the local expansions

r_{t}(h,U)=\mathrm{id}+\sum_{k\geq 1}h^{k}r_{t,k}(U),\qquad d_{s}(h,U)=\mathrm{id}+\sum_{k\geq 1}h^{k}d_{s,k}(U).

Define a filtration on formal series with coefficients in
\mathfrak{U}^{\mathrm{fil}}
by
[
99
,
42
,
78
]

\mathfrak{F}_{(m)}=\Big\{\ \mathrm{id}+\sum_{k\geq m}h^{k}W_{k}\ :\ W_{k}\in\mathfrak{U}^{\mathrm{fil}}\ \Big\},\qquad m\geq 1.

Then
(\mathfrak{F}_{(m)})_{m\geq 1}
is decreasing and compatible with composition:

(\mathrm{id}+O(h^{m}))\circ(\mathrm{id}+O(h^{n}))=\mathrm{id}+O(h^{m+n}),\quad\mathfrak{F}_{(m)}\circ\mathfrak{F}_{(n)}\subseteq\mathfrak{F}_{(m+n)}.

Consequently the completion

\widehat{\mathfrak{U}}=\varprojlim_{m}\big(\mathrm{id}+\sum_{k<m}h^{k}\mathfrak{U}^{\mathrm{fil}}\big)

is pronilpotent
[
99
]
, and every element
V\in\widehat{\mathfrak{U}}
with
V=\mathrm{id}+O(h)

admits a (unique, formal) logarithm and exponential in the completed free Lie algebra
with coefficients in
\mathfrak{U}^{\mathrm{fil}}
.

The automonodromic structure
of the practical algorithm is the composition

U_{t,s}(h,U):=d_{s}(h,U)\circ r_{t}(h,U)\in\mathfrak{U},

1.2
Universality

Let
X
be a finite–dimensional real Hilbert space with inner product
\langle\cdot\rangle{\cdot}
and induced Frobenius pairing
\langle A\rangle{B}:=\Tr(A^{\ast}B)
on
\End(X)
; let
m\in\mathbb{N}
,
Y:=X^{m}
, and
h\in\mathbb{R}
. Let
\theta
be a finite tuple of algorithmic parameters (stepsizes, momenta, penalties, preconditioners, filters, stochastic weights). An
m
–step rule

x_{k+1}=\Phi_{\theta}(h;\,x_{k},\dots,x_{k-m+1})

is lifted to a one–step map
\mathcal{S}(h;\theta):Y\to Y
by
[
57
]

\mathcal{S}(h;\theta)\!\begin{bmatrix}x_{k}\\
x_{k-1}\\
\vdots\\
x_{k-m+1}\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}\Phi_{\theta}(h;\,x_{k},\dots,x_{k-m+1})\\
x_{k}\\
\vdots\\
x_{k-m+2}\end{bmatrix}.

Fix a base state
y_{\ast}\in Y
. Assume Fréchet differentiability in
y
at
y_{\ast}
and differentiability in
h
at
h=0
. Define the linearized one–step operator and its
h
–derivative

S(h;\theta):=D\mathcal{S}(h;\theta)\big|_{y_{\ast}}\in\End(Y),\qquad S(0;\theta)=\Id_{Y},

G(\theta):=\partial_{h}S(h;\theta)\big|_{h=0}\in\End(Y).

Let the involution
A\mapsto A^{\ast}
be the adjoint in the Frobenius pairing on
\End(Y)
.

Define

H(\theta):=\tfrac{1}{2}\!\big(G(\theta)+G(\theta)^{\ast}\big),\qquad K(\theta):=\tfrac{1}{2}\!\big(G(\theta)-G(\theta)^{\ast}\big).

Then
H(\theta)^{\ast}=H(\theta)
,
K(\theta)^{\ast}=-K(\theta)
,
G(\theta)=H(\theta)+K(\theta)
, and the split is unique
[
57
]
. By Baker–Campbell–Hausdorff for
h\to 0
,

S(h;\theta)=\exp\!\big(hH(\theta)\big)\,\exp\!\big(hK(\theta)\big)+\mathcal{O}(h^{2}).

For any
y\in Y
,

\frac{\,\mathrm{d}}{\,\mathrm{d}h}\Big|_{h=0}\lVert e^{hH(\theta)}y\rVert^{2}=2\,\langle H(\theta)y\rangle{y},\qquad\frac{\,\mathrm{d}}{\,\mathrm{d}h}\Big|_{h=0}\lVert e^{hK(\theta)}y\rVert^{2}=2\,\langle K(\theta)y\rangle{y}=0.

Hence
K(\theta)
generates an isometric flow to first order, while
H(\theta)
is dissipative to first order whenever
\langle H(\theta)y\rangle{y}\leq 0
; in particular, if
-H(\theta)
is positive semidefinite (e.g., after absorbing a stepsize into
h
), then
\lVert e^{hH(\theta)}y\rVert\leq\lVert y\rVert+\mathcal{O}(h^{2})
(nonexpansive semigroup at first order). Thus
\exp(hK(\theta))
is the
drift
(conservative to first order) and
\exp(hH(\theta))
the
diffusion
(dissipative/nonexpansive to first order).

If
\Phi_{\theta}
is differentiable in
(x_{k},\dots,x_{k-m+1})
at
y_{\ast}
with block Jacobians
B_{i}(\theta):=\partial_{x_{k-i}}\Phi_{\theta}(0;\,y_{\ast})\in\End(X)
,
i=0,\dots,m-1
, then

S(0;\theta)=\begin{bmatrix}B_{0}(\theta)&B_{1}(\theta)&\cdots&B_{m-1}(\theta)\\
I&0&\cdots&0\\
\vdots&\ddots&\ddots&\vdots\\
0&\cdots&I&0\end{bmatrix},

G(\theta)=\partial_{h}S(0;\theta)=\begin{bmatrix}\dot{B}_{0}(\theta)&\dot{B}_{1}(\theta)&\cdots&\dot{B}_{m-1}(\theta)\\
0&\cdots&\cdots&0\\
\vdots&\ddots&\ddots&\vdots\\
0&\cdots&0&0\end{bmatrix},

where dots denote
h
–derivatives at
0
. All hyperparameters
\theta
(steps, momenta, penalties, preconditioners, EMA rates) enter linearly into
G(\theta)
through these blocks; the canonical map
\Theta\to(H(\theta),K(\theta))
is given by the adjoint split of
G(\theta)
in
\End(Y)
.

Let
A(\theta):Y\rightrightarrows Y
be maximally monotone and
\alpha(\theta)>0
. The resolvent
J_{\alpha hA}:=(\Id_{Y}+\alpha(\theta)hA(\theta))^{-1}
is firmly nonexpansive and admits the expansion
[
86
,
12
,
88
]

J_{\alpha hA}=\Id_{Y}-\alpha(\theta)h\,A_{h}(\theta)+\mathcal{O}(h^{2}),

where
A_{h}(\theta)
is the Yosida approximation (single–valued, monotone, Lipschitz)
[
12
,
20
]
. Setting

D_{s}(h,\theta):=J_{\alpha hA},\qquad r_{t}(h,\theta):=\exp\!\big(hK(\theta)\big),

yields

S(h;\theta)=D_{s}(h,\theta)\,r_{t}(h,\theta)+\mathcal{O}(h^{2}),\qquad D_{s}(h,\theta)=\exp\!\big(-\alpha(\theta)h\,A_{h}(\theta)\big)+\mathcal{O}(h^{2}),

so that, to first order, the diffusion channel is the resolvent (firmly nonexpansive), and the drift channel is isometric
[
68
]
. Different orders
D_{s}\circ r_{t}
vs
r_{t}\circ D_{s}
agree at
\mathcal{O}(h)
, their difference is
\mathcal{O}(h^{2})

[
57
]
.

The assignment

\Theta\ \longrightarrow\ \big(H(\theta),\,K(\theta),\,A(\theta)\big)

collects: metric, preconditioning, penalties, regularization, smoothing into
H
or
A
; inertial/momentum/extrapolation couplings (in
Y=X^{m}
) into
K
; stochastic weights into first–order variations of
H
(and into the choice of
A
when clipping/prox is used). This provides local coordinates on the moduli of methods at
y_{\ast}
; the factorization is unique up to
\mathcal{O}(h^{2})
and commuting central corrections
[
102
,
34
]
.

Let
\mathbf{Alg}^{(1)}
have objects
(Y,G)
,
G\in\End(Y)
, and morphisms
(Y,G)\xrightarrow{T}(Y^{\prime},G^{\prime})
given by linear isomorphisms
T:Y\to Y^{\prime}
with
G^{\prime}=TGT^{-1}
. Let
\mathbf{TwoCh}
have objects
(Y;H,K)
with
H^{\ast}=H
,
K^{\ast}=-K
, and morphisms
(Y;H,K)\xrightarrow{T}(Y^{\prime};H^{\prime},K^{\prime})
given by
T
with
H^{\prime}=THT^{-1}
,
K^{\prime}=TKT^{-1}
. Define

U:\mathbf{TwoCh}\to\mathbf{Alg}^{(1)},\qquad U(Y;H,K):=(Y,H+K),\quad U(T):=T.

For each
(Y,G)
define

\mathrm{split}(Y,G):=\big(Y;\tfrac{1}{2}(G+G^{\ast}),\,\tfrac{1}{2}(G-G^{\ast})\big).

Then for every
(Y,G)
there exists a unique morphism

\eta_{(Y,G)}:(Y,G)\longrightarrow U\!\big(\mathrm{split}(Y,G)\big),\qquad\eta_{(Y,G)}=\Id_{Y},

and for any
(Y^{\prime};H^{\prime},K^{\prime})
and any
\phi:(Y,G)\to U(Y^{\prime};H^{\prime},K^{\prime})
there exists a unique
\tilde{\phi}:\mathrm{split}(Y,G)\to(Y^{\prime};H^{\prime},K^{\prime})
with
U(\tilde{\phi})\circ\eta_{(Y,G)}=\phi
. Hence
\mathrm{split}:\mathbf{Alg}^{(1)}\to\mathbf{TwoCh}
is left adjoint to
U
; the decomposition
G=H+K
is the canonical two–channel normal form of any linearized multistep generator, and the associated drift/diffusion channels are characterized by first–order isometry/nonexpansiveness as above.

2
Automonodromic Theory

2.1
Methods

2.1.1
Flat Connection

For any
\pi=(t,s),\pi^{\prime}=(t^{\prime},s^{\prime})\in\mathcal{P}
define
the discrete transport operators
[
99
,
53
]

R(t\!\to\!t^{\prime};h)=r_{t^{\prime}}(h,U)\,r_{t}(h,U)^{-1},\qquad D(s\!\to\!s^{\prime};h)=d_{s^{\prime}}(h,U)\,d_{s}(h,U)^{-1}.

The holonomy of the elementary rectangle
(t,s)\to(t^{\prime},s^{\prime})

is the composition

\mathrm{Hol}(t,s;t^{\prime},s^{\prime};h):=R(t\!\to\!t^{\prime};h)\,D(s\!\to\!s^{\prime};h)\,R(t\!\to\!t^{\prime};h)^{-1}\,D(s\!\to\!s^{\prime};h)^{-1}\in\mathfrak{U}.

The automonodromic structure is flat of class
\alpha

if the discrete holonomy around any infinitesimal rectangle is identity up to
O(h^{\alpha})

[
53
,
16
]
,

\|\mathrm{Hol}(t,s;t^{\prime},s^{\prime};h)-\mathrm{id}\|=O(h^{\alpha})\quad\text{for}\quad(t^{\prime},s^{\prime})\to(t,s),\ h\to 0.

Equivalently, the elementary updates commute
up to the same order:

r_{t}(h,U)\,d_{s}(h,U)=d_{s}(h,U)\,r_{t}(h,U)+O(h^{\alpha}).

On the other hand,
\widehat{\mathfrak{U}}
is pronilpotent
[
99
]
, thus
r_{t}(h,U)
and
d_{s}(h,U)
possess a
formal logarithm in the completed free Lie algebra
[
99
]

\widehat{\mathfrak{L}}\langle U\rangle
:

r_{t}(h,U)=\exp(\Omega_{t}(h)),\qquad d_{s}(h,U)=\exp(\Psi_{s}(h)),

where
\Omega_{t}(h),\Psi_{s}(h)
are formal series

\Omega_{t}(h)=\sum_{k=1}^{\alpha}h^{k}\Omega_{t,k}
,

\Psi_{s}(h)=\sum_{k=1}^{\alpha}h^{k}\Psi_{s,k}
.
These logarithms are
O(h^{\alpha+1})
classes.

The connection is flat of jet-class
\alpha
if
all mixed Lie brackets in the BCH expansion vanish up to order
\alpha

[
9
,
55
,
42
,
78
,
99
]
:

[\Omega_{t,k},\Psi_{s,\ell}]=0,\qquad k+\ell\leq\alpha.

Example 4
.

For many admissible update schemes in the drift and diffusion hierarchies,
the formal logarithms
\Omega_{t}(h),\Psi_{s}(h)
exist in
\widehat{\mathfrak{L}}\langle U\rangle
.

1.

Drift updates.

(a)

Taylor or polynomial truncations
[
57
,
23
]
:

r_{p}(h,\lambda)=\sum_{k=0}^{p}(-h\lambda)^{k}/k!

are partial sums of
\exp(-h\lambda)
, hence

\log r_{p}(h,\lambda)=-h\lambda+O(h^{p+1})
.

(b)

Padé and Cayley rational forms
[
8
,
57
,
23
]
:

r_{[m/n]}(h,\lambda)=P_{m}(h\lambda)/Q_{n}(h\lambda)

are rational approximations of
e^{-h\lambda}
,
so
\log r_{[m/n]}(h,\lambda)=-h\lambda+O(h^{m+n+1})
.

(c)

Resolvent or proximal steps
[
12
]
:

r(h,A)=(\mathrm{id}+hA)^{-1}
satisfy

\log r(h,A)=-\log(\mathrm{id}+hA)=-hA+\tfrac{1}{2}h^{2}A^{2}-\dots
.

2.

Diffusion updates.

(a)

Stochastic or Orlicz channels
[
115
]
:

d_{s}(h,U)=\mathrm{id}+hE+O(h^{2})
, thus
\log d_{s}(h,U)=hE+O(h^{2})
.

(b)

Model-homotopy:

d_{s}(h,U)=U_{\varepsilon}=\mathrm{id}+hE_{\varepsilon}+O(h^{2})
,
so
\log d_{s}(h,U)=hE_{\varepsilon}+O(h^{2})
.

In each case
r_{t},d_{s}
are invertible and
near the identity in the filtration by
h
,
hence formal logarithms exist in the completed Lie algebra
and are unique modulo
O(h^{\alpha+1})
.

The following theorem states that class
\alpha
and jet-class
\alpha
coincide.

Theorem 1

(Holonomy flatness
\Leftrightarrow
jet flatness)
.

Work in the filtered subalgebra
\mathfrak{U}^{\mathrm{fil}}\subset\mathfrak{U}
generated by the update families

r_{t}(h,\cdot),d_{s}(h,\cdot)
and their coefficients.
Assume
r_{t}(h,U)=\exp(\Omega(h))
,
d_{s}(h,U)=\exp(\Psi(h))
with

\Omega(h)=\sum_{k=1}^{\alpha}h^{k}\,\Omega_{k},\qquad\Psi(h)=\sum_{k=1}^{\alpha}h^{k}\,\Psi_{k},\qquad\Omega_{k},\Psi_{k}\in\widehat{\mathfrak{L}}\langle U\rangle,

understood modulo
O(h^{\alpha+1})
.
Then for any fixed
(t,s)
the following are equivalent:

(H)

Holonomy flatness of class
\alpha
:

\mathrm{Hol}(t,s;t^{\prime},s^{\prime};h)=\exp(\Psi(h))\,\exp(\Omega(h))\,\exp(-\Psi(h))\,\exp(-\Omega(h))=\mathrm{id}+O(h^{\alpha})

for
(t^{\prime},s^{\prime})\to(t,s)
(so that the same
\Omega,\Psi
are used) and
h\to 0
;
equivalently,
\log\mathrm{Hol}(t,s;t^{\prime},s^{\prime};h)=O(h^{\alpha})
.

(J)

Jet flatness of order
\alpha
:

all mixed Lie brackets up to total order
\alpha-1
vanish:

[\Omega_{k},\Psi_{\ell}]=0\qquad\text{for all }k,\ell\geq 1\ \text{ with }\ k+\ell\leq\alpha-1.

Consequently, under
(H)
(or
(J)
),

\exp(\Psi(h))\,\exp(\Omega(h))=\exp(\Omega(h))\,\exp(\Psi(h))=\exp\big(\Omega(h)+\Psi(h)\big)\ \text{mod }O(h^{\alpha}).

Proof.

All equalities are in the completed filtered algebra and understood modulo
O(h^{\alpha})
.

Step 0 (notations and grading).

Give degree
\deg(h^{m}X)=m
to a homogeneous term.
If
A(h)=\sum_{m\geq 1}h^{m}A_{m}
and
B(h)=\sum_{m\geq 1}h^{m}B_{m}
, then

\deg([A_{k},B_{\ell}])=k+\ell
and every Lie polynomial built from

\{A_{1},\dots,A_{m-1},B_{1},\dots,B_{m-1}\}
and at least one bracket has degree at least
2
.

Step 1 (BCH for two factors, truncated).

The BCH series in a filtered (pronilpotent) Lie algebra is a
formal
identity:

\log\big(\exp X\,\exp Y\big)=X+Y+\frac{1}{2}[X,Y]+\frac{1}{12}\big([X,[X,Y]]+[Y,[Y,X]]\big)+\cdots,

where every omitted term has degree at least
3
in
X,Y
.
Truncating modulo
O(h^{\alpha})
is legitimate because degrees strictly increase with each extra bracket.

Step 2 (logarithm of the holonomy).

Set
X=\Psi(h)
and
Y=\Omega(h)
. Then

\mathrm{Hol}=\exp X\,\exp Y\,\exp(-X)\,\exp(-Y).

Apply BCH twice:
first
Z_{1}:=\log(\exp X\,\exp Y)
, then
Z:=\log\big(\exp Z_{1}\,\exp(-X)\big)
, and finally

\log\mathrm{Hol}=\log\big(\exp Z\,\exp(-Y)\big)
.
By Step 1, each stage produces a Lie series whose degree-
m
term is a
universal Lie polynomial

in
X,Y
with total degree
m
. Crucially, the degree-
m
coefficient of
\log\mathrm{Hol}
has the form

\big(\log\mathrm{Hol}\big)^{[m]}=\sum_{k+\ell=m}[\Psi_{k},\Omega_{\ell}]\;+\;\mathcal{P}_{m}(\{\Psi_{<m},\Omega_{<m}\}),

(1)

where
\mathcal{P}_{m}
is a (finite)
\mathbb{Q}
–linear combination of nested brackets each involving
only

indices
<m
and with total bracket-depth at least
2
.
This structure is obtained by direct inspection of BCH:
the
[X,Y]
term contributes exactly
\sum_{k+\ell=m}[\Psi_{k},\Omega_{\ell}]
at degree
m
,
while all higher BCH terms necessarily involve at least two brackets and therefore
only lower-degree components
\Psi_{<m},\Omega_{<m}
to reach total degree
m
.

Step 3 (
\text{(J)}\Rightarrow\text{(H)}
).

Assume (J). Then for every
m\leq\alpha-1
, the sum
\sum_{k+\ell=m}[\Omega_{k},\Psi_{\ell}]=0
.
In (
1
), each
\mathcal{P}_{m}
is a Lie polynomial in brackets of strictly lower total degree,
which vanish by the induction on
m
(base
m=2
:
\mathcal{P}_{2}\equiv 0
).
Hence
(\log\mathrm{Hol})^{[m]}=0
for all
m\leq\alpha-1
, i.e.
\log\mathrm{Hol}=O(h^{\alpha})
,
which is exactly (H).

Step 4 (
\text{(H)}\Rightarrow\text{(J)}
).

Assume (H). Then
(\log\mathrm{Hol})^{[m]}=0
for all
m\leq\alpha-1
.
We show by induction on
m
that
\sum_{k+\ell=m}[\Psi_{k},\Omega_{\ell}]=0
for
m\leq\alpha-1
,
and then deduce termwise vanishing
[\Psi_{k},\Omega_{\ell}]=0
for all
k+\ell\leq\alpha-1
.

For
m=2
we have, by (
1
),
(\log\mathrm{Hol})^{[2]}=[\Psi_{1},\Omega_{1}]=0
.

Suppose the claim holds for all degrees
<m
(with
m\leq\alpha-1
).
Then all brackets among
\Psi_{<m},\Omega_{<m}
vanish, hence
\mathcal{P}_{m}\equiv 0

in (
1
), and
(\log\mathrm{Hol})^{[m]}=\sum_{k+\ell=m}[\Psi_{k},\Omega_{\ell}]=0
.
Thus the
sum
of mixed brackets at total degree
m
vanishes.

To strengthen this to
termwise
vanishing, note that the
\{\Omega_{\ell}\}_{\ell\geq 1}
and

\{\Psi_{k}\}_{k\geq 1}
are the homogeneous components of fixed formal logarithms
\Omega(h),\Psi(h)
.
Fixing
m
and varying
(t,s)
within an arbitrarily small neighborhood changes the families

\{\Omega_{\ell}\},\{\Psi_{k}\}
independently (by the axioms from the Definitions section).
Since the identity
\sum_{k+\ell=m}[\Psi_{k},\Omega_{\ell}]=0
holds for all such pairs,
and the bracket is bilinear, each individual mixed bracket
[\Psi_{k},\Omega_{\ell}]
with
k+\ell=m

must vanish. (Indeed, choose variations where only one pair
(k,\ell)
is modified while others are held fixed.)
Hence
[\Psi_{k},\Omega_{\ell}]=0
whenever
k+\ell=m
.
By induction on
m
we obtain (J).

Step 5 (commutation and single-exponential form).

Under (J), the BCH series gives

\log\big(\exp(\Psi(h))\,\exp(\Omega(h))\big)=\Omega(h)+\Psi(h)\ \text{mod }O(h^{\alpha}),

hence
\exp(\Psi)\exp(\Omega)=\exp(\Omega)\exp(\Psi)=\exp(\Omega+\Psi)\ \text{mod }O(h^{\alpha})
,
as claimed.
∎

Theorem 2

(Gauge normal form under jet flatness)
.

Work in the filtered subalgebra
\mathfrak{U}^{\mathrm{fil}}\subset\mathfrak{U}
generated by the coefficients
of the admissible update families.
Let

r_{t}(h,U)=\exp(\Omega(h)),\qquad d_{s}(h,U)=\exp(\Psi(h)),

with formal logarithms

\Omega(h)=\sum_{k=1}^{\alpha}h^{k}\,\Omega_{k},\qquad\Psi(h)=\sum_{k=1}^{\alpha}h^{k}\,\Psi_{k},\qquad\Omega_{k},\Psi_{k}\in\widehat{\mathfrak{L}}\langle U\rangle,

understood modulo
O(h^{\alpha+1})
.
Assume
jet flatness of order
\alpha
:

[\Omega_{k},\Psi_{\ell}]=0\qquad\text{for all }k,\ell\geq 1\ \text{ with }\ k+\ell\leq\alpha.

(2)

Then there exists a gauge
[
16
]

W(h)=\exp\!\Big(\sum_{m=1}^{\alpha-1}h^{m}\,Z_{m}\Big)\ \in\ \exp\big(h\,\mathfrak{U}^{\mathrm{fil}}\big)

such that, for the conjugated pair

r_{t}^{W}(h):=W(h)^{-1}\,r_{t}(h,U)\,W(h)=\exp\big(\widetilde{\Omega}(h)\big),

d_{s}^{W}(h):=W(h)^{-1}\,d_{s}(h,U)\,W(h)=\exp\big(\widetilde{\Psi}(h)\big),

the following hold modulo
O(h^{\alpha+1})
:

(i)

Degreewise commutation:
for all
k,\ell\geq 1
with
k+\ell\leq\alpha
,

[\widetilde{\Omega}_{k},\widetilde{\Psi}_{\ell}]=0.

(ii)

Single-exponential composition:

d_{s}^{W}(h)\,r_{t}^{W}(h)=\exp\big(\widetilde{\Omega}(h)+\widetilde{\Psi}(h)\big).

Moreover,
W(h)
is unique up to multiplication by a central gauge

\exp(\sum_{m=1}^{\alpha-1}h^{m}C_{m})
with each
C_{m}
lying in the center of

\widehat{\mathfrak{U}^{\mathrm{fil}}}
modulo
O(h^{\alpha+1})
.

Proof.

All computations are formal in the pronilpotent completion and carried out modulo
O(h^{\alpha+1})
.
We construct
Z_{1},\dots,Z_{\alpha-1}
by induction on the degree.

Preliminaries.

Assign
\deg(h^{m}X)=m
. Conjugation by
W=\exp(h^{m-1}Z_{m-1})
acts on a logarithm
L(h)
as

L\ \mapsto\ L^{\prime}\ =\ e^{-\operatorname{ad}_{h^{m-1}Z_{m-1}}}L\ =\ L\ -\ [h^{m-1}Z_{m-1},L]\ +\ \tfrac{1}{2}[h^{m-1}Z_{m-1},[h^{m-1}Z_{m-1},L]]\ -\ \cdots.

Hence the
first
degree that changes under this conjugation is exactly
m
:
the leading variation is
-\,[Z_{m-1},L_{1}]\cdot h^{m}
, because
[h^{m-1}Z_{m-1},h^{k}L_{k}]
has degree
m-1+k
,
and
k=1
is minimal.

Inductive hypothesis.

Assume for some
m
with
2\leq m\leq\alpha
we have already chosen
Z_{1},\dots,Z_{m-2}
so that
after conjugation by
\exp(\sum_{q=1}^{m-2}h^{q}Z_{q})
the transformed logarithms

\Omega^{[<m]}(h)=\sum_{k=1}^{m-1}h^{k}\Omega_{k}^{[<m]},\ \Psi^{[<m]}(h)=\sum_{\ell=1}^{m-1}h^{\ell}\Psi_{\ell}^{[<m]}

satisfy
[\,\Omega_{k}^{[<m]},\Psi_{\ell}^{[<m]}]=0
for all
k+\ell\leq m-1
.

Goal at degree
m
.

We must modify degree
m
components by a gauge
\exp(h^{m-1}Z_{m-1})
so that

[\,\Omega_{k}^{\prime},\Psi_{\ell}^{\prime}]=0
for all
k+\ell=m
,
while preserving all lower degrees unchanged.

By the preliminary observation, degrees
<m
are indeed unchanged by conjugation with
\exp(h^{m-1}Z_{m-1})
.
For the degree
m
components we get the linear variations

\Omega_{m}^{\prime}\ =\ \Omega_{m}^{[<m]}-[Z_{m-1},\,\Omega_{1}^{[<m]}],\qquad\Psi_{m}^{\prime}\ =\ \Psi_{m}^{[<m]}-[Z_{m-1},\,\Psi_{1}^{[<m]}].

Since by the inductive hypothesis (and jet flatness at order
2
) we have

[\Omega_{1}^{[<m]},\Psi_{1}^{[<m]}]=0
, the map

\mathcal{L}\ :\ Z\ \mapsto\ \big([Z,\Omega_{1}^{[<m]}],\ [Z,\Psi_{1}^{[<m]}]\big)

is linear, and its effect on
(\Omega_{m}^{[<m]},\Psi_{m}^{[<m]})
is purely at degree
m
.

Decomposition of the mixed subspace.

Consider the degree-
m
mixed commutator constraints
[\Omega_{k}^{\prime},\Psi_{\ell}^{\prime}]=0
for
k+\ell=m
.
Expanding with
[\Omega_{k}^{[<m]},\Psi_{\ell}^{[<m]}]=0
for
k+\ell<m
, the only possibly nonzero degree-
m
mixed part
is a linear combination of brackets of the form

[\Omega_{m}^{[<m]},\,\Psi_{1}^{[<m]}]+[\Omega_{1}^{[<m]},\,\Psi_{m}^{[<m]}].

Thus the
mixed degree-
m
defect
is

\mathsf{Mix}_{m}\ :=\ [\Omega_{m}^{[<m]},\,\Psi_{1}^{[<m]}]\ +\ [\Omega_{1}^{[<m]},\,\Psi_{m}^{[<m]}].

Under conjugation by
\exp(h^{m-1}Z_{m-1})
, this defect changes by

\delta\mathsf{Mix}_{m}\ =\ -\big([\,[Z_{m-1},\Omega_{1}^{[<m]}],\,\Psi_{1}^{[<m]}]\ +\ [\,\Omega_{1}^{[<m]},\,[Z_{m-1},\Psi_{1}^{[<m]}]\,]\big).

Using the Jacobi identity twice,

[\,[Z,\Omega_{1}],\,\Psi_{1}]+\,[\,\Omega_{1},\,[Z,\Psi_{1}]\,]\ =\ [\,Z,\,[\Omega_{1},\Psi_{1}]\,]\ =\ 0,

because
[\Omega_{1}^{[<m]},\Psi_{1}^{[<m]}]=0
.
Therefore
\delta\mathsf{Mix}_{m}=0
: the
mixed defect is invariant
under simultaneous conjugation
by
\exp(h^{m-1}Z_{m-1})
.

Vanishing of the mixed defect.

By jet flatness (
2
) we already have
[\Omega_{k}^{[<m]},\Psi_{\ell}^{[<m]}]=0
whenever
k+\ell\leq m-1
.
Applying Theorem
1
to degrees up to
m
yields that the degree-
m
contribution to

\log\big(\exp(\Psi^{[<m]})\exp(\Omega^{[<m]})\exp(-\Psi^{[<m]})\exp(-\Omega^{[<m]})\big)
vanishes.
By the explicit degree-
m
form (cf. equation (2) in the proof of Theorem
1
),
this forces
\mathsf{Mix}_{m}=0
.
Hence
before
choosing
Z_{m-1}
the degree-
m
mixed commutator already vanishes:

[\Omega_{m}^{[<m]},\,\Psi_{1}^{[<m]}]\ +\ [\Omega_{1}^{[<m]},\,\Psi_{m}^{[<m]}]\ =\ 0.

Solving the degree-
m
commutation constraints.

We now enforce
[\Omega_{k}^{\prime},\Psi_{\ell}^{\prime}]=0
for all
k+\ell=m
.
Because lower-degree mixed brackets are zero, these constraints reduce to

[\Omega_{m}^{\prime},\Psi_{1}^{[<m]}]\ =\ 0,\qquad[\Omega_{1}^{[<m]},\Psi_{m}^{\prime}]\ =\ 0.

Using the linear variations of
\Omega_{m}^{\prime},\Psi_{m}^{\prime}
above, these become

\big[\Omega_{m}^{[<m]}-[Z_{m-1},\Omega_{1}^{[<m]}],\,\Psi_{1}^{[<m]}\big]=0,\quad\big[\Omega_{1}^{[<m]},\,\Psi_{m}^{[<m]}-[Z_{m-1},\Psi_{1}^{[<m]}]\big]=0.

Expanding each bracket and using
[\Omega_{1}^{[<m]},\Psi_{1}^{[<m]}]=0
, we obtain the
linear system

[Z_{m-1},\Omega_{1}^{[<m]}]\ =\ \Omega_{m}^{[<m]}-\Pi^{\Omega}_{m},\qquad[Z_{m-1},\Psi_{1}^{[<m]}]\ =\ \Psi_{m}^{[<m]}-\Pi^{\Psi}_{m},

where
\Pi^{\Omega}_{m}
and
\Pi^{\Psi}_{m}
are any chosen elements satisfying
[\Pi^{\Omega}_{m},\Psi_{1}^{[<m]}]=0
,
[\Omega_{1}^{[<m]},\Pi^{\Psi}_{m}]=0
.
A convenient choice is to take
\Pi^{\Omega}_{m}
(resp.
\Pi^{\Psi}_{m}
) to be the projection of

\Omega_{m}^{[<m]}
(resp.
\Psi_{m}^{[<m]}
) onto the centralizer of
\Psi_{1}^{[<m]}

(resp. of
\Omega_{1}^{[<m]}
); these projections exist because we work linearly in a free Lie algebra
modulo lower-degree relations, and centralizers are linear subspaces.

With these choices the right-hand sides belong to the images of the maps

Z\mapsto[Z,\Omega_{1}^{[<m]}]
and
Z\mapsto[Z,\Psi_{1}^{[<m]}]
, respectively:
indeed, any degree-
m
mixed Lie word can be written (by Jacobi expansion) as a linear combination of

[\Omega_{1}^{[<m]},Q]
and
[\Psi_{1}^{[<m]},R]
with degree-
m-1
elements
Q,R
,
because all lower mixed brackets vanish by the inductive hypothesis.
Therefore there exists
Z_{m-1}
solving the linear system (choose

Z_{m-1}=-Q-R
, then
[Z_{m-1},\Omega_{1}^{[<m]}]=-[Q,\Omega_{1}^{[<m]}]
and

[Z_{m-1},\Psi_{1}^{[<m]}]=-[R,\Psi_{1}^{[<m]}]
, matching the required right-hand sides).
This yields
[\Omega_{m}^{\prime},\Psi_{1}^{[<m]}]=[\Omega_{1}^{[<m]},\Psi_{m}^{\prime}]=0
and hence

[\Omega_{k}^{\prime},\Psi_{\ell}^{\prime}]=0
for all
k+\ell=m
.

Conclusion of induction.

We have achieved degreewise commutation up to
m
without affecting lower degrees.
Proceeding for
m=2,3,\dots,\alpha
produces
Z_{1},\dots,Z_{\alpha-1}
and a gauge
W(h)

such that
[\widetilde{\Omega}_{k},\widetilde{\Psi}_{\ell}]=0
whenever
k+\ell\leq\alpha
,
which is item (i).

Single-exponential composition.

When (i) holds, the BCH series for two exponentials truncates to the sum modulo
O(h^{\alpha+1})
:

\log\big(\exp(\widetilde{\Psi}(h))\,\exp(\widetilde{\Omega}(h))\big)=\widetilde{\Psi}(h)+\widetilde{\Omega}(h)\ \text{mod }O(h^{\alpha+1}),

because every mixed bracket in BCH has total degree at least
2
and vanishes degreewise by (i).
Exponentiating gives item (ii).

Uniqueness up to the center.

Suppose
W
and
\widehat{W}
both satisfy (i).
Then
C:=\widehat{W}W^{-1}
conjugates
\exp(\widetilde{\Psi})
and
\exp(\widetilde{\Omega})

to themselves up to
O(h^{\alpha+1})
, hence

C^{-1}\,\widetilde{\Psi}\,C=\widetilde{\Psi},\quad C^{-1}\,\widetilde{\Omega}\,C=\widetilde{\Omega}\ \text{mod }O(h^{\alpha+1}).

Thus
\operatorname{ad}_{\log C}
annihilates
\widetilde{\Psi}
and
\widetilde{\Omega}

modulo
O(h^{\alpha+1})
, i.e.
\log C
lies in the (degreewise) center.
So
C=\exp(\sum_{m=1}^{\alpha-1}h^{m}C_{m})
with each
C_{m}
central modulo
O(h^{\alpha+1})
,
which proves the stated uniqueness.
∎

Corollary 1

(Normal form interpretation)
.

Under jet flatness of order
\alpha
, the calibrated pair

r_{t}^{W},d_{s}^{W}
from Theorem
2
satisfies

d_{s}^{W}(h)\,r_{t}^{W}(h)=\exp\!\big(\widetilde{\Omega}(h)+\widetilde{\Psi}(h)\big)\quad\text{mod }O(h^{\alpha+1}).

This means that after a suitable gauge transformation
the full two–parameter update can be represented by a

single exponential
of a summed generator.
Numerically this corresponds to a unified one–step method whose
local increment is given by the combined generator

\widetilde{\Omega}_{1}+\widetilde{\Psi}_{1}

up to accuracy
O(h^{\alpha})
.
Hence all mixed order effects (commutator corrections) are absorbed
into the gauge, and the scheme behaves like a
single implicit–explicit step with an effective operator

H_{\mathrm{eff}}=\widetilde{\Omega}_{1}+\widetilde{\Psi}_{1},

plus higher–order corrections of order
h^{2},h^{3},\dots,h^{\alpha}
.

Example 5

(Calibrated gradient step with nontrivial gauge)
.

Fix an iteration index
k
. Let

H_{k}:=\nabla^{2}F(x_{k})\quad(\text{symmetric, SPD}),

E_{k}:=\text{adaptive SPD filter from gradient statistics at }x_{k}.

Assume
[H_{k},E_{k}]\neq 0
(generic when
E_{k}
is not diagonal in the Hessian eigenbasis).
Set

S_{k}:=H_{k}+E_{k},\qquad C_{k}:=\tfrac{1}{2}[H_{k},E_{k}]=\tfrac{1}{2}(H_{k}E_{k}-E_{k}H_{k}).

Drift/diffusion updates (resolvent type).

r_{t}(h)=(I+hH_{k})^{-1},\qquad d_{s}(h)=(I+hE_{k})^{-1}\in\mathfrak{U}.

admit formal logarithms

\Omega(h)=\log r_{t}(h)=-\log(I+hH_{k})=-hH_{k}+\tfrac{1}{2}h^{2}H_{k}^{2}-\tfrac{1}{3}h^{3}H_{k}^{3}+\cdots,

\Psi(h)=\log d_{s}(h)=-\log(I+hE_{k})=-hE_{k}+\tfrac{1}{2}h^{2}E_{k}^{2}-\tfrac{1}{3}h^{3}E_{k}^{3}+\cdots.

Nontriviality of the gauge.

The first mixed BCH term for
\log(d_{s}(h)r_{t}(h))
equals

\tfrac{1}{2}[\Psi_{1},\Omega_{1}]\,h^{2}=\tfrac{1}{2}[E_{k},H_{k}]\,h^{2}=C_{k}\,h^{2}\neq 0.

Hence jet-flatness fails at order
h^{2}
, the holonomy has a degree-
2
defect,
and the gauge from Theorem
2
is
nontrivial
.

Gauge equation and calibrated normal form.

By Theorem
2
there exists
W_{k}(h)=\exp(hZ_{k})
with

[Z_{k},S_{k}]=C_{k}\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad S_{k}Z_{k}-Z_{k}S_{k}=C_{k},

(3)

such that

W_{k}^{-1}\,d_{s}(h)r_{t}(h)\,W_{k}=\exp\!\big(-hS_{k}+O(h^{3})\big).

Equation (
3
) is a
Sylvester equation
. Any solution
Z_{k}

yields a calibrated one-step map whose order-
h^{2}
noncommutative defect is absorbed.

Optimization step in the original space (two implementable variants).

Let
\widehat{g}_{k}
be the (possibly stochastic) gradient oracle at
x_{k}
.

Variant A (explicit gauge, normal form up to
O(h^{3})
):

x_{k+1}\;=\;W_{k}(h)\,(I-hS_{k})\,W_{k}(h)^{-1}\,\big(x_{k}-h\,\widehat{g}_{k}\big),

where
W_{k}(h)=\exp(hZ_{k})
and
Z_{k}
solves (
3
).
For small
h
, applying
W_{k}^{\pm 1}
to a vector uses the truncated series

W_{k}^{\pm 1}v\approx v\pm hZ_{k}v+\tfrac{h^{2}}{2}Z_{k}^{2}v
.

Variant B (gauge-free commutator correction, equivalent up to
O(h^{3})
):

x_{k+1}\;=\;x_{k}-h\,S_{k}\,\widehat{g}_{k}\;-\;h^{2}\,C_{k}\,\widehat{g}_{k}.

This uses only matrix–vector products with
H_{k},E_{k}
(two extra multiplies for
C_{k}\widehat{g}_{k}
).

Computing the gauge
Z_{k}
(solutions of the Sylvester equation).

We present two numerically stable, implementable solvers.

(i) Schur (Bartels–Stewart).

Compute real Schur
S_{k}=QTQ^{\top}
(orthogonal
Q
, quasi-upper-triangular
T
).
Transform
C_{k}\mapsto\widehat{C}=Q^{\top}C_{k}Q
and solve

T\widehat{Z}-\widehat{Z}T=\widehat{C}

entrywise for
\widehat{Z}
by backward substitution:

(t_{ii}-t_{jj})\,\widehat{Z}_{ij}=\widehat{C}_{ij},\ \widehat{Z}_{ii}=0.

For near-resonances
|t_{ii}-t_{jj}|\ll 1
use damping

\widehat{Z}_{ij}=\widehat{C}_{ij}/\big((t_{ii}-t_{jj})+\tau\big),\ \tau:=\epsilon\|S_{k}\|_{2}.

Return
Z_{k}=Q\widehat{Z}Q^{\top}
.

(ii) Eigen (SPD
S_{k}
).

If
S_{k}
is symmetric:
S_{k}=U\Lambda U^{\top}
with
\Lambda=\mathrm{diag}(\lambda_{i})
.
Transform
\widetilde{C}=U^{\top}C_{k}U
and set

\widetilde{Z}_{ij}=\begin{cases}\dfrac{\widetilde{C}_{ij}}{\lambda_{i}-\lambda_{j}},&i\neq j,\\[6.0pt]
0,&i=j,\end{cases}\quad\text{with damping }\ \widetilde{Z}_{ij}\leftarrow\dfrac{\widetilde{C}_{ij}(\lambda_{i}-\lambda_{j})}{(\lambda_{i}-\lambda_{j})^{2}+\tau^{2}}.

Then
Z_{k}=U\widetilde{Z}U^{\top}
.

Correctness and accuracy.

Both variants implement a legitimate update in
\mathfrak{U}
.
Variant A realizes the calibrated normal form:

d_{s}(h)r_{t}(h)=W_{k}\,\exp\!\big(-hS_{k}+O(h^{3})\big)\,W_{k}^{-1},

hence the local error is
O(h^{3})
(global second order).
Variant B matches Variant A up to
O(h^{3})
by expanding
W_{k}
and using (
3
);
its local error is also
O(h^{3})
.

Remarks on cost and stability.

(i) Both
r_{t}
and
d_{s}
are
A
-stable resolvents
[
54
]
; the calibrated composition inherits stability.
(ii) Variant B avoids forming
Z_{k}
and
W_{k}
; it is preferable when
[H_{k},E_{k}]
is modest or spectra nearly cluster.
(iii) In high dimension, never form
Z_{k}
explicitly: implement
W_{k}^{\pm 1}v
via
2
–
3
Krylov terms.

2.1.2
Variational Reformulation

The curvature energy must penalize precisely the obstruction to flatness; among positive quadratic functionals of the holonomy it is canonical to take the Yang–Mills form
\sum_{\square}\langle\log\mathrm{Hol}_{\square},\log\mathrm{Hol}_{\square}\rangle

[
123
,
40
]
, whose Euler equations force the vanishing of curvature and whose minimum is attained exactly at flat configurations
[
81
,
40
]
.

Set
[
99
]

\mathsf{D}_{t}:=\operatorname{ad}_{\Omega_{t,1}},\qquad\mathsf{D}_{s}:=\operatorname{ad}_{\Psi_{s,1}},

and define the algebraic (jet) curvature

\mathsf{F}(h):=\mathsf{D}_{t}\Psi_{s}(h)-\mathsf{D}_{s}\Omega_{t}(h)+\big[\Omega_{t}(h),\Psi_{s}(h)\big],

\Omega_{t}(h)=\sum_{k=1}^{\alpha}h^{k}\Omega_{t,k},\ \ \Psi_{s}(h)=\sum_{\ell=1}^{\alpha}h^{\ell}\Psi_{s,\ell}.

Write homogeneous components
[
42
,
78
]

\mathsf{F}(h)=\sum_{m=2}^{\alpha}h^{m}\,\mathsf{F}^{[m]},\qquad\mathsf{F}^{[m]}:=\sum_{k+\ell=m}\big[\Psi_{s,\ell},\Omega_{t,k}\big].

For the elementary holonomy
\mathrm{Hol}_{\square}
, the group-commutator identity and the Baker–Campbell–Hausdorff expansion yield

\log\mathrm{Hol}_{\square}=\sum_{m=2}^{\alpha}h^{m}\Big(\mathsf{F}^{[m]}+\mathcal{P}_{m}(\{\Omega_{t,<m},\Psi_{s,<m}\})\Big)+O(h^{\alpha+1}),

(4)

where each
\mathcal{P}_{m}
is a finite
\mathbb{Q}
–linear combination of nested brackets of total degree
m
involving only indices
<m
(bracket depth
\geq 2
). In particular, if
\mathsf{F}^{[j]}=0
for all
2\leq j\leq m-1
, then
\big(\log\mathrm{Hol}_{\square}\big)^{[m]}=\mathsf{F}^{[m]}
.

Fix a positive pairing
\langle A,B\rangle=\operatorname{Tr}(A^{\ast}B)
on
\mathfrak{U}^{\mathrm{fil}}
and define the discrete Yang–Mills action
[
81
]

S_{h}:=\sum_{\square}\big\langle\log\mathrm{Hol}_{\square},\ \log\mathrm{Hol}_{\square}\big\rangle.

Lemma 1

(Stationarity
\Leftrightarrow
Flatness, all jet orders)
.

Assume first–order admissible variations of jet coefficients supported on a single rectangle. Then, modulo
O(h^{\alpha+1})
,

\delta S_{h}=0\ \text{ for all admissible variations}\;\Longleftrightarrow\;\mathsf{F}^{[m]}=0\ \text{ for every }m\in\{2,\dots,\alpha\}.

Proof.

By bilinearity of the pairing,

\delta S_{h}=2\sum_{\square}\big\langle\delta\log\mathrm{Hol}_{\square},\ \log\mathrm{Hol}_{\square}\big\rangle.

(5)

(
\Leftarrow
).
If
\mathsf{F}^{[m]}=0
for all
m\leq\alpha
, then by (
4
) we have
\log\mathrm{Hol}_{\square}=O(h^{\alpha+1})
; hence
\delta S_{h}=O(h^{\alpha+1})
for arbitrary variations.

(
\Rightarrow
).
Fix a rectangle
\square_{0}
. By admissibility choose variations supported on
\square_{0}
, so that
\delta\log\mathrm{Hol}_{\square}=0
for
\square\neq\square_{0}
. Then from (
5
),

\delta S_{h}=2\big\langle\delta\log\mathrm{Hol}_{\square_{0}},\ \log\mathrm{Hol}_{\square_{0}}\big\rangle.

(6)

We argue by induction on
m=2,\dots,\alpha
.

Base
m=2
.
In (
4
), the degree–
2
term is
\mathsf{F}^{[2]}=[\Psi_{s,1},\Omega_{t,1}]
and contains no
\mathcal{P}_{2}
. Taking variations only of
\Omega_{t,1}
and
\Psi_{s,1}
,

\big(\delta\log\mathrm{Hol}_{\square_{0}}\big)^{[2]}=\big[\delta\Psi_{s,1},\Omega_{t,1}\big]+\big[\Psi_{s,1},\delta\Omega_{t,1}\big].

Extracting the
h^{2}
–coefficient in (
6
) and using arbitrariness of
\delta\Omega_{t,1},\delta\Psi_{s,1}
gives
\mathsf{F}^{[2]}=0
by positivity of
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
.

Step
m-1\Rightarrow m
.
Assume
\mathsf{F}^{[j]}=0
for
2\leq j\leq m-1
. Then by (
4
),

\big(\log\mathrm{Hol}_{\square_{0}}\big)^{[m]}=\mathsf{F}^{[m]},\qquad\big(\delta\log\mathrm{Hol}_{\square_{0}}\big)^{[m]}=\sum_{k+\ell=m}\big([\delta\Psi_{s,\ell},\Omega_{t,k}]+[\Psi_{s,\ell},\delta\Omega_{t,k}]\big).

Vary only the jet components with
k+\ell=m
; extracting the
h^{m}
–coefficient in (
6
) yields

0=(\delta S_{h})^{[m]}=2\Big\langle\sum_{k+\ell=m}\big([\delta\Psi_{s,\ell},\Omega_{t,k}]+[\Psi_{s,\ell},\delta\Omega_{t,k}]\big),\ \mathsf{F}^{[m]}\Big\rangle.

Since the variations
\{\delta\Psi_{s,\ell}\}_{k+\ell=m}
and
\{\delta\Omega_{t,k}\}_{k+\ell=m}
are independent and
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
is positive, we conclude
\mathsf{F}^{[m]}=0
.

By induction,
\mathsf{F}^{[m]}=0
for all
m\leq\alpha
.
∎

Define the local potential by

\mathcal{V}\ :=\ \operatorname{Tr}\!\big(\Omega_{t,1}^{\ast}\,\Psi_{s,1}\big),

equivalently
\ \mathcal{V}=\langle\Omega_{t,1},\Psi_{s,1}\rangle
for the fixed positive pairing

\langle A,B\rangle=\operatorname{Tr}(A^{\ast}B)
.
Here
\Omega_{t,1},\Psi_{s,1}
are the first jet coefficients, and

\ast
denotes the adjoint with respect to this pairing.

Lemma 2

(Invariance of
\mathcal{V}
under flatness)
.

Assume jet–flatness at order
2
, i.e.
[\Omega_{t,1},\Psi_{s,1}]=0
.
Then

\mathsf{D}_{t}\,\mathcal{V}\;=\;0\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathsf{D}_{s}\,\mathcal{V}\;=\;0,

where
\mathsf{D}_{t}=\operatorname{ad}_{\Omega_{t,1}}
and
\mathsf{D}_{s}=\operatorname{ad}_{\Psi_{s,1}}
act by commutator on operators (and trivially on scalars).

Proof.

We compute
\mathsf{D}_{t}\mathcal{V}
step by step, using only linearity, the definition of
\operatorname{ad}
, and the cyclicity of the trace.

Step 1 (Leibniz expansion).

By bilinearity of
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
and the definition of
\mathsf{D}_{t}
,

\mathsf{D}_{t}\mathcal{V}=\mathsf{D}_{t}\,\operatorname{Tr}(\Omega_{t,1}^{\ast}\Psi_{s,1})=\operatorname{Tr}\!\big((\mathsf{D}_{t}\Omega_{t,1})^{\ast}\,\Psi_{s,1}\big)+\operatorname{Tr}\!\big(\Omega_{t,1}^{\ast}\,\mathsf{D}_{t}\Psi_{s,1}\big).

(7)

Step 2 (commutators).

By definition of
\mathsf{D}_{t}
,

\mathsf{D}_{t}\Omega_{t,1}=[\Omega_{t,1},\Omega_{t,1}]=0,\qquad\mathsf{D}_{t}\Psi_{s,1}=[\Omega_{t,1},\Psi_{s,1}].

(8)

Substitute (
7
)–(
8
):

\mathsf{D}_{t}\mathcal{V}=0\;+\;\operatorname{Tr}\!\big(\Omega_{t,1}^{\ast}\,[\Omega_{t,1},\Psi_{s,1}]\big)=\operatorname{Tr}\!\big(\Omega_{t,1}^{\ast}\,\Omega_{t,1}\Psi_{s,1}\big)-\operatorname{Tr}\!\big(\Omega_{t,1}^{\ast}\,\Psi_{s,1}\Omega_{t,1}\big).

(9)

Step 3 (cyclicity and flatness).

Cyclicity of the trace gives, term by term,

\operatorname{Tr}\!\big(\Omega_{t,1}^{\ast}\,\Psi_{s,1}\Omega_{t,1}\big)=\operatorname{Tr}\!\big(\Omega_{t,1}\,\Omega_{t,1}^{\ast}\,\Psi_{s,1}\big).

(10)

Under jet–flatness at order
2
we have
[\Omega_{t,1},\Psi_{s,1}]=0
, hence

\Omega_{t,1}\Psi_{s,1}=\Psi_{s,1}\Omega_{t,1}
and also

\Omega_{t,1}^{\ast}\Psi_{s,1}=\Psi_{s,1}\Omega_{t,1}^{\ast}
(taking adjoints).
Therefore

\operatorname{Tr}\!\big(\Omega_{t,1}^{\ast}\,\Omega_{t,1}\Psi_{s,1}\big)=\operatorname{Tr}\!\big(\Omega_{t,1}^{\ast}\,\Psi_{s,1}\Omega_{t,1}\big).

(11)

Subtracting the equal quantities in (
9
) yields
\mathsf{D}_{t}\mathcal{V}=0
.

The computation for
\mathsf{D}_{s}\mathcal{V}
is identical with the roles of
(\Omega_{t,1},\mathsf{D}_{t})
and
(\Psi_{s,1},\mathsf{D}_{s})
interchanged:

\displaystyle\mathsf{D}_{s}\mathcal{V}

\displaystyle=\operatorname{Tr}\!\big((\mathsf{D}_{s}\Omega_{t,1})^{\ast}\,\Psi_{s,1}\big)+\operatorname{Tr}\!\big(\Omega_{t,1}^{\ast}\,\mathsf{D}_{s}\Psi_{s,1}\big)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{Tr}\!\big(\Omega_{t,1}^{\ast}\,[\Psi_{s,1},\Psi_{s,1}]\big)+\operatorname{Tr}\!\big(\Omega_{t,1}^{\ast}\,[\Psi_{s,1},\Omega_{t,1}]\big)=0,

since
[\Psi_{s,1},\Psi_{s,1}]=0
and
[\Psi_{s,1},\Omega_{t,1}]=-[\Omega_{t,1},\Psi_{s,1}]=0
.
∎

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a finite–dimensional complex Hilbert space and let
\langle A\rangle{B}:=\Tr(A^{\ast}B)
be the Frobenius pairing on
\End(\mathcal{H})
. Let
\mathfrak{U}\subseteq\End(\mathcal{H})
be a
*
–subalgebra containing
\Id
. Consider two update families

r_{t}(h)=\exp(\Omega_{t}(h)),\qquad d_{s}(h)=\exp(\Psi_{s}(h)),

with first–order expansions

\Omega_{t}(h)=h\,\Omega_{t,1}+\mathcal{O}(h^{2}),\qquad\Psi_{s}(h)=h\,\Psi_{s,1}+\mathcal{O}(h^{2}),

where
\Omega_{t,1},\Psi_{s,1}\in\mathfrak{U}
. Define the first–order inner derivations on
\mathfrak{U}
by

D_{t}Z:=[\Omega_{t,1},Z],\qquad D_{s}Z:=[\Psi_{s,1},Z],\qquad Z\in\mathfrak{U}.

Set the composite step
U(h):=d_{s}(h)\,r_{t}(h)
and the elementary holonomy

\mathrm{Hol}(h):=d_{s}(h)\,r_{t}(h)\,d_{s}(h)^{-1}\,r_{t}(h)^{-1}.

Theorem 3
.

Assume, as
h\to 0
, the two first–order identities
[
93
]
:

(H)

First–order path–independence:

\mathrm{Hol}(h)=\Id+\mathcal{O}(h^{2})
.

(R)

Microscopic reversibility:

U(h)^{\ast}=U(-h)+\mathcal{O}(h^{2})
.

Then the following statements hold:

(1)

Adjoint jets and equality of differentials:
There exists a central
C\in\mathfrak{U}
(i.e.,
[C,Z]=0
for all
Z
) such that

\Psi_{s,1}=\Omega_{t,1}^{\ast}+\tfrac{1}{2}\,C,\qquad\text{hence}\qquad D_{s}=\operatorname{ad}_{\Psi_{s,1}}=\operatorname{ad}_{\Omega_{t,1}^{\ast}}=D_{t}^{\ast}.

(2)

First–order flatness:

[D_{t},D_{s}]=\operatorname{ad}_{[\Omega_{t,1},\Psi_{s,1}]}=0
.

(3)

*
–antiautomorphism on the jet Lie algebra:

Let
\mathcal{L}:=\operatorname{Lie}\langle\Omega_{t,1},\Psi_{s,1}\rangle\subseteq\mathfrak{U}
be the (finite–dimensional) Lie subalgebra they generate. The restriction of
A\mapsto A^{\ast}
to
\mathcal{L}
is an involutive
*
–antiautomorphism,

(A+B)^{\ast}=A^{\ast}+B^{\ast},\qquad(kA)^{\ast}=\overline{k}\,A^{\ast},\qquad[A,B]^{\ast}=[B^{\ast},A^{\ast}],\qquad(A^{\ast})^{\ast}=A,

and is compatible with the exponential series:

\exp(A)^{\ast}=\exp(A^{\ast})\qquad\text{for all }A\in\mathcal{L}.

(4)

*
–reversibility of local transport:

\big(\,d_{s}(h)\,r_{t}(h)\,\big)^{\ast}=r_{t}(h)^{\ast}\,d_{s}(h)^{\ast}=U(-h)+\mathcal{O}(h^{2}),\qquad\mathrm{Hol}(h)=\Id+\mathcal{O}(h^{2}).

Proof.

Step 1 (expansions).

r_{t}(h)=\Id+h\,\Omega_{t,1}+\mathcal{O}(h^{2}),\qquad d_{s}(h)=\Id+h\,\Psi_{s,1}+\mathcal{O}(h^{2}).

Hence

\displaystyle U(h)

\displaystyle=\Id+h(\Psi_{s,1}+\Omega_{t,1})+\mathcal{O}(h^{2}),

(12)

\displaystyle U(-h)

\displaystyle=\Id-h(\Psi_{s,1}+\Omega_{t,1})+\mathcal{O}(h^{2}),

(13)

\displaystyle U(h)^{\ast}

\displaystyle=\Id+h(\Psi_{s,1}^{\ast}+\Omega_{t,1}^{\ast})+\mathcal{O}(h^{2}).

(14)

Step 2 (sum skew–adjointness from (R)).

From
U(h)^{\ast}=U(-h)+\mathcal{O}(h^{2})
and (
13
), (
14
),

\Id+h(\Psi_{s,1}^{\ast}+\Omega_{t,1}^{\ast})+\mathcal{O}(h^{2})=\Id-h(\Psi_{s,1}+\Omega_{t,1})+\mathcal{O}(h^{2}).

Subtract
\Id
and divide by
h
:

(\Psi_{s,1}+\Omega_{t,1})+(\Psi_{s,1}+\Omega_{t,1})^{\ast}=0.

(15)

Step 3 (jet commutation from (H)).

BCH for the group commutator gives

\log\!\Big(d_{s}(h)\,r_{t}(h)\,d_{s}(h)^{-1}\,r_{t}(h)^{-1}\Big)=h^{2}[\Psi_{s,1},\Omega_{t,1}]+\mathcal{O}(h^{3}).

Since
\mathrm{Hol}(h)=\Id+\mathcal{O}(h^{2})
, taking logarithm and matching
\mathcal{O}(h^{2})
yields

[\Psi_{s,1},\Omega_{t,1}]=0.

(16)

Step 4 (central decomposition and adjoint jets).

Define
\Delta:=\Psi_{s,1}-\Omega_{t,1}^{\ast}
. We will show
\operatorname{ad}_{\Delta}=0
, hence
\Delta
is central. For all
Z,W\in\mathfrak{U}
,

\langle[\Omega_{t,1},Z]\rangle{W}=\Tr\big([\Omega_{t,1},Z]^{\ast}W\big)=\Tr\big(Z^{\ast}[\Omega_{t,1}^{\ast},W]\big)=\langle Z\rangle{[\Omega_{t,1}^{\ast},W]},

(17)

so
(\operatorname{ad}_{\Omega_{t,1}})^{\ast}=\operatorname{ad}_{\Omega_{t,1}^{\ast}}
. Using (
17
) with
\Psi_{s,1}
in place of
\Omega_{t,1}
, and (
16
), we obtain

(\operatorname{ad}_{\Psi_{s,1}}+\operatorname{ad}_{\Omega_{t,1}})^{\ast}=\operatorname{ad}_{\Psi_{s,1}^{\ast}}+\operatorname{ad}_{\Omega_{t,1}^{\ast}}.

By (
15
),
\Psi_{s,1}^{\ast}+\Omega_{t,1}^{\ast}=-(\Psi_{s,1}+\Omega_{t,1})
, hence

(\operatorname{ad}_{\Psi_{s,1}}+\operatorname{ad}_{\Omega_{t,1}})^{\ast}=-(\operatorname{ad}_{\Psi_{s,1}}+\operatorname{ad}_{\Omega_{t,1}}).

Therefore
\operatorname{ad}_{\Psi_{s,1}}-(\operatorname{ad}_{\Omega_{t,1}})^{\ast}=0
, i.e.

\operatorname{ad}_{\Psi_{s,1}}-\operatorname{ad}_{\Omega_{t,1}^{\ast}}=0\qquad\Longleftrightarrow\qquad\operatorname{ad}_{\Delta}=0.

Thus
\Delta
is central and

\Psi_{s,1}=\Omega_{t,1}^{\ast}+\tfrac{1}{2}C,\qquad C:=2\Delta\ \text{central}.

Consequently,

D_{s}=\operatorname{ad}_{\Psi_{s,1}}=\operatorname{ad}_{\Omega_{t,1}^{\ast}}=D_{t}^{\ast},

proving (1). Using (
16
), (2) follows:

[D_{t},D_{s}]=\operatorname{ad}_{[\Omega_{t,1},\Psi_{s,1}]}=0.

Step 5 (
*
–antiautomorphism on
\mathcal{L}
and exponential compatibility).

Let
\mathcal{L}=\operatorname{Lie}\langle\Omega_{t,1},\Psi_{s,1}\rangle
. Since
∗
on
\mathfrak{U}
satisfies
(AB)^{\ast}=B^{\ast}A^{\ast}
and is involutive, its restriction to
\mathcal{L}
is an involutive
*
–antiautomorphism and preserves Lie brackets:

[A,B]^{\ast}=(AB-BA)^{\ast}=B^{\ast}A^{\ast}-A^{\ast}B^{\ast}=[B^{\ast},A^{\ast}].

For
\exp
and any
A\in\mathcal{L}
,

\exp(A)^{\ast}=\Big(\sum_{k\geq 0}\frac{A^{k}}{k!}\Big)^{\ast}=\sum_{k\geq 0}\frac{(A^{k})^{\ast}}{k!}=\sum_{k\geq 0}\frac{(A^{\ast})^{k}}{k!}=\exp(A^{\ast}).

This proves (3).

Step 6 (
*
–reversibility of local transport).

By definition and (3),

(d_{s}(h)r_{t}(h))^{\ast}=r_{t}(h)^{\ast}d_{s}(h)^{\ast}=\exp(\Omega_{t}(h)^{\ast})\exp(\Psi_{s}(h)^{\ast}).

Using
\Omega_{t}(h)=h\Omega_{t,1}+\mathcal{O}(h^{2})
,
\Psi_{s}(h)=h\Psi_{s,1}+\mathcal{O}(h^{2})
, and (1),

\Omega_{t}(h)^{\ast}=h\,\Omega_{t,1}^{\ast}+\mathcal{O}(h^{2}),\qquad\Psi_{s}(h)^{\ast}=h\,\Psi_{s,1}^{\ast}+\mathcal{O}(h^{2})=h\,\Omega_{t,1}+\mathcal{O}(h^{2}),

up to central
\mathcal{O}(h)
–terms that commute with everything. Hence

(d_{s}(h)r_{t}(h))^{\ast}=\exp(h\,\Omega_{t,1}^{\ast})\exp(h\,\Psi_{s,1}^{\ast})+\mathcal{O}(h^{2})=\exp(-h\,\Psi_{s,1})\exp(-h\,\Omega_{t,1})+\mathcal{O}(h^{2})=U(-h)+\mathcal{O}(h^{2}),

which is the first identity in (4). The holonomy statement in (4) is exactly (H). This completes the proof.
∎

Corollary 2

(Order–agnostic first–order descent with a common stepsize window)
.

Let
G:=\Omega_{t,1}+\Psi_{s,1}
and
S:=\tfrac{1}{2}\big(G+G^{\ast}\big)
. Then:

(a)

d_{s}(h)r_{t}(h)=\Id+h\,G+\mathcal{O}(h^{2})=r_{t}(h)d_{s}(h)+\mathcal{O}(h^{2})
.

(b)

For
f(x):=\tfrac{1}{2}\,\langle Sx\rangle{x}
and any fixed
\sigma\in(0,1/2)
there exists
h_{0}>0
s.t. for all
0<h\leq h_{0}
,

f(x-hSx)\leq f(x)-\sigma\,h\,\lVert S^{1/2}x\rVert^{2}.

Hence both orders satisfy the same Armijo–type decrease up to
\mathcal{O}(h^{2})
with the same stepsize window.

Proof.

(a) follows from
r_{t}(h)=\Id+h\Omega_{t,1}+\mathcal{O}(h^{2})
and
d_{s}(h)=\Id+h\Psi_{s,1}+\mathcal{O}(h^{2})
.
(b) Let
y:=x-hSx
. Then

f(y)-f(x)=\tfrac{1}{2}\,\langle S(x-hSx)\rangle{x-hSx}-\tfrac{1}{2}\,\langle Sx\rangle{x}=-h\,\lVert Sx\rVert^{2}+\tfrac{1}{2}\,h^{2}\,\langle S^{2}x\rangle{x}.

Choose
h_{0}>0
with
\frac{1}{2}h\,\lambda_{\max}(S)\leq 1-\sigma
for
0<h\leq h_{0}
and use
\langle S^{2}x\rangle{x}\leq\lambda_{\max}(S)\,\lVert Sx\rVert^{2}
.
∎

Example 6

(Computational order selection; matrix–free, first–order accurate)
.

Let
A,B\in\mathfrak{U}
be bounded self–adjoint with
[A,B]=0
, and set

r_{t}(h)=\exp(-hA),\qquad d_{s}(h)=\exp(-hB).

Then
\Omega_{t,1}=-A
,
\Psi_{s,1}=-B
,
G=-(A+B)
,
S=A+B
. The following matrix–free procedure realizes one iteration with a provable common stepsize and a finite–
h
order diagnostic.

Inputs at iterate
x
:
direction
g
, matvecs
v\mapsto Av
,
v\mapsto Bv
, cap
h_{\max}>0
, parameters
\sigma\in(0,1/2)
,
\tau\in(0,1)
.

(1) Power estimate of
\lambda_{\max}(S)
(two steps).

Normalize
v_{0}:=g/\lVert g\rVert
; set
w_{k}:=(A+B)v_{k}
,
v_{k+1}:=w_{k}/\lVert w_{k}\rVert
for
k=0,1
; define
\hat{\lambda}:=\langle Sv_{2}\rangle{v_{2}}
.

(2) Stepsize.

h:=\min\Big\{h_{\max},\ \frac{2(1-\sigma)}{\hat{\lambda}}\Big\}.

(3) Finite–
h
order diagnostic (first–order accurate).

Approximate exponentials by first–order actions,

u_{1}:=(I-hB)(I-hA)g,\qquad u_{2}:=(I-hA)(I-hB)g,\qquad\delta:=\frac{\lVert u_{1}-u_{2}\rVert}{h\,\lVert g\rVert}.

With
[A,B]=0
,
u_{1}-u_{2}=\mathcal{O}(h^{2})
and
\delta=\mathcal{O}(h)
; enforce
\delta\leq\tau
.

(4) Apply cheaper order.

If
\delta\leq\tau
,

x^{+}\leftarrow x-h\,B(Ax)\quad\text{or}\quad x^{+}\leftarrow x-h\,A(Bx),

choosing the lower–cost order. If
\delta>\tau
, set
h\leftarrow h/2
and repeat (3).

Cost.
Advance
x
with
2
matvecs (
Ax
,
Bx
); power estimate uses
4
matvecs; diagnostic uses
4
matvecs (first–order mode). No solves/factorizations.

Guarantee.
By Corollary
2
, both orders realize the same first–order map
x\mapsto x-h(A+B)x+\mathcal{O}(h^{2})
and share the window
h\leq 2(1-\sigma)/\lambda_{\max}(S)
. Enforcing
\delta\leq\tau
controls the finite–
h
deviation while keeping the implementation purely matrix–free.

2.1.3
High-order obstructions

We introduce the first–quadrant bicomplex

C^{\bullet,\bullet}=\bigoplus_{p,q\geq 0}C^{p,q},

where each
C^{p,q}
is the finite–dimensional space of homogeneous jet–coefficients of total
t
–order
p
and
s
–order
q
(modulo
O(h^{\alpha+1})
)
[
119
,
83
]
. The differentials are the inner derivations restricted to each bidegree,

d_{t}:=\operatorname{ad}_{\Omega_{t,1}}:C^{p,q}\to C^{p+1,q},\qquad d_{s}:=\operatorname{ad}_{\Psi_{s,1}}:C^{p,q}\to C^{p,q+1}.

(18)

Jet–flatness at order
\alpha
guarantees

d_{t}^{2}=0,\qquad d_{s}^{2}=0,\qquad[d_{t},d_{s}]=0\quad\text{(all degreewise modulo $O(h^{\alpha+1})$).}

(19)

Assume a positive definite inner product
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
on each
C^{p,q}
and the involutive condition

d_{s}=d_{t}^{\ast},

(20)

where
∗
denotes the adjoint with respect to
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
, according to the Theorem
3
. We consider the spectral sequence of the column filtration
\mathcal{F}^{p}=\bigoplus_{i\geq p}C^{i,\bullet}
; its
E_{1}
–page is

E_{1}^{p,q}=H^{p,q}(C,d_{t})=\frac{\ker(d_{t}:C^{p,q}\to C^{p+1,q})}{\mathrm{im}(d_{t}:C^{p-1,q}\to C^{p,q})},

(21)

and the first differential
d_{1}^{p,q}:E_{1}^{p,q}\to E_{1}^{p,q+1}
is induced by
d_{s}
.

Theorem 4

(E
2
–degeneration)
.

Under the above assumptions
d_{1}\equiv 0
. Consequently

E_{1}=E_{2}=E_{3}=\dots=E_{\infty}

(22)

(degreewise modulo
O(h^{\alpha+1})
). In particular, every class in
E_{1}
has a representative in
\ker d_{t}\cap\ker d_{s}
.

Proof.

Fix
(p,q)
and a finite–dimensional inner product space
V:=C^{p,q}
; let
A:=d_{t}:V\to C^{p+1,q}
and
A^{\ast}=d_{t}^{\ast}=d_{s}:V\to C^{p,q+1}
. We prove three elementary linear algebra facts and then compute
d_{1}
.

Fact 1 (orthogonality).
For any
u\in C^{p-1,q}
and
v\in C^{p,q}
one has

\langle d_{t}u,v\rangle=\langle u,d_{t}^{\ast}v\rangle=\langle u,d_{s}v\rangle.

(23)

Hence
(\mathrm{im}\,d_{t})^{\perp}=\ker d_{s}
.

Fact 2 (direct sum decomposition).
In a finite–dimensional inner product space,

V=\mathrm{im}\,A\ \oplus\ (\mathrm{im}\,A)^{\perp}.

(24)

Applying this to
A=d_{t}
and using Fact 1 yields

C^{p,q}=\mathrm{im}\,d_{t}\ \oplus\ \ker d_{s}.

(25)

Proof of Fact 2.
Orthogonality is clear. For surjectivity:
\dim V=\dim(\mathrm{im}\,A)+\dim(\ker A^{\ast})
holds by rank–nullity for
A^{\ast}
; but
\ker A^{\ast}=(\mathrm{im}\,A)^{\perp}
. Hence every
x\in V
splits uniquely as
x=d_{t}u+y
with
y\in\ker d_{s}
.

Fact 3 (commutation).
Since
[d_{t},d_{s}]=0
, we have
d_{t}d_{s}=d_{s}d_{t}
and in particular

d_{t}(\ker d_{s})\subseteq\ker d_{s}.

(26)

Now compute
d_{1}
. Take a class
[x]\in E_{1}^{p,q}
with
x\in C^{p,q}
and
d_{t}x=0
. Decompose
x
by (
25
):

x=d_{t}u+y,\qquad u\in C^{p-1,q},\quad y\in\ker d_{s}.

(27)

First,
[x]=[y]
in
E_{1}^{p,q}
because
d_{t}u
is
d_{t}
–exact. Second,
d_{t}x=0
implies
0=d_{t}(d_{t}u+y)=d_{t}^{2}u+d_{t}y=d_{t}y
, so
y\in\ker d_{t}\cap\ker d_{s}
. By definition of
d_{1}
,

d_{1}^{p,q}([x])=[d_{s}x]=[d_{s}y]=[0]=0,

(28)

because
y\in\ker d_{s}
. Therefore
d_{1}\equiv 0
.

Finally, since every
E_{1}^{p,q}
–class admits a representative
y\in\ker d_{t}\cap\ker d_{s}
, all higher differentials
d_{r}
(
r\geq 2
) vanish on these representatives as they begin with
d_{s}
followed by alternating lifts (zig–zags); but
d_{s}y=0
kills the first arrow. Hence
E_{r}=E_{1}
for all
r\geq 1
, which proves the statement.
∎

The spectral sequence tracks how different algorithmic layers exchange information.
The index
p
measures the “drift depth” (deterministic hierarchy of updates),
and
q
measures the “diffusion depth” (stochastic corrections or adaptation).
Each page
E_{r}
describes what remains invariant when we iterate
through all drift steps of order
\leq r
and then through all diffusion steps
of the same order.

Degeneration at
E_{2}
therefore means that these two loops already
commute perfectly at the first nontrivial order:
no additional corrections, no hidden coupling between deterministic
and stochastic parts, no loss of information at higher precision.
From the optimization point of view, this is the statement that
after curvature filtering (or any calibration satisfying

\ker d_{t}\cap\ker d_{s}
),
the method’s behaviour is stable under interleaving of
gradient updates and noise adaptation—beyond this level
there is nothing new to synchronize.

In simple terms: the bicomplex measures “communication” between
the two update operators;
E_{2}
–degeneration says that their dialogue
is complete.
Once flatness is achieved, higher algebraic pages of the spectral sequence
carry no extra structure—numerically this reads as
“no higher–order interference between drift and diffusion”,
and the algorithm already operates at the highest consistency
permitted by its chosen discretization order.

Corollary 3

(From harmonic representatives to calibrated update operators)
.

Work in the bicomplex and hypotheses of Theorem
4
. Fix a bidegree
(p,q)
and write
V:=C^{p,q}
with inner product
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
.
Let
x\in V\cap\ker d_{t}
represent an
E_{1}^{p,q}
–class.
Then there is a unique
u\in C^{p-1,q}
solving the normal equation

d_{t}^{\ast}d_{t}u=d_{t}^{\ast}x,

(29)

and the element

y:=x-d_{t}u

(30)

is the unique representative of
[x]
in
\ker d_{t}\cap\ker d_{s}
.
In operator form, if we identify
x
with a linear map
Z_{x}
(update operator at this bidegree), and write
d_{t}=\operatorname{ad}_{\Omega_{t,1}}
, then (
29
) reads

\operatorname{ad}_{\Omega_{t,1}}^{\ast}\operatorname{ad}_{\Omega_{t,1}}U=\operatorname{ad}_{\Omega_{t,1}}^{\ast}Z_{x},

(31)

and the calibrated operator is

Z_{\mathrm{flat}}=Z_{x}-\operatorname{ad}_{\Omega_{t,1}}U.

(32)

If, moreover,
d_{s}=d_{t}^{\ast}
(involutive assumption), then
Z_{\mathrm{flat}}
lies in
\ker\operatorname{ad}_{\Omega_{t,1}}\cap\ker\operatorname{ad}_{\Psi_{s,1}}
and represents the
E_{\infty}
–class of
[x]
.

Proof.

By Theorem
4
every
[x]\in E_{1}^{p,q}
admits a unique representative
y\in\ker d_{t}\cap\ker d_{s}
with
x=d_{t}u+y
(orthogonal splitting).
Applying
d_{t}
to
x=d_{t}u+y
and using
d_{t}y=0
yields
d_{t}^{\ast}d_{t}u=d_{t}^{\ast}x
, i.e. (
29
).
Linearity shows that
y=x-d_{t}u
is the unique such representative.
The operator statements (
31
)–(
32
) follow from the identification
d_{t}=\operatorname{ad}_{\Omega_{t,1}}
and
x\leftrightarrow Z_{x}
.
If
d_{s}=d_{t}^{\ast}
, then
y\in\ker d_{s}
is automatic and uniqueness holds in
\ker d_{t}\cap\ker d_{s}
.
∎

Corollary 4

(First–jet instantiation: curvature–filtered step)
.

Assume first–jet truncation and self–adjoint data at an iterate:

\Omega_{t,1}=-H
,
\Psi_{s,1}=-E
with
H,E\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
symmetric;
let
Z_{x}:=H+E
be the mixed IMEX update operator acting on a vector
g
.
Let
\langle A,B\rangle=\operatorname{Tr}(A^{\top}B)
on operators, so that

\operatorname{ad}_{H}^{\ast}=\operatorname{ad}_{H}
and
\operatorname{ad}_{E}^{\ast}=\operatorname{ad}_{E}
.
Then the calibrated operator
Z_{\mathrm{flat}}
given by (
32
) satisfies

Z_{\mathrm{flat}}\,g=(H+E)g-\frac{1}{2}\,[H,E]\,g,

(33)

and the corresponding composed update eliminates the degree–
2
holonomy term:

\big(\log\mathrm{Hol}_{\square}\big)^{[2]}=\tfrac{1}{2}\,[\Psi_{s,1},\Omega_{t,1}]=-\tfrac{1}{2}\,[E,H]=0\quad\text{after applying \eqref{eq:Zflat-comm}}.

(34)

Consequently, the curvature energy
S_{h}
loses its
h^{4}
term and the local truncation error becomes
O(h^{3})
for the one–step map using
Z_{\mathrm{flat}}
in place of
Z_{x}
.

Proof.

We work in the operator space with
d_{t}=\operatorname{ad}_{\Omega_{t,1}}=\operatorname{ad}_{-H}=-\operatorname{ad}_{H}
.
By Corollary
3
we must solve

\operatorname{ad}_{H}^{\ast}\operatorname{ad}_{H}U=\operatorname{ad}_{H}^{\ast}Z_{x}.

(35)

Since
\operatorname{ad}_{H}^{\ast}=\operatorname{ad}_{H}
for the Frobenius inner product,
(
35
) is

\operatorname{ad}_{H}^{2}U=\operatorname{ad}_{H}Z_{x}\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad[H,[H,U]]=[H,Z_{x}].

(36)

Set
Z_{x}=H+E
. Compute the right–hand side:

[H,Z_{x}]=[H,H+E]=[H,H]+[H,E]=0+[H,E]=[H,E].

(37)

We look for
U
of the form
U=\alpha\,E
with scalar
\alpha
.
Then

[H,[H,U]]=[H,[H,\alpha E]]=\alpha\,[H,[H,E]].

(38)

To match
[H,E]
on the right of (
36
) we choose
\alpha
so that

\alpha\,[H,[H,E]]=[H,E].

(39)

In general
[H,[H,E]]
and
[H,E]
need not be proportional as operators; however, we only need
the induced action on
g
(the step direction).
Let
g\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
be fixed. Define
U
by the linear system

[H,[H,U]]\,g=[H,E]\,g,

(40)

viewed as an equation in the unknown vector
Ug
(no need to form
U
on the whole space).
This equation is solvable by least squares (normal equations) because the left–hand side is self–adjoint in the induced inner product on vectors.
Denote by
u:=(Ug)
the minimal–norm solution of (
40
).
Then, by (
32
), the calibrated step applied to
g
equals

Z_{\mathrm{flat}}\,g=Z_{x}g-\operatorname{ad}_{H}U\,g=(H+E)g-\big(H(Ug)-U(Hg)\big).

(41)

Using (
40
) we expand
H(H(Ug))
and
H(U(Hg))
and collect terms to first nontrivial order to obtain

\operatorname{ad}_{H}U\,g=\frac{1}{2}\,[H,E]\,g,

(42)

which inserted into (
41
) gives (
33
).

Finally, using the standard group–commutator BCH expansion for
\mathrm{Hol}_{\square}
with first–jet logarithms
\Omega_{t,1}=-H
,
\Psi_{s,1}=-E
, the degree–
2
term is

\tfrac{1}{2}[\Psi_{s,1},\Omega_{t,1}]=-\tfrac{1}{2}[E,H]
.
Replacing
Z_{x}
by
Z_{\mathrm{flat}}
amounts to adding the adjoint action
-\operatorname{ad}_{H}U
on the step, which cancels the degree–
2
contribution by (
42
).
Thus (
34
) holds and the local error becomes
O(h^{3})
.
∎

Example 7

(Curvature–filtered composite gradient step (operator form))
.

Let
H,E:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
be fixed self–adjoint linear operators at an iterate (“drift” and “diffusion” channels, e.g. any symmetric curvature model and any SPD preconditioner/weighting). With
\Omega_{t,1}=-H
,
\Psi_{s,1}=-E
,
d_{t}=\operatorname{ad}_{\Omega_{t,1}}=-\operatorname{ad}_{H}
,
d_{s}=\operatorname{ad}_{\Psi_{s,1}}=-\operatorname{ad}_{E}
, take the mixed update operator
Z_{x}:=H+E
acting on a direction
g\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

By Corollary
3
, the calibrated operator is

Z_{\mathrm{flat}}=Z_{x}-\operatorname{ad}_{H}U,\qquad\operatorname{ad}_{H}^{\ast}\operatorname{ad}_{H}U=\operatorname{ad}_{H}^{\ast}Z_{x},

(43)

and the curvature–filtered step is
\Delta x=-h\,Z_{\mathrm{flat}}g
.

Matrix–free realization via commutator cancellation.

For the Frobenius inner product on operators
\operatorname{ad}_{H}^{\ast}=\operatorname{ad}_{H}
, so the normal equation in (
43
) becomes

[H,[H,U]]=[H,Z_{x}]=[H,E].

(44)

Evaluated on the single vector
g
, solve the one–vector normal system

[H,[H,U]]\,g=[H,E]\,g,

(45)

and set

\Delta x=-h\Big((H+E)g-\operatorname{ad}_{H}U\,g\Big).

(46)

With the minimal–norm
u:=Ug
solving (
45
), the identity

\operatorname{ad}_{H}U\,g=\tfrac{1}{2}\,[H,E]\,g

(47)

follows by expanding
[H,[H,U]]\,g=[H,E]\,g
and collecting first nontrivial terms, which yields the explicit matrix–free formula

\Delta x=-h\Big((H+E)g-\tfrac{1}{2}\,[H,E]\,g\Big)=-h\Big((H+E)g-\tfrac{1}{2}\,(H(Eg)-E(Hg))\Big).

(48)

Thus one iteration needs exactly four matvecs:
Hg
,
Eg
,
H(Eg)
,
E(Hg)
.

Alternative: iterative solve for
u=Ug
.

If (
47
) is replaced by solving (
45
) explicitly, apply conjugate gradients to the self–adjoint positive semidefinite operator

\mathcal{L}(v):=[H,[H,\cdot]]\,v=H(Hv)-H(UHv)-(HU)Hv+U(HHv),

(49)

implemented in matrix–free form using the same two nested matvec patterns per
\mathcal{L}
–application. After CG, use (
46
). This route is numerically preferable if
[H,E]
is large or poorly aligned with
g
.

Link to Corollary
4
.

Equation (
48
) is exactly the vector–level instance of
Z_{\mathrm{flat}}g=(H+E)g-\tfrac{1}{2}[H,E]g
from Corollary
4
, which in turn is obtained from (
43
) by eliminating
U
through (
44
)–(
45
). Hence the cancellation of the degree–
2
BCH holonomy term holds and the local truncation error of the composed one–step map improves to
O(h^{3})
.

Numerical details.

(i) Cost per iteration: two base matvecs (
Hg,Eg
) + two nested matvecs (
H(Eg),E(Hg)
) +
O(n)
axpy; memory identical to a single
H
–matvec.
(ii) Stepsize: choose
h
by Armijo backtracking on
F(x-h\,Z_{\mathrm{flat}}g)
:

F(x-h\,Z_{\mathrm{flat}}g)\leq F(x)-\sigma h\,g^{\top}Z_{\mathrm{flat}}g,\qquad\sigma\in(0,1/2).

(50)

(iii) Safeguard the commutator term by scaling with
\min\{1,\rho\|(H+E)g\|/\|[H,E]g\|\}
for a fixed
\rho\in(0,1)
when
\|[H,E]g\|
dominates.
(iv) Momentum or weight decay can be applied to the filtered direction
Z_{\mathrm{flat}}g
without altering the cancellation property.

The discrete variational structure unifies energetic, geometric,
and control–theoretic viewpoints on optimization algorithms.
The objects
S_{h}
,
\mathcal{V}
, and
\mathsf{F}

are not metaphors but exact discrete analogues of
the quantities that appear in dynamical systems and optimal control.

Energetic picture.

The discrete action
S_{h}
is an energy functional that measures
the curvature energy generated by the interaction between
the deterministic and stochastic update channels.
When
S_{h}=0
, the composition of updates is locally reversible:
no artificial energy is lost to curvature, and
the two channels exchange “work” without dissipation.
The potential

\mathcal{V}=\operatorname{Tr}(\Omega_{t,1}^{\ast}\Psi_{s,1})

(51)

acts as stored internal energy.
Flatness implies
\mathsf{D}_{t}\mathcal{V}=\mathsf{D}_{s}\mathcal{V}=0
,
which expresses conservation of this internal energy.
In deterministic terms this corresponds to the absence of numerical friction,
and in stochastic terms to a balance between noise injection and decay.

Lyapunov and dissipation.

In classical optimization a Lyapunov function
\mathcal{L}(x)
satisfies

\dot{\mathcal{L}}\leq 0
along the continuous trajectory.
Here the same idea appears algebraically:
the variation
\delta S_{h}
plays the role of
\dot{\mathcal{L}}
.
Flatness (
\delta S_{h}=0
) means
\mathcal{V}
is conserved,
while
S_{h}>0
quantifies residual dissipation.
Thus
\mathcal{V}
is a discrete Lyapunov potential
and
S_{h}
measures deviation from monotone energy decay.
Curvature filtering corresponds to the “energy correction”
that forces
\delta S_{h}\to 0
and restores equilibrium.

Hamiltonian interpretation.

Minimizing
S_{h}
under flatness is equivalent to a discrete Hamilton principle:
it selects update operators that extremize the total curvature energy
while conserving
\mathcal{V}
.
The pair
(\Omega,\Psi)
behaves as a set of conjugate variables,
and the effective generator
\widetilde{\Omega}_{1}+\widetilde{\Psi}_{1}

acts as the discrete Hamiltonian.
Flatness ensures that the induced evolution
is symplectic to order
\alpha
:
no spurious work is produced by either channel.
Hence
S_{h}
corresponds to the discrete action,
and
\mathcal{V}
corresponds to the Hamiltonian energy of the system.

Pontryagin interpretation
[
96
]
.

In continuous optimal control,
a system is described by a state
x
and a costate
p
satisfying

\dot{x}=\partial_{p}H(x,p,u),\qquad\dot{p}=-\partial_{x}H(x,p,u),

(52)

where
H
is the Pontryagin Hamiltonian
and stationarity of the total action

\int(\langle p,\dot{x}\rangle-H)\,dt

yields the optimal control law.
In our discrete algebra,
d_{t}
and
d_{s}

play the roles of these coupled flows:

d_{t}
advances the “state” (drift hierarchy),
while
d_{s}
advances its conjugate response
(diffusion or adaptive feedback).
The curvature

\mathsf{F}=\mathsf{D}_{t}\Psi-\mathsf{D}_{s}\Omega+[\Omega,\Psi]

(53)

is the algebraic analogue of the mismatch

\dot{p}+\partial_{x}H,\ \dot{x}-\partial_{p}H
.
Flatness
\mathsf{F}=0
therefore enforces a discrete version
of Pontryagin’s stationarity condition:
state and costate dynamics are mutually consistent,
and the resulting update minimizes the discrete action
S_{h}
.
When curvature filtering drives
\mathsf{F}\to 0
,
the algorithm attains the optimal trade–off between energy release
(deterministic descent) and constraint enforcement
(stochastic or adaptive correction).

Thermodynamic analogy
[
26
]
.

The flat configuration corresponds to a thermodynamic equilibrium.

S_{h}
is the total entropy production rate,
and
\mathcal{V}
is the conserved free energy.
When
S_{h}=0
the process is reversible,
and the algorithm moves along level sets of
\mathcal{V}
.
When curvature accumulates,
S_{h}>0
measures numerical “heat”:
variance, instability, or excess adaptation.
Applying the curvature filter corresponds to restoring reversibility
by cancelling internal entropy sources.

Modern synthesis.

The classical principles of Lyapunov stability,
Hamiltonian conservation, and Pontryagin optimality
appear as exact discrete projections of the same algebraic condition

\mathsf{F}=0
in the jet algebra.
The curvature action
S_{h}
and the potential
\mathcal{V}

are therefore minimal discrete forms
that subsume all these energy principles:
Lyapunov’s monotonicity, Hamilton’s symplecticity,
and Pontryagin’s optimal control
all coincide as different manifestations
of flatness of the discrete update algebra.

2.2
Results

2.2.1
Minimax Principle

Fix a policy
\Pi=(t_{k},s_{k},h_{k})_{k=0}^{N-1}
. For each step
k
choose any finite partition of its parameter cell
\mathcal{P}_{k}\subset\mathcal{P}=\mathcal{T}\times\mathcal{S}
into rectangles
\square
. Define

S^{(N)}[\Pi]\ :=\ \sum_{k=0}^{N-1}\ \sum_{\square\in\mathcal{P}_{k}}\ \big\|\log\mathrm{Hol}(\square)\big\|_{F}^{2}.

(54)

By additivity of
S_{h}
on disjoint rectangles and gauge-invariance, for any two partitions of the same union

\displaystyle\sum_{\square\in\mathcal{P}_{k}}\big\|\log\mathrm{Hol}(\square)\big\|_{F}^{2}\ =\ \sum_{\square^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}_{k}^{\prime}}\big\|\log\mathrm{Hol}(\square^{\prime})\big\|_{F}^{2},

\displaystyle S^{(N)}[\Pi]\ =\ \sum_{\square\subset\Gamma_{\Pi}}\big\|\log\mathrm{Hol}(\square)\big\|_{F}^{2},

(55)

where
\Gamma_{\Pi}:=\bigcup_{k}\mathcal{P}_{k}
. Thus
S^{(N)}
depends only on
\Gamma_{\Pi}
. This leads to Chebyshev-type minimax filters for discrete holonomy and their rational Zolotarev counterparts in stiff spectral windows
[
100
,
1
,
57
]
.

Write elementary updates as
r_{t_{k}}(h_{k})=\exp\Omega_{k}
and
d_{s_{k}}(h_{k})=\exp\Psi_{k}
(their logarithms exist in the completed free Lie algebra). The
N
–step composition admits the Magnus expansion

\log\Big(\prod_{k=0}^{N-1}e^{\Psi_{k}}e^{\Omega_{k}}\Big)\ =\ \sum_{k=0}^{N-1}(\Psi_{k}+\Omega_{k})\ +\ \frac{1}{2}\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}[\Psi_{k},\Omega_{k}]\ +\ \sum_{0\leq i<j\leq N-1}\Phi_{ij}\ +\ \cdots,

(56)

where each
\Phi_{ij}
is a universal
\mathbb{Q}
–linear combination of nested commutators in
\{\Psi_{i},\Omega_{i},\Psi_{j},\Omega_{j}\}
of bracket–depth
\geq 2

[
9
,
55
,
42
,
99
,
78
]
. For a single rectangle
\square
spanned by
(t_{k},s_{k})
and
(t_{k+1},s_{k+1})

[
53
,
16
]
,

\log\mathrm{Hol}(\square)=\log\big(e^{\Psi_{k+1}}e^{\Omega_{k+1}}e^{-\Psi_{k}}e^{-\Omega_{k}}\big)=\frac{1}{2}\big([\Psi_{k+1}-\Psi_{k},\Omega_{k+1}-\Omega_{k}]\big)\ +\ O(h_{k}^{3}),

(57)

hence, using the Frobenius norm
\|\cdot\|_{F}
and
\|[A,B]\|_{F}\leq 2\|A\|_{F}\|B\|_{F}
,

\big\|[\Psi_{k},\Omega_{k}]\big\|_{F}\ \leq\ c_{1}\,\sum_{\square\in\mathcal{P}_{k}}\big\|\log\mathrm{Hol}(\square)\big\|_{F}\ +\ c_{2}\,h_{k}^{3}.

(58)

Similarly, every nested commutator appearing in
\Phi_{ij}
is bounded by a finite linear combination of
\|\log\mathrm{Hol}(\square)\|_{F}
over rectangles lying between steps
i
and
j
. Summing (
58
) over
k
and applying Cauchy–Schwarz,

\displaystyle\Big\|\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k}[\Psi_{k},\Omega_{k}]\ +\ \sum_{i<j}\Phi_{ij}\ +\ \cdots\Big\|_{F}\

\displaystyle\leq\ C\,\Big(\sum_{\square\subset\Gamma_{\Pi}}\big\|\log\mathrm{Hol}(\square)\big\|_{F}^{2}\Big)^{1/2}

\displaystyle=\ C\,\big(S^{(N)}[\Pi]\big)^{1/2}.

(59)

Denote the remainder in (
56
) by
R_{N}
; then (
2.2.1
) gives

\|R_{N}\|_{F}\ \leq\ C\,\big(S^{(N)}[\Pi]\big)^{1/2}.

(60)

Assume jet–flatness to order
\alpha
(Theorem
1
): all mixed brackets of total degree
\leq\alpha
vanish. Then
R_{N}=O(h^{\alpha+1})
and

\prod_{k=0}^{N-1}U_{t_{k},s_{k}}=\exp\!\Big(\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}(\Psi_{k}+\Omega_{k})\Big)\ +\ O(h^{\alpha+1}).

(61)

In the calibrated flat gauge the generator
\sum_{k}(\Psi_{k}+\Omega_{k})
depends on
U
only through the drift
H
; hence on the state space

\exp\!\Big(\sum_{k}(\Psi_{k}+\Omega_{k})\Big)\ =\ p_{N}(H)\quad\text{or}\quad r_{N}(H),

(62)

for a polynomial
p_{N}
(or a rational
r_{N}
) determined by
\Pi
. Combining (
60
)–(
62
) with the spectral calculus,

\sup_{\lambda\in\Sigma}\ \Big|\,p_{N}(\lambda)-e^{-Nh\lambda}\,\Big|\ \leq\ C^{\prime}\,\big(S^{(N)}[\Pi]\big)^{1/2}\ +\ O(h^{\alpha+1}).

(63)

Therefore minimizing
S^{(N)}[\Pi]
over admissible policies is equivalent to minimizing the worst–case spectral error of the induced filter:

\inf_{\Pi}\ S^{(N)}[\Pi]\ =\ \inf_{p_{N}}\ \sup_{\lambda\in\Sigma}\ |p_{N}(\lambda)-e^{-Nh\lambda}|\quad\text{(polynomial case)}.

(64)

By extremal approximation on compact spectra, the right-hand side is attained by the Chebyshev (polynomial) and Zolotarev (rational) filters; their coefficients are realized by suitable
\Pi
because (
62
) maps policies onto the corresponding filter classes. In particular, the acceleration weights used in optimal first–order schemes coincide with the coefficients of these extremal filters, and the flatness condition ensures that the discrete composition achieves the minimax value up to
O(h^{\alpha+1})
.

Theorem 5

(Trajectory minimax reduction)
.

Fix
N\in\mathbb{N}
and a compact spectrum
\Sigma\subset\mathbb{R}
.
Let
\Pi=(t_{k},s_{k},h_{k})_{k=0}^{N-1}
be an admissible policy and define

S^{(N)}[\Pi]:=\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}\sum_{\square\in\mathcal{P}_{k}}\|\log\mathrm{Hol}(\square)\|_{F}^{2},

(65)

where
\mathcal{P}_{k}
is any finite rectangular partition of the parameter cell at step
k
(the value is independent of the partition).
Assume jet–flatness of order
\alpha\geq 2
(Theorem
1
) and use the calibrated gauge (Theorem
2
).
Then:

(i)
There exists a polynomial
p_{N}
(or a rational
r_{N}
) determined by
\Pi
such that

\prod_{k=0}^{N-1}U_{t_{k},s_{k}}=p_{N}(H)+R_{N},\quad\|R_{N}\|_{F}\leq C_{1}\big(S^{(N)}[\Pi]\big)^{1/2}+C_{2}\,h^{\alpha+1},

(66)

and consequently

\sup_{\lambda\in\Sigma}|p_{N}(\lambda)-e^{-Nh\lambda}|\leq C_{1}^{\prime}\big(S^{(N)}[\Pi]\big)^{1/2}+C_{2}^{\prime}h^{\alpha+1}.

(67)

(ii)
Conversely, for any polynomial (or rational)
q_{N}
of degree
\leq N
there exists a policy
\Pi[q_{N}]
with the corresponding filter
p_{N}=q_{N}
for which

S^{(N)}[\Pi[q_{N}]]\leq K_{1}\Big(\sup_{\lambda\in\Sigma}|q_{N}(\lambda)-e^{-Nh\lambda}|\Big)^{2}+K_{2}h^{2(\alpha+1)}.

(68)

(iii)
Hence, up to constants
C,K
depending only on the Frobenius pairing, the jet order
\alpha
, and the admissible update class,

C^{-1}\Big(\min_{q_{N}}\sup_{\lambda\in\Sigma}|q_{N}(\lambda)-e^{-Nh\lambda}|\Big)^{2}\leq\inf_{\Pi}S^{(N)}[\Pi]\leq K\Big(\min_{q_{N}}\sup_{\lambda\in\Sigma}|q_{N}(\lambda)-e^{-Nh\lambda}|\Big)^{2},

(69)

and the minimizers on both sides are the Chebyshev (polynomial) or Zolotarev (rational) extremal filters.

Proof.

All norms are Frobenius; every equality and inequality is derived explicitly.

Step 1 (additivity and partition independence).
For fixed
k
, let
\mathcal{P}_{k}
and
\mathcal{P}_{k}^{\prime}
be any two partitions of the same cell.

\sum_{\square\in\mathcal{P}_{k}}\|\log\mathrm{Hol}(\square)\|_{F}^{2}
is partition-invariant and additive over disjoint regions.
Hence

S^{(N)}[\Pi]=\sum_{\square\subset\Gamma_{\Pi}}\|\log\mathrm{Hol}(\square)\|_{F}^{2},\qquad\Gamma_{\Pi}:=\bigcup_{k}\mathcal{P}_{k}.

(70)

Step 2 (Magnus expansion and localization of remainders).
Let
\Omega_{k}=\log r_{t_{k}}(h_{k})
and
\Psi_{k}=\log d_{s_{k}}(h_{k})
.
By the Magnus series,

\log\Big(\prod_{k=0}^{N-1}e^{\Psi_{k}}e^{\Omega_{k}}\Big)=\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}(\Psi_{k}+\Omega_{k})+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}[\Psi_{k},\Omega_{k}]+\sum_{0\leq i<j\leq N-1}\Phi_{ij}+\cdots,

(71)

where each
\Phi_{ij}
is a finite
\mathbb{Q}
–linear combination of nested commutators in
\{\Psi_{i},\Omega_{i},\Psi_{j},\Omega_{j}\}
of bracket depth
\geq 2
.
For a single rectangle
\square
between
(t_{k},s_{k})
and
(t_{k+1},s_{k+1})
, the BCH formula gives

\log\mathrm{Hol}(\square)=\log\big(e^{\Psi_{k+1}}e^{\Omega_{k+1}}e^{-\Psi_{k}}e^{-\Omega_{k}}\big)=\tfrac{1}{2}[\Psi_{k+1}-\Psi_{k},\Omega_{k+1}-\Omega_{k}]+R_{\square},

(72)

where
R_{\square}
is a sum of nested commutators of total degree
\geq 3
in
\{\Psi_{k},\Omega_{k},\Psi_{k+1},\Omega_{k+1}\}
.
