Step 3 (bounding the remainders by
\log\mathrm{Hol}
).
Using
\|[A,B]\|_{F}\leq 2\|A\|_{F}\|B\|_{F}
and linearity of the Frobenius norm, summing (
72
) over
\square\in\mathcal{P}_{k}
and telescoping in
k
yields

\Big\|\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}[\Psi_{k},\Omega_{k}]\Big\|_{F}\leq C_{a}\sum_{\square\subset\Gamma_{\Pi}}\|\log\mathrm{Hol}(\square)\|_{F}+C_{b}\,h^{\alpha+1}.

(73)

Each nested commutator in
\sum_{i<j}\Phi_{ij}
is supported on the union of rectangles between steps
i
and
j
and satisfies the same bound.
Applying Cauchy–Schwarz and combining,

\Big\|\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k}[\Psi_{k},\Omega_{k}]+\sum_{i<j}\Phi_{ij}+\cdots\Big\|_{F}\leq C\Big(\sum_{\square\subset\Gamma_{\Pi}}\|\log\mathrm{Hol}(\square)\|_{F}^{2}\Big)^{1/2}+C^{\prime}\,h^{\alpha+1}.

(74)

Let
R_{N}
denote the total remainder in (
71
); combining (
70
) and (
74
) gives

\|R_{N}\|_{F}\leq C_{1}\big(S^{(N)}[\Pi]\big)^{1/2}+C_{2}\,h^{\alpha+1},

(75)

which is exactly (
66
).

Step 4 (transition to spectral representation).
In the calibrated flat gauge (Theorem
2
),

\sum_{k}(\Psi_{k}+\Omega_{k})=-Nh\,H_{\mathrm{eff}}+O(h^{\alpha+1})
,
and the corresponding operator acts on the state space as a polynomial (or rational) filter
p_{N}(H)
.
Therefore for each
\lambda\in\Sigma
,

|p_{N}(\lambda)-e^{-Nh\lambda}|\leq\|R_{N}\|_{F},

(76)

and substituting (
75
) yields (
67
).

Step 5 (reverse estimate and realizability).
Given any polynomial
q_{N}
of degree
\leq N
,
A policy
\Pi[q_{N}]
realizing
q_{N}(H)
to leading order:

\prod_{k}U_{t_{k},s_{k}}=q_{N}(H)+\widetilde{R}_{N},\qquad\|\widetilde{R}_{N}\|_{F}\leq c\,h^{\alpha+1}.

(77)

Repeating the localization argument for this composition yields

S^{(N)}[\Pi[q_{N}]]\leq K_{1}\Big(\sup_{\lambda\in\Sigma}|q_{N}(\lambda)-e^{-Nh\lambda}|\Big)^{2}+K_{2}h^{2(\alpha+1)},

(78)

which is exactly (
68
).

Step 6 (equivalence of optimization problems).
From (
67
),

\min_{q_{N}}\sup_{\lambda}|q_{N}(\lambda)-e^{-Nh\lambda}|\leq\inf_{\Pi}\Big(C_{1}^{\prime}\,S^{(N)}[\Pi]^{1/2}+C_{2}^{\prime}h^{\alpha+1}\Big),

so the left-hand side is bounded by
C\,\inf_{\Pi}S^{(N)}[\Pi]^{1/2}+o(1)
.
Conversely, (
68
) for the extremal
q_{N}
(Chebyshev/Zolotarev) gives a policy
\Pi^{\star}
with

S^{(N)}[\Pi^{\star}]\leq K\Big(\min_{q_{N}}\sup_{\lambda}|q_{N}(\lambda)-e^{-Nh\lambda}|\Big)^{2}+o(1).

Combining both inequalities and absorbing
o(1)
into constants (for fixed
N,\alpha
) yields (
69
).
The minimizers are the classical extremal filters; their attainability follows from (
77
).
∎

Continuing from Theorem
5
, the global deviation of the discrete composition from the ideal exponential flow can be controlled explicitly through the accumulated curvature energy.
Starting from the Magnus expansion (
71
) and the bound (
75
), one has for any policy
\Pi

\displaystyle\log\!\Big(\prod_{k=0}^{N-1}e^{\Psi_{k}}e^{\Omega_{k}}\Big)=\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}(\Psi_{k}+\Omega_{k})+R_{N},

\displaystyle\|R_{N}\|_{F}\leq C_{1}\big(S^{(N)}[\Pi]\big)^{1/2}+C_{2}h^{\alpha+1}.

(79)

For a flat calibrated policy (
\mathsf{F}=0
up to order
\alpha
), all mixed brackets vanish to this order and the exponential composition reduces to a single exponential:

\prod_{k=0}^{N-1}U_{t_{k},s_{k}}=\exp\!\Big(\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}(\Psi_{k}+\Omega_{k})\Big)+O(h^{\alpha+1})=\exp(-NhH_{\mathrm{eff}})+O(h^{\alpha+1}).

(80)

Acting on any eigenvector
v_{\lambda}
of
H
with eigenvalue
\lambda\in\Sigma
, the operator
\prod_{k}U_{t_{k},s_{k}}
gives

\prod_{k=0}^{N-1}U_{t_{k},s_{k}}\,v_{\lambda}=\big(p_{N}(\lambda)+\rho_{N}(\lambda)\big)v_{\lambda},

(81)

where
p_{N}(\lambda)
is the filter associated with the main exponential in (
80
) and
\rho_{N}(\lambda)
is the scalar remainder induced by
R_{N}
.
Taking norms and using
\|\rho_{N}(H)\|_{F}\leq\|R_{N}\|_{F}
yields

|\,p_{N}(\lambda)-e^{-Nh\lambda}\,|\leq C_{1}\big(S^{(N)}[\Pi]\big)^{1/2}+C_{2}h^{\alpha+1},\qquad\forall\lambda\in\Sigma.

(82)

Therefore, for every fixed horizon
Nh>0
, the spectral radius of the composed update satisfies

\rho\!\Big(\prod_{k=0}^{N-1}U_{t_{k},s_{k}}\Big)\leq e^{-Nh\mu}+C_{1}\big(S^{(N)}[\Pi]\big)^{1/2}+C_{2}h^{\alpha+1},

(83)

where
\mu=\min\Sigma>0
.
If
S^{(N)}[\Pi]
is bounded independently of
N
, the method converges geometrically with rate
e^{-Nh\mu}
; minimizing
S^{(N)}
ensures that this rate coincides with the minimax limit of Nemirovski–Yudin.

To include the nonasymptotic “volume” functional, consider the covariance (or metric) sequence induced by the adjoint structure
d_{s}=d_{t}^{\ast}
.
At each step
k
, let

\Sigma_{k+1}=J_{k}\Sigma_{k}J_{k}^{\top}+Q_{k},\qquad J_{k}=\exp(h_{k}(\Omega_{t_{k},1}+\Psi_{s_{k},1})+B_{k}),\quad Q_{k}\succeq 0,

(84)

where
B_{k}
collects all commutator corrections of total order
\geq 2
.
Applying the matrix identity
\det(A+B)\leq\det(A)\exp(\mathrm{tr}(A^{-1}B))
for
A\succ 0
, the concavity of
\log\det
, and submultiplicativity of norms, one obtains

\log\det\Sigma_{k+1}\leq\log\det(J_{k}\Sigma_{k}J_{k}^{\top})+\mathrm{tr}(\Sigma_{k}^{-1/2}J_{k}^{-1}Q_{k}J_{k}^{-\top}\Sigma_{k}^{-1/2})+\|B_{k}\|_{F}^{2}.

(85)

Iterating (
85
) over
k=0,\dots,N-1
and summing gives

\log\det\Sigma_{N}-\log\det\Sigma_{0}\leq 2\sum_{k}\mathrm{tr}\log|J_{k}|+\sum_{k}\mathrm{tr}(\Sigma_{k}^{-1}Q_{k})+\sum_{k}\|B_{k}\|_{F}^{2}.

(86)

The commutator term
\|B_{k}\|_{F}^{2}
can be estimated by the same curvature energy as in (
72
):

\sum_{k}\|B_{k}\|_{F}^{2}\leq C_{3}S^{(N)}[\Pi].

(87)

Combining (
86
) and (
87
) yields

\Delta(\log\det\Sigma_{N}):=\log\det\Sigma_{N}-\log\det\Sigma_{0}\leq C_{3}S^{(N)}[\Pi]+C_{4}\sum_{k}\mathrm{tr}(\Sigma_{k}^{-1}Q_{k}).

(88)

When the structure is flat (
\mathsf{F}=0
), each
B_{k}=0
and
Q_{k}
represents purely diffusive noise.
In this case
\log\det\Sigma_{N}
grows exactly as
\sum_{k}\mathrm{tr}(\Sigma_{k}^{-1}Q_{k})
, which is the optimal
\log\det
–minimax criterion for nonasymptotic estimation (Nemirovski’s ellipsoid type bound).
For any nonflat policy, curvature adds a positive excess bounded by
C_{3}S^{(N)}[\Pi]
; minimizing
S^{(N)}
therefore removes all geometric dissipation and restores the optimal volume decay rate of the flat configuration.

Theorem 6

(Joint minimax and
t
/
s
factorization)
.

Fix
N\in\mathbb{N}
and a compact spectrum
\Sigma\subset\mathbb{R}
. For an admissible policy
\Pi=(t_{k},s_{k},h_{k})_{k=0}^{N-1}
, define the cumulative curvature energy

S^{(N)}[\Pi]=\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}\sum_{\square\in\mathcal{P}_{k}}\|\log\mathrm{Hol}(\square)\|_{F}^{2},

and for each rectangle
\square
spanning
(t_{k},s_{k})
,
(t_{k+1},s_{k+1})
set
\Delta\Psi_{k}:=\Psi_{k+1}-\Psi_{k}
,
\Delta\Omega_{k}:=\Omega_{k+1}-\Omega_{k}
, where
\Psi_{k}=\log d_{s_{k}}(h_{k})
,
\Omega_{k}=\log r_{t_{k}}(h_{k})
. Define the one–axis energies

\displaystyle S_{t}^{(N)}[\Pi]:=\sum_{k}\sum_{\square\in\mathcal{P}_{k}}\Big\|\frac{1}{2}\,[\Delta\Psi_{k},\ \Omega_{k}]\Big\|_{F}^{2},

\displaystyle S_{s}^{(N)}[\Pi]:=\sum_{k}\sum_{\square\in\mathcal{P}_{k}}\Big\|\frac{1}{2}\,[\Psi_{k},\ \Delta\Omega_{k}]\Big\|_{F}^{2}.

(89)

Assume jet–flatness of order
\alpha\geq 2
is available as a hypothesis when invoked. Then:

(i) (Two–sided decomposition)
For every policy
\Pi
there exist constants
c_{1},c_{2}>0
independent of
\Pi
such that

\frac{1}{2}\big(S_{t}^{(N)}[\Pi]+S_{s}^{(N)}[\Pi]\big)\ -\ c_{1}\,h^{2(\alpha+1)}\ \leq\ S^{(N)}[\Pi]\ \leq\ 2\big(S_{t}^{(N)}[\Pi]+S_{s}^{(N)}[\Pi]\big)\ +\ c_{2}\,h^{2(\alpha+1)}.

(90)

(ii) (Flatness
\Leftrightarrow
additive factorization)
The following are equivalent:

\mathsf{F}=0\ \text{ to order }\alpha\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad S^{(N)}[\Pi]\ =\ S_{t}^{(N)}[\Pi]+S_{s}^{(N)}[\Pi]\ +\ O(h^{\alpha+1}).

(91)

(iii) (Joint minimax = two one–parameter minimaxes)
Under
\mathsf{F}=0
to order
\alpha
,

\inf_{\Pi}\ S^{(N)}[\Pi]\ =\ \inf_{\Pi_{t}}S_{t}^{(N)}[\Pi_{t}]\ +\ \inf_{\Pi_{s}}S_{s}^{(N)}[\Pi_{s}]\ +\ O(h^{\alpha+1}),

(92)

and the infima on the right are attained by the one–parameter Chebyshev/Zolotarev extremal filters (drift–only and diffusion–only). The composed policy that concatenates these two extremals (in any order) attains the joint infimum on the left up to
O(h^{\alpha+1})
. Conversely, if a policy
\widehat{\Pi}
attains equality in (
92
) up to
o(1)
and its
t
– and
s
–marginals are one–parameter extremals, then necessarily
\mathsf{F}=0
to order
\alpha
.

Proof.

All norms are Frobenius. Rectangular partitions
\mathcal{P}_{k}
are arbitrary; by additivity and gauge–invariance the values do not depend on the choice of partition.

Step 1 (BCH for an elementary rectangle; exact algebraic split).
For a fixed rectangle
\square
spanning
(t_{k},s_{k})
and
(t_{k+1},s_{k+1})
,

\log\mathrm{Hol}(\square)=\log\big(e^{\Psi_{k+1}}e^{\Omega_{k+1}}e^{-\Psi_{k}}e^{-\Omega_{k}}\big)=\frac{1}{2}\,[\Delta\Psi_{k},\Delta\Omega_{k}]\ +\ R_{\square},

(93)

where
R_{\square}
is a finite
\mathbb{Q}
–linear combination of nested commutators of total degree
\geq 3
in
\{\Psi_{k},\Omega_{k},\Psi_{k+1},\Omega_{k+1}\}
(BCH). Expand the bracket bilinearly:

[\Delta\Psi_{k},\Delta\Omega_{k}]\ =\ [\Delta\Psi_{k},\ \Omega_{k}]\ +\ [\Psi_{k},\ \Delta\Omega_{k}]\ +\ [\Delta\Psi_{k},\ \Delta\Omega_{k}]^{\prime}.

(94)

The last term
[\Delta\Psi_{k},\Delta\Omega_{k}]^{\prime}
collects all products where both increments appear; since
\Delta\Psi_{k}=O(h_{k})
and
\Delta\Omega_{k}=O(h_{k})
, one has

\big\|[\Delta\Psi_{k},\Delta\Omega_{k}]^{\prime}\big\|_{F}\ \leq\ C\,h_{k}^{2}.

(95)

By (
93
)–(
94
),

\log\mathrm{Hol}(\square)\ =\ A_{k,\square}\ +\ B_{k,\square}\ +\ E_{k,\square},\quad A_{k,\square}:=\tfrac{1}{2}[\Delta\Psi_{k},\Omega_{k}],\ \ B_{k,\square}:=\tfrac{1}{2}[\Psi_{k},\Delta\Omega_{k}],

(96)

where the error
E_{k,\square}:=\tfrac{1}{2}[\Delta\Psi_{k},\Delta\Omega_{k}]^{\prime}+R_{\square}
satisfies

\|E_{k,\square}\|_{F}\ \leq\ C_{0}\,h_{k}^{2}\ +\ C_{1}\,h_{k}^{\alpha+1}.

(97)

Step 2 (Two–sided norm bounds per rectangle).
For any matrices
X,Y,Z
,

\|X+Y+Z\|_{F}^{2}\ \leq\ 2\|X\|_{F}^{2}+2\|Y\|_{F}^{2}+2\|Z\|_{F}^{2},

\|X+Y+Z\|_{F}^{2}\ \geq\ \tfrac{1}{2}\|X\|_{F}^{2}+\tfrac{1}{2}\|Y\|_{F}^{2}-\|Z\|_{F}^{2}.

Applying these to (
96
) gives

\|\log\mathrm{Hol}(\square)\|_{F}^{2}\ \leq\ 2\|A_{k,\square}\|_{F}^{2}+2\|B_{k,\square}\|_{F}^{2}+2\|E_{k,\square}\|_{F}^{2},

(98)

\|\log\mathrm{Hol}(\square)\|_{F}^{2}\ \geq\ \tfrac{1}{2}\|A_{k,\square}\|_{F}^{2}+\tfrac{1}{2}\|B_{k,\square}\|_{F}^{2}-\|E_{k,\square}\|_{F}^{2}.

(99)

Step 3 (Summation over the trajectory).
Summing (
98
)–(
99
) over all rectangles and using (
6
) yields

S^{(N)}[\Pi]\ \leq\ 2\big(S_{t}^{(N)}[\Pi]+S_{s}^{(N)}[\Pi]\big)\ +\ 2\sum_{k,\square}\|E_{k,\square}\|_{F}^{2},

S^{(N)}[\Pi]\ \geq\ \tfrac{1}{2}\big(S_{t}^{(N)}[\Pi]+S_{s}^{(N)}[\Pi]\big)\ -\ \sum_{k,\square}\|E_{k,\square}\|_{F}^{2}.

By (
97
) and the boundedness of the number/size of rectangles per step (fixed
N
and admissible partitions),

\sum_{k,\square}\|E_{k,\square}\|_{F}^{2}\ \leq\ c\,h^{2(\alpha+1)}.

(100)

Inserting this into the two inequalities gives exactly (
90
).

Step 4 (Flatness
\Rightarrow
additive factorization).
Under jet–flatness to order
\alpha
, the mixed Lie monomials of total degree
\leq\alpha
vanish. In particular, the error terms in (
93
)–(
96
) satisfy
E_{k,\square}=O(h_{k}^{\alpha+1})
, and the bilinear remainder
[\Delta\Psi_{k},\Delta\Omega_{k}]^{\prime}=O(h_{k}^{\alpha+1})
. Summing and using (
100
) gives

S^{(N)}[\Pi]\ =\ \sum_{k,\square}\|A_{k,\square}+B_{k,\square}\|_{F}^{2}\ +\ O(h^{\alpha+1}).

But
A_{k,\square}
and
B_{k,\square}
involve disjoint increments and commute to leading order under flatness, hence

\|A_{k,\square}+B_{k,\square}\|_{F}^{2}\ =\ \|A_{k,\square}\|_{F}^{2}+\|B_{k,\square}\|_{F}^{2}\ +\ O(h^{\alpha+1}),

and after summation we obtain (
91
).

Step 5 (Additive factorization
\Rightarrow
flatness).
Assume (
91
). Then necessarily
\sum_{k,\square}\langle A_{k,\square},B_{k,\square}\rangle_{F}=O(h^{\alpha+1})
because

\|A+B\|_{F}^{2}=\|A\|_{F}^{2}+\|B\|_{F}^{2}+2\langle A,B\rangle_{F},

and the equality
S^{(N)}=S_{t}^{(N)}+S_{s}^{(N)}+O(h^{\alpha+1})
forces the cross inner product to vanish to this order. Varying the policy within a small neighborhood so that only one of the increments
(\Delta\Psi_{k},\Delta\Omega_{k})
changes at a time, bilinearity implies
\langle A_{k,\square},B_{k,\square}\rangle_{F}=O(h^{\alpha+1})
for each rectangle. This in turn forces the mixed brackets
[\Delta\Psi_{k},\Omega_{k}]
and
[\Psi_{k},\Delta\Omega_{k}]
to be orthogonal in Frobenius pairing for all
k,\square
, which is only possible (uniformly in all such variations) if all mixed Lie monomials of total degree
\leq\alpha
vanish. By Theorem
1
, this is exactly jet–flatness to order
\alpha
.

Step 6 (Joint minimax).
Under flatness, (
91
) and (
90
) imply

\displaystyle\inf_{\Pi}S^{(N)}[\Pi]

\displaystyle=\inf_{\Pi}\big(S_{t}^{(N)}[\Pi]+S_{s}^{(N)}[\Pi]\big)+O(h^{\alpha+1})

\displaystyle=\inf_{\Pi_{t}}S_{t}^{(N)}[\Pi_{t}]+\inf_{\Pi_{s}}S_{s}^{(N)}[\Pi_{s}]+O(h^{\alpha+1}).

By Theorem
5
applied on a single axis,
\inf_{\Pi_{t}}S_{t}^{(N)}
and
\inf_{\Pi_{s}}S_{s}^{(N)}
are attained by the one–parameter Chebyshev/Zolotarev extremals (polynomial/rational), and concatenating these two commuting extremals attains the sum. Conversely, if a policy
\widehat{\Pi}
attains the sum of the axis infima up to
o(1)
while its axis marginals are extremal, then any nonzero curvature would add a strictly positive excess by (
90
), contradicting optimality; hence
\mathsf{F}=0
to order
\alpha
.
∎

2.2.2
Semi–algebraic Completion

Fix the rectangular policy strip and its finite cell partition. Let
K
be the resulting oriented
2
–complex with vertex set
V(K)
, edge set
E(K)
, and
2
–cells
F(K)
(the rectangles). Denote by

C^{p}(K;M):=\{p\text{–cochains on }K\text{ with values in }M\},\qquad\delta:C^{p}(K;M)\to C^{p+1}(K;M)

the cellular coboundary. As coefficient module take the underlying real vector space

M:=\big(\widehat{\mathfrak{U}}^{\mathrm{fil}}\big)^{(\alpha)}
equipped with the Frobenius inner product
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{F}
and the adjoint action

\operatorname{Ad}
(truncated at jet–order
\alpha
).

Discrete MC
2
–cochain.

For each face
\square\in F(K)
define

c_{K}(\square)\ :=\ \log\mathrm{Hol}(\square)\ \in\ M,\qquad c_{K}\in C^{2}(K;M),

(101)

with the logarithm understood modulo
O(h^{\alpha+1})
as in (BCH/Magnus). The curvature energy is

S^{(N)}[\Pi]\ =\ \sum_{\square\in F(K)}\ \|c_{K}(\square)\|_{F}^{2}\ =\ \|c_{K}\|_{L^{2}(K;M)}^{2}.

(102)

Gauge as a
1
–cochain.

A (jet–class) gauge assigns to every edge
e\in E(K)
an element

\Xi(e)\in M
and acts on
c_{K}
by

c_{K}\ \mapsto\ c_{K}^{\prime}\ =\ c_{K}\ -\ \delta\Xi\ +\ R_{\Xi},\qquad\|R_{\Xi}\|_{L^{2}(K;M)}\ =\ O(h^{\alpha+1}).

(103)

This is the exact discrete counterpart of the linearized conjugation:
on each
1
–skeleton loop bounding a face, the product–conjugation by

\exp(h\Xi)
changes
\log\mathrm{Hol}
by the cellular coboundary

\delta\Xi
; the remainder
R_{\Xi}
collects Lie monomials of total degree

\geq\alpha+1
(BCH truncation). No continuous differentials are used.

Vershik–type semi–algebraic model.

Admissible policies
\Pi
(choice of update families and jet coefficients up to order
\alpha
) form a semi–algebraic set:
all constraints (resolvent/Padé/CFET coefficients, jet flatness bounds, and BCH identities up to order
\alpha
) are polynomial equalities/inequalities in the coefficients
[
11
,
17
]
. The map

\Pi\ \longmapsto\ c_{K}(\Pi)\ \in\ C^{2}(K;M)

(104)

is semi–algebraic, and so is the feasibility set

\{\Pi,\Xi:\ c_{K}(\Pi)-\delta\Xi=O(h^{\alpha+1})\}
.
This matches Vershik’s paradigm: algorithms are constrained by semi–algebraic relations; existence of a global “section/certificate” is a solvability of a semi–algebraic system
[
114
,
113
]
.

Theorem 7

(Discrete cohomology class and energy minimization
[
38
,
36
]
)
.

Let
c_{K}\in C^{2}(K;M)
be given by (
101
). Consider the least–squares gauge–reduction problem

\inf_{\Xi\in C^{1}(K;M)}\ \big\|\,c_{K}-\delta\Xi\,\big\|_{L^{2}(K;M)}^{2}.

(105)

Then, with
\delta^{\ast}
denoting the
L^{2}
–adjoint of
\delta
,

\Xi^{\star}\ \text{ solves }\ \eqref{eq:lsq}\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\delta^{\ast}\delta\,\Xi^{\star}=\delta^{\ast}c_{K},

(106)

and the minimum value equals the squared
L^{2}
–norm of the harmonic representative of the cohomology class
[c_{K}]\in H^{2}(K;M)
:

\inf_{\Xi}\ \|c_{K}-\delta\Xi\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\ =\ \|\,\mathsf{H}(c_{K})\,\|_{L^{2}}^{2},

(107)

where
\mathsf{H}
is the
L^{2}
–orthogonal projector onto the space of
2
–cocycles orthogonal to
\mathrm{im}\,\delta
(the discrete Hodge projector). Consequently,

c_{-}\,\|[c_{K}]\|_{H^{2}}^{2}\ \leq\ \inf_{\Xi}\ \|c_{K}-\delta\Xi\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\ \leq\ c_{+}\,\|[c_{K}]\|_{H^{2}}^{2},

(108)

with constants
c_{\pm}>0
determined by the conditioning of the discrete Hodge operator on
K
.

Proof.

All spaces are finite–dimensional. The functional in (
105
) is strictly convex; its Gâteaux derivative in the direction
\eta\in C^{1}
is

\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\epsilon}\Big\|c_{K}-\delta(\Xi+\epsilon\eta)\Big\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\Big|_{\epsilon=0}=-2\langle c_{K}-\delta\Xi,\ \delta\eta\rangle_{L^{2}}=-2\langle\delta^{\ast}(c_{K}-\delta\Xi),\ \eta\rangle_{L^{2}}.

Thus (
106
) is the necessary and sufficient optimality condition. Writing the discrete Hodge decomposition

C^{2}(K;M)=\underbrace{\mathrm{im}\,\delta}_{\text{exact}}\ \oplus\ \underbrace{\ker\delta^{\ast}}_{\text{co–closed}}\!,

with orthogonal sum, every
c_{K}
splits uniquely as
c_{K}=\delta\Xi^{\star}+\mathsf{H}(c_{K})
where
\Xi^{\star}
solves (
106
) and
\mathsf{H}(c_{K})\in\ker\delta^{\ast}
. Orthogonality gives

\|c_{K}-\delta\Xi\|_{L^{2}}^{2}=\|\mathsf{H}(c_{K})\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\|\delta\Xi^{\star}-\delta\Xi\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\ \geq\ \|\mathsf{H}(c_{K})\|_{L^{2}}^{2},

with equality at
\Xi=\Xi^{\star}
, proving (
107
). Since the
L^{2}
–norm of
\mathsf{H}(c_{K})
is equivalent (by finite–dimensional norm equivalence) to any fixed Hilbertian norm of the cohomology class
[c_{K}]\in H^{2}(K;M)
, we obtain (
108
).
∎

Corollary 5

(Exact flat calibration and semi–algebraic section)
.

The following are equivalent
[
11
,
114
]
:

[c_{K}]=0\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\exists\,\Xi\in C^{1}(K;M)\ \text{s.t.}\ c_{K}=\delta\Xi\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\inf_{\Xi}\|c_{K}-\delta\Xi\|_{L^{2}}^{2}=0.

(109)

Under the jet–order truncation, the exact conjugation by
\exp(h\Xi)
produces
c_{K}^{\prime}=c_{K}-\delta\Xi+O(h^{\alpha+1})
, so
[c_{K}]=0
implies a calibrated policy with
S^{(N)}[\Pi]=O(h^{2(\alpha+1)})
. Conversely, if
S^{(N)}[\Pi]=O(h^{2(\alpha+1)})
for a family of partitions, then
c_{K}=\delta\Xi+O(h^{\alpha+1})
and hence
[c_{K}]=0
in the jet–class. The feasibility condition
c_{K}=\delta\Xi
is a semi–algebraic system in the jet coefficients of
\Pi
and the entries of
\Xi
(all relations are polynomial identities from BCH up to order
\alpha
); thus, in Vershik’s sense, a certificate/section exists if and only if
[c_{K}]=0
(cf. Vershik’s semi–algebraic certificates and invariant selections).

Theorem 8

(Vershik–type extremality and joint minimax)
.

Let
\Sigma
be the spectral set and let the admissible families (drift/diffusion) be fixed. Then:

[c_{K}]=0\ \Longrightarrow\ \inf_{\Pi}S^{(N)}[\Pi]\ =\ \inf_{\Pi_{t}}S_{t}^{(N)}[\Pi_{t}]\ +\ \inf_{\Pi_{s}}S_{s}^{(N)}[\Pi_{s}]\ +\ O(h^{\alpha+1}),

(110)

and the infima on the right are attained by the Chebyshev/Zolotarev one–axis extremals on
\Sigma
(Theorem
5
). If
[c_{K}]\neq 0
, then by (
108
)

\inf_{\Pi}S^{(N)}[\Pi]\ \geq\ c_{-}\,\|[c_{K}]\|_{H^{2}}^{2}\ >\ 0,

(111)

i.e. there is an unavoidable positive mixed–curvature penalty that cannot be removed by any policy within the given semi–algebraic class. This is the discrete, operator–theoretic analogue of Vershik’s obstructions to global extremality via semi–algebraic certificates (extremum unattainable unless a global section exists).

Proof.

If
[c_{K}]=0
, by Corollary
5
we can choose a calibrated gauge with
c_{K}=O(h^{\alpha+1})
and hence
S^{(N)}[\Pi]=S_{t}^{(N)}[\Pi]+S_{s}^{(N)}[\Pi]+O(h^{\alpha+1})
(Theorem
6
); optimizing over
\Pi_{t},\Pi_{s}
yields (
110
). If
[c_{K}]\neq 0
, then (
108
) gives (
111
). Both statements are independent of the partition by additivity.
∎

GL
–invariant volume terms.

For metric–type preconditioners
G\succ 0
and gauge
T\in GL
, one has

\log\det(T^{\top}GT)\ =\ \log\det G\ +\ 2\log|\det T|,

(112)

i.e.
\log\det
is a
GL
–character. Hence the nonasymptotic volume functional of transforms functorially under admissible gauges; the mixed penalty is entirely due to
[c_{K}]
, and vanishes exactly when
[c_{K}]=0
. This places our
\log\det
–based bounds within the same invariant framework emphasized by Vershik (algebraic invariants and extremality under group actions).

The trio (
107
)–(
109
)–(
111
) provides the exact discrete counterpart of Vershik’s “certificate/section
\Longleftrightarrow
attainability; obstruction
\Longleftrightarrow
positive gap” principle, now proved in our jet–algebra setting by explicit cochain minimization on the
2
–complex
K
rather than by continuous differential calculus.

2.2.3
Adaptive Geometry

Define the parallel composition on invertible elements of
\mathfrak{U}^{\mathrm{fil}}
by
[
5
,
72
]

A\square B:=(A^{-1}+B^{-1})^{-1}.

(113)

Then
(\mathfrak{U}^{\mathrm{fil}},\square)
is a commutative monoid
[
5
,
72
,
14
]
and

(I+hA)^{-1}(I+hB)^{-1}=(I+h(A\square B)+O(h^{2}))^{-1}.

(114)

For a local step
k
let
H_{k}:=\Omega_{t,1}^{(k)}
and
E_{k}:=\Psi_{s,1}^{(k)}
. The internal preconditioner is
[
12
,
20
,
34
]

G_{k}:=H_{k}\square E_{k}=(H_{k}^{-1}+E_{k}^{-1})^{-1}.

(115)

The BCH expansion yields, for each face
\square
adjacent to step
k
,

\log\mathrm{Hol}(\square)=\tfrac{1}{2}\big([\Delta\Psi^{(k)},H_{k}]+[\Psi^{(k)},\Delta\Omega^{(k)}]\big)+O(h^{\alpha+1}),

(116)

hence

S^{(N)}=\sum_{\square}\|\log\mathrm{Hol}(\square)\|_{F}^{2}=C\sum_{\square}\|E_{k}^{-1}[E_{k},H_{k}]E_{k}^{-1}\|_{F}^{2}+O(h^{2(\alpha+1)}),

(117)

so
\mathsf{F}=0
iff
[E_{k},H_{k}]=0
, equivalently
G_{k}
is invariant along both hierarchies
[
103
,
76
,
43
,
84
]
.

Theorem 9

(Local variational principle from
S^{(N)}
)
.

Fix
k
. Consider admissible perturbations
(\delta H,\delta E)
supported on faces adjacent to
k
. Then

\mathrm{D}S^{(N)}\cdot(\delta H,\delta E)=2\sum_{\square\ni k}\left\langle\log\mathrm{Hol}(\square),\,\mathrm{D}\log\mathrm{Hol}(\square)\cdot(\delta H,\delta E)\right\rangle_{F}.

(118)

Consequently
(H_{k},E_{k})
is stationary for
S^{(N)}
iff

\sum_{\square\ni k}\operatorname{Proj}_{\square}\big(\log\mathrm{Hol}(\square)\big)=0,

(119)

where
\operatorname{Proj}_{\square}
is the
L^{2}
–adjoint of
(\delta H,\delta E)\mapsto\mathrm{D}\log\mathrm{Hol}(\square)\cdot(\delta H,\delta E)
. If
\log\mathrm{Hol}(\square)=O(h^{\alpha+1})
for all adjacent faces, then (
119
) holds and
S^{(N)}
is locally minimal up to
O(h^{2(\alpha+1)})
.

Proof.

S^{(N)}=\sum_{\square}\|\log\mathrm{Hol}(\square)\|_{F}^{2}
is a sum of smooth (jet–truncated) face functionals. Differentiation gives (
118
). Stationarity is the normal equation (
119
). If each face term is
O(h^{\alpha+1})
, then the derivative is
O(h^{2(\alpha+1)})
for all admissible directions, hence local minimality in jet class.
∎

We now construct a consistent adaptive rule that follows directly from Theorem
9
and preserves the algebra (
113
)–(
115
). Let
\widehat{H}_{k+1}
and
\widehat{E}_{k+1}
be any data–driven targets computed from information available up to step
k
(e.g. empirical curvature and noise proxies). Define

\mathcal{Q}_{k}(H,E):=\sum_{\square\ni k}\big\langle E_{k}^{-1}[E_{k},H_{k}]E_{k}^{-1},\,E^{-1}[E,H]E^{-1}\big\rangle_{F},

(120)

the quadratic majorant of the facewise contribution in (
117
) at
(H_{k},E_{k})
. The minimal–change update is the parallel projection

H_{k+1}:=\big((1-\eta_{k})H_{k}^{-1}+\eta_{k}\,\widehat{H}_{k+1}^{-1}\big)^{-1},\qquad E_{k+1}:=\big((1-\zeta_{k})E_{k}^{-1}+\zeta_{k}\,\widehat{E}_{k+1}^{-1}\big)^{-1},

(121)

with step–sizes
\eta_{k},\zeta_{k}\in[0,1]
chosen so that

\mathcal{Q}_{k}(H_{k+1},E_{k+1})\ \leq\ \mathcal{Q}_{k}(H_{k},E_{k})

(122)

and

\sum_{\square\ni k}\big\langle\log\mathrm{Hol}(\square),\,\mathrm{D}\log\mathrm{Hol}(\square)\cdot(H_{k+1}-H_{k},E_{k+1}-E_{k})\big\rangle_{F}\leq 0.

(123)

The inequalities (
122
) and (
123
) are satisfied for all sufficiently small
\eta_{k},\zeta_{k}
by continuity; if
\widehat{H}_{k+1}
and
\widehat{E}_{k+1}
commute with
H_{k}
and
E_{k}
respectively, then (
121
) enforces
[E_{k+1},H_{k}]=0
at first order and thus solves the normal equation (
119
) to jet accuracy.

Corollary 6

(Two–channel curvature–aware recursion)
.

The recursion (
121
) preserves the internal preconditioner:

G_{k+1}=H_{k+1}\square E_{k+1}=\Big(G_{k}^{-1}+\eta_{k}(H_{k}^{-1}-\widehat{H}_{k+1}^{-1})+\zeta_{k}(E_{k}^{-1}-\widehat{E}_{k+1}^{-1})\Big)^{-1},

(124)

and yields a descent for the quadratic majorant
\mathcal{Q}_{k}
; if the targets commute with current operators, the first–order stationarity (
119
) holds and the facewise energy decreases by
O(\eta_{k}+\zeta_{k})
.

Proof.

Apply (
113
) to (
121
) and expand
G_{k+1}^{-1}
linearly in
\eta_{k},\zeta_{k}
, then use (
117
) and the definition (
120
).
∎

Monotone sector (convex potentials) is a special case
[
102
,
88
,
86
,
12
,
20
]
. If
H_{k}=\partial f_{k}
and
E_{k}=\partial e_{k}
for proper, closed, convex
f_{k},e_{k}
, then the identities of convex analysis (Rockafellar) give

\partial(f_{k}\Box e_{k})=\partial f_{k}\square\partial e_{k},\qquad(f_{k}\Box e_{k})^{\!*}=f_{k}^{\!*}+e_{k}^{\!*},\qquad(\partial f_{k})^{-1}=\partial(f_{k}^{\!*}),

(125)

so
G_{k}=\partial(f_{k}\Box e_{k})
and the parallel projection (
121
) corresponds to sequential infimal convolutions of the channel potentials and additive updates of conjugates. Under flatness, the potential level is additive by (
125
); when flatness fails, the mixed term in (
117
) quantifies the obstruction.

3
Isomonodromic theory

3.1
Methods

3.1.1
Integrable Structure

Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be a smooth domain endowed with the Lebesgue measure.
A potential
S:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}
defines the elementary transformation

T(x)=x-\nabla S(x),

called the AT–plane map
[
87
,
3
,
19
,
82
]
. The function
S
is assumed twice continuously differentiable and strictly convex, so that
\nabla^{2}S(x)
is symmetric positive definite almost everywhere.
The Jacobian of
T
is
\nabla T(x)=I-\nabla^{2}S(x)
, and its determinant describes the infinitesimal volume change along the flow
[
24
,
52
,
116
]
.

A density
\rho_{\star}:\Omega\to(0,\infty)
is invariant for
T
if

\rho_{\star}(T(x))\,\det\nabla T(x)=\rho_{\star}(x)\qquad\text{for a.e. }x\in\Omega.

We parametrize
\rho_{\star}
by a smooth positive weight
w(x)
and a constant
c\in\mathbb{R}
as

\rho_{\star}(x)=w(x)\,e^{-cS(x)},

which ensures compatibility with the canonical normalization adopted previously.
Under this parametrization, the invariance condition relates the Jacobian of
T
and the curvature of
S
; its logarithmic form will later yield the Monge–Ampère equation.

For each admissible potential
S
we associate a self–adjoint elliptic operator
\Delta
on
\Omega
with boundary conditions compatible with the variational structure (Dirichlet, Neumann, or mixed)
[
98
,
107
,
49
,
15
,
46
]
.
The zeta–regularized determinant of
\Delta
is
[
107
,
37
,
49
,
98
]

\log\det{}^{\prime}\Delta=-\Bigl.\frac{d}{ds}\zeta_{\Delta}(s)\Bigr|_{s=0},\qquad\zeta_{\Delta}(s)=\sum_{\lambda_{i}>0}\lambda_{i}^{-s},

where zero eigenvalues are omitted.
Dependence on the boundary data and on the metric structure is summarized by a single smooth correction functional
\mathcal{Q}_{\mathrm{CS}}
, collecting the Quillen and Chern–Simons terms
[
28
,
122
,
47
]
.
The resulting scalar functional
[
61
,
60
,
32
,
33
,
45
]

\log\tau=\tfrac{1}{2}\log\det{}^{\prime}\Delta+\mathcal{Q}_{\mathrm{CS}}

is the isomonodromic potential. It plays the role of a canonical energy, invariant under metric rescalings and gauge transformations up to additive constants.

The canonical energy
\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{can}}(S)
, introduced in the previous section, is variationally consistent with
\log\tau

[
49
,
68
,
15
,
79
,
13
,
66
,
10
]
.
For infinitesimal perturbations
S\mapsto S+\varepsilon\sigma
, the variation of
\log\det{}^{\prime}\Delta
satisfies
[
37
,
107
,
15
]

\delta\log\det{}^{\prime}\Delta=\operatorname{Tr}\!\left(\Delta^{-1}\delta\Delta\right),

so that

\delta\log\tau=\tfrac{1}{2}\,\operatorname{Tr}\!\left(\Delta^{-1}\delta\Delta\right)+\delta\mathcal{Q}_{\mathrm{CS}},\qquad\delta\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{can}}(S)=\langle\mathcal{G}[S],\,\sigma\rangle,

where
\mathcal{G}[S]
is the geometric gradient depending on the induced metric and curvature.
The equality of these two variations will be established later and constitutes the analytic bridge between the Monge–Ampère geometry and the isomonodromic energy functional
[
52
,
79
,
13
,
45
]
.

We recall that the AT–plane map

T(x)=x-\nabla S(x),\qquad S\in C^{2}(\Omega),

acts on a probability density
\rho
by the pushforward

(T_{\#}\rho)(y)\,dy=\rho(x)\,dx,\qquad y=T(x),

so that
T_{\#}\rho(y)=\rho(T^{-1}(y))\det\nabla T^{-1}(y)
.
For a fixed smooth strictly convex potential
S
the Jacobian satisfies

\nabla T(x)=I-\nabla^{2}S(x),\qquad\det\nabla T(x)=\det(I-\nabla^{2}S(x)).

An invariant density
\rho_{\star}
for
T
is one satisfying

T_{\#}\rho_{\star}=\rho_{\star}\qquad\Longleftrightarrow\qquad\rho_{\star}(T(x))\,\det\nabla T(x)=\rho_{\star}(x)\quad\text{for almost all }x\in\Omega.

(126)

This equality expresses conservation of mass under the transformation
T
; it is the discrete analogue of the Liouville condition in the automonodromic theory.

We introduce the structural ansatz

\rho_{\star}(x)=w(x)e^{-cS(x)},

(127)

where
w\in C^{1}(\Omega)
is strictly positive and
c\in\mathbb{R}
is constant.
This form mirrors the stationary measure derived from the canonical energy balance.
The condition (
126
) then becomes a functional equation coupling
S
and
w
through the Jacobian determinant of
T
.
We now establish that, under regularity and invertibility assumptions, this functional equation is equivalent to the Monge–Ampère relation.

Theorem 10

(Equivalence of AT–plane invariance and Monge–Ampère structure)
.

Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be open and bounded, and let
S\in C^{2}(\Omega)
be strictly convex so that
T(x)=x-\nabla S(x)
is a diffeomorphism of
\Omega
onto its image.
Let
w\in C^{1}(\Omega)
be strictly positive and
c\in\mathbb{R}
fixed.
Define
\rho_{\star}
by (
127
).
Then the following statements are equivalent:

(A)

\rho_{\star}
is invariant under
T
in the sense of (
126
).

(B)

S
satisfies the Monge–Ampère equation

\det\nabla^{2}S(x)=w(x)e^{-cS(x)}\qquad\text{for a.e. }x\in\Omega.

(128)

If, in addition,
\int_{\Omega}w(x)e^{-cS(x)}dx=1
, then
S
is unique up to an additive constant.

Proof.

(A)
\Rightarrow
(B).

Assume the invariance relation (
126
).
Since
T
is a diffeomorphism, the change–of–variables formula for any integrable
\phi
gives

\int_{\Omega}\phi(y)\rho_{\star}(y)\,dy=\int_{\Omega}\phi(T(x))\,\rho_{\star}(x)\,dx.

Differentiating this identity with respect to variations of
\phi
supported in an infinitesimal neighborhood implies equality of the densities in the sense of Radon–Nikodym derivatives:

\rho_{\star}(y)=\rho_{\star}(T^{-1}(y))\det\nabla T^{-1}(y)\qquad\text{for a.e. }y\in T(\Omega).

Replacing
y
by
T(x)
and applying the inverse Jacobian identity
\det\nabla T^{-1}(T(x))=(\det\nabla T(x))^{-1}
yields

\rho_{\star}(T(x))\,\det\nabla T(x)=\rho_{\star}(x).

(129)

Substituting
\rho_{\star}=we^{-cS}
and
\nabla T(x)=I-\nabla^{2}S(x)
gives

w(T(x))\,e^{-cS(T(x))}\,\det(I-\nabla^{2}S(x))=w(x)\,e^{-cS(x)}.

(130)

To extract a pointwise condition at each
x
, we evaluate (
130
) along the image
y=T(x)
and use the smooth invertibility of
T
.
Define
\Phi(x):=\det\nabla^{2}S(x)-w(x)e^{-cS(x)}
.
We claim that
\Phi(x)\equiv 0
on
\Omega
.

Indeed, differentiate both sides of (
130
) with respect to
x_{i}
using the chain rule.
Writing
A(x):=\nabla^{2}S(x)
and noting that
\partial_{x_{i}}\det(I-A)=-\,\det(I-A)\,\operatorname{Tr}\!\big((I-A)^{-1}\partial_{x_{i}}A\big)
, we obtain

\nabla_{x}\!\big(\log w(T(x))-cS(T(x))+\log\det(I-A(x))\big)=\nabla_{x}(\log w(x)-cS(x)).

Since
T
is invertible and
\nabla T=I-A
, we can replace
\nabla_{x}
acting on
w(T(x))
by
(I-A(x))^{\top}\nabla w(T(x))
.
After expanding both sides and rearranging, one obtains the first–order system

\nabla\log\det(I-A(x))=(I-A(x))^{\top}\nabla(\log w(x)-cS(x))-\nabla(\log w(T(x))-cS(T(x))).

Under the assumption that all functions are
C^{1}
and
T
is a
C^{1}
diffeomorphism, the right–hand side vanishes if and only if
\det A(x)=w(x)e^{-cS(x)}
.
Hence (
128
) holds.

(B)
\Rightarrow
(A).

Assume (
128
).
Let
y=T(x)=x-\nabla S(x)
and compute
\det\nabla T(x)=\det(I-\nabla^{2}S(x))
.
Since
\det\nabla^{2}S(x)=w(x)e^{-cS(x)}
, we have

\det\nabla T(x)=\frac{\det(I-\nabla^{2}S(x))}{\det\nabla^{2}S(x)}\,w(x)e^{-cS(x)}.

Using the determinant identity
\det(I-A)=\exp(\operatorname{Tr}\log(I-A))
and the convexity of
S
, the matrix
(I-\nabla^{2}S(x))
is invertible and its determinant positive.
Define
y=T(x)
and invert the map:
x=T^{-1}(y)=y+\nabla S(x)
.
By the change–of–variables formula,

\rho_{\star}(y)=\rho_{\star}(x)\det\nabla T(x)^{-1}=w(x)e^{-cS(x)}(\det(I-\nabla^{2}S(x)))^{-1}.

Replacing
x
by
T^{-1}(y)
and using
\det\nabla^{2}S(T^{-1}(y))=w(T^{-1}(y))e^{-cS(T^{-1}(y))}
gives

\rho_{\star}(y)=w(T^{-1}(y))e^{-cS(T^{-1}(y))}\det\nabla T^{-1}(y),

which is precisely the invariance relation (
126
).

Finally, if
\int_{\Omega}we^{-cS}=1
and
S
and
S+\mathrm{const}
both satisfy (
128
), then
we^{-c(S+\mathrm{const})}=e^{-c\,\mathrm{const}}we^{-cS}
, so normalization fixes the additive constant, proving uniqueness.
∎

Corollary 7

(Monge–Ampère residual and convergence test)
.

For any
S\in C^{2}(\Omega)
define the residual

\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{MA}}(x):=\log\det\nabla^{2}S(x)-\log w(x)+cS(x).

Then
\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{MA}}\equiv 0
if and only if the transformation
T(x)=x-\nabla S(x)
preserves the density
\rho_{\star}=we^{-cS}
.
In numerical schemes where
S
is updated iteratively, the norm

\|\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{MA}}\|_{L^{2}(\rho_{\star})}:=\Bigl(\int_{\Omega}|\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{MA}}(x)|^{2}\rho_{\star}(x)\,dx\Bigr)^{1/2}

serves as a computable criterion of invariance; convergence is declared when
\|\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{MA}}\|_{L^{2}(\rho_{\star})}\leq\varepsilon_{\mathrm{MA}}
for a prescribed tolerance.

Example 8

(Ellipsoidal trust–region calibration)
.

Consider a local quadratic model of the potential

S(x)=\tfrac{1}{2}(x-x_{k})^{\top}H_{k}(x-x_{k}),\qquad H_{k}\succ 0.

Then
\nabla^{2}S(x)\equiv H_{k}
and the Monge–Ampère condition (
128
) becomes

\det H_{k}=w(x_{k})e^{-cS(x_{k})}.

For given
w(x_{k})
this relation calibrates the determinant of
H_{k}
, i.e. the volume of the ellipsoidal level set
\{x:\,S(x)\leq 1\}
, so that it matches the invariant measure.
A practical update preserving positive definiteness is

H_{k+1}:=\exp\!\Big(\log H_{k}+\eta_{k}(\log w(x_{k})-\log\det H_{k}+cS(x_{k}))\,I\Big),

(131)

where
\eta_{k}\in(0,1)
, and the matrix exponential acts diagonally on the eigenvalues of
H_{k}
.
In implementation,
\log\det H_{k}
is evaluated via the Cholesky factor
H_{k}=R_{k}^{\top}R_{k}
as

\log\det H_{k}=2\sum_{i}\log(R_{k})_{ii}
.
This multiplicative update enforces
\det H_{k+1}\to w(x_{k})e^{-cS(x_{k})}
exponentially fast in
\eta_{k}
while maintaining symmetry and positive definiteness.
In stochastic or mini–batch settings one replaces
w(x_{k})
by its empirical mean
\overline{w}_{k}
and uses the same rule (
131
).
Convergence of the sequence
\{H_{k}\}
is monitored by
\|\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{MA}}\|_{L^{2}(\rho_{\star})}
, and when it falls below
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{MA}}
, the step
x_{k+1}=x_{k}-\nabla S(x_{k})
is accepted.
Thus the Monge–Ampère condition provides a rigorous geometric calibration of trust–region volume consistent with the invariant density
\rho_{\star}
and with the formalism of Theorem
10
.

Theorem 11

(Determinant line and equality of energy with
\log\tau
)
.

Let
\Omega
,
S
,
w
,
c
,
\rho_{\star}
,
\Delta=\Delta(S)
,
\Pi
(the orthogonal projector onto
\ker\Delta
), and
Q_{\mathrm{CS}}(S)
be as above.
Assume the admissibility hypotheses:
\Delta(S)
is a nonnegative self–adjoint elliptic operator on a fixed Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
with compact resolvent; its kernel is finite–dimensional and independent of
S
within the admissible class (fixed boundary data); the heat kernel satisfies the standard Gaussian bounds and admits the small–time asymptotic expansion required for zeta–regularization; the map
S\mapsto\Delta(S)
is
C^{1}
in the strong resolvent sense along admissible
C^{1}
–paths with fixed boundary values; and
Q_{\mathrm{CS}}
is a local counterterm whose first variation
\delta Q_{\mathrm{CS}}(S)
is well–defined and continuous with respect to
\delta S
.
Define

\displaystyle\log\tau(S)\ :=\ \tfrac{1}{2}\,\log\det{}^{\prime}\Delta(S)\ +\ Q_{\mathrm{CS}}(S),

\displaystyle\log\det{}^{\prime}\Delta:=-\zeta^{\prime}_{\Delta}(0),\quad\zeta_{\Delta}(s):=\operatorname{Tr}\!\big(\Delta^{-s}(I-\Pi)\big).

(132)

Then the following statements hold.

(i) First variation. For every admissible variation
\delta S
with induced
\delta\Delta
,

\delta\log\tau(S)\ =\ \tfrac{1}{2}\,\operatorname{Tr}\!\big(\Delta^{-1}\delta\Delta\,(I-\Pi)\big)\ +\ \delta Q_{\mathrm{CS}}(S).

(133)

(ii) Equality with the canonical energy. There exists a constant
C\in\mathbb{R}
, independent of
S
, such that

E_{\mathrm{can}}(S)\ =\ \log\tau(S)\ +\ C.

(134)

Proof.

The proof is organized into three lemmas followed by the conclusion.

Lemma 1 (Heat representation and analytic continuation).
For
\Re s
sufficiently large one has

\zeta_{\Delta}(s)\ =\ \frac{1}{\Gamma(s)}\int_{0}^{\infty}t^{\,s-1}\,\operatorname{Tr}\!\big(e^{-t\Delta}(I-\Pi)\big)\,dt,

(135)

and
\zeta_{\Delta}
extends meromorphically to
\mathbb{C}
with no pole at
s=0
.
Proof of Lemma 1.
Since
\Delta\geq 0
is self–adjoint with compact resolvent, the spectrum consists of a sequence
0=\lambda_{1}=\cdots=\lambda_{r}<\lambda_{r+1}\leq\lambda_{r+2}\leq\cdots\to\infty
, repeated by multiplicity, and
e^{-t\Delta}
is trace–class for every
t>0
. The spectral theorem yields

\operatorname{Tr}(e^{-t\Delta}(I-\Pi))=\sum_{j>r}e^{-t\lambda_{j}}
and
\operatorname{Tr}(\Delta^{-s}(I-\Pi))=\sum_{j>r}\lambda_{j}^{-s}
for
\Re s
large. The Laplace transform identity

\lambda^{-s}=\Gamma(s)^{-1}\int_{0}^{\infty}t^{s-1}e^{-t\lambda}\,dt
for
\Re s>0
and
\lambda>0
gives (
135
) by Fubini–Tonelli.
Small–time heat kernel asymptotics on a bounded domain with admissible boundary conditions yield an expansion
\operatorname{Tr}(e^{-t\Delta}(I-\Pi))\sim\sum_{k=0}^{K}a_{k}t^{(k-d)/2}
as
t\downarrow 0
plus an exponentially small remainder and, as
t\to\infty
, exponential decay
O(e^{-t\lambda_{r+1}})
. These standard properties imply meromorphic continuation of
\zeta_{\Delta}
with at most simple poles at half–integers up to
d/2
, none at
s=0
; hence
\zeta^{\prime}_{\Delta}(0)
is finite. ∎

Lemma 2 (Differentiation under the integral and Duhamel formula).
Let
\Delta_{\varepsilon}=\Delta(S+\varepsilon\delta S)
for
|\varepsilon|<\varepsilon_{0}
, where
\varepsilon_{0}>0
is small so that admissibility persists. Then for each fixed
t>0
,

\frac{d}{d\varepsilon}\,e^{-t\Delta_{\varepsilon}}\Big|_{\varepsilon=0}\ =\ -\int_{0}^{t}e^{-(t-u)\Delta}\,(\delta\Delta)\,e^{-u\Delta}\,du,

(136)

and the map
\varepsilon\mapsto\operatorname{Tr}\!\big(e^{-t\Delta_{\varepsilon}}(I-\Pi)\big)
is differentiable with

\frac{d}{d\varepsilon}\,\operatorname{Tr}\!\big(e^{-t\Delta_{\varepsilon}}(I-\Pi)\big)\Big|_{\varepsilon=0}\ =\ -t\,\operatorname{Tr}\!\big((\delta\Delta)\,e^{-t\Delta}(I-\Pi)\big).

(137)

Proof of Lemma 2.
The strong
C^{1}
dependence of
\Delta_{\varepsilon}
and the Trotter–Kato theory give (
136
) (Bochner integral in the strong operator topology).
For trace class justification, note that
e^{-(t-u)\Delta}
and
e^{-u\Delta}
are Hilbert–Schmidt for
u\in(0,t)
, and
\delta\Delta
is bounded from the graph norm of
\Delta
to
\mathcal{H}
along admissible directions (elliptic perturbation of coefficients induced by
\delta S
); therefore the integrand is trace–class and the Bochner integral defines a trace–class operator.
Taking the trace in (
136
), using cyclicity of the trace, and integrating in
u
yields (
137
). ∎

Lemma 3 (First variation of the zeta determinant).
With
\zeta_{\Delta}
as in Lemma
135
, one has

\delta\log\det{}^{\prime}\Delta\ =\ \operatorname{Tr}\!\big(\Delta^{-1}\delta\Delta\,(I-\Pi)\big).

(138)

Proof of Lemma 3.
For
\Re s
large, combine (
135
) with Lemma 2:

\delta\zeta_{\Delta}(s)=\frac{1}{\Gamma(s)}\int_{0}^{\infty}t^{\,s-1}\,\delta\operatorname{Tr}\!\big(e^{-t\Delta}(I-\Pi)\big)\,dt=-\frac{1}{\Gamma(s)}\int_{0}^{\infty}t^{\,s}\,\operatorname{Tr}\!\big((\delta\Delta)e^{-t\Delta}(I-\Pi)\big)\,dt.

By spectral calculus and
\int_{0}^{\infty}t^{\,s}e^{-t\lambda}\,dt=\Gamma(s+1)\lambda^{-(s+1)}
for
\lambda>0
,

\delta\zeta_{\Delta}(s)=-s\,\operatorname{Tr}\!\big((\delta\Delta)\,\Delta^{-(s+1)}(I-\Pi)\big).

The right–hand side is meromorphic in
s
and regular at
s=0
(resolvent trace on
P\mathcal{H}
,
P=I-\Pi
). Differentiating at
s=0
gives

\delta\zeta^{\prime}_{\Delta}(0)=-\operatorname{Tr}\!\big((\delta\Delta)\Delta^{-1}P\big)
.
Since
\log\det{}^{\prime}\Delta=-\zeta^{\prime}_{\Delta}(0)
, the claim (
138
) follows. ∎

We now conclude the proof of the theorem.
By (
11
) and Lemma 3,

\delta\log\tau(S)=\tfrac{1}{2}\,\delta\log\det{}^{\prime}\Delta(S)+\delta Q_{\mathrm{CS}}(S)=\tfrac{1}{2}\,\operatorname{Tr}\!\big(\Delta^{-1}\delta\Delta\,(I-\Pi)\big)+\delta Q_{\mathrm{CS}}(S),

which proves (i).
The first variation of the canonical energy satisfies

\delta E_{\mathrm{can}}(S)=\tfrac{1}{2}\,\operatorname{Tr}\!\big(\Delta^{-1}\delta\Delta\,(I-\Pi)\big)+\delta Q_{\mathrm{CS}}(S),

hence
\delta\big(E_{\mathrm{can}}(S)-\log\tau(S)\big)=0
for all admissible
\delta S
.
The admissible manifold of
S
with fixed boundary data is path–connected by linear interpolation followed by elliptic regularization within the strictly convex class; therefore
E_{\mathrm{can}}(S)-\log\tau(S)=C
for a constant
C
independent of
S
, which is (ii).
∎

Corollary 8

(Stationarity equivalence and Euler–Lagrange identity)
.

For any admissible
S
the following are equivalent:

\displaystyle\delta\log\tau(S)=0\ \ \text{for all admissible }\delta S

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\delta E_{\mathrm{can}}(S)=0\ \ \text{for all admissible }\delta S

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\det\nabla^{2}S=w\,e^{-cS}\ \text{ a.e. on }\Omega.

Proof.
The equivalence of the first two statements follows from Theorem
11
(i).
The last equivalence is the Euler–Lagrange identity for the Monge–Ampère residual, already established there.

Corollary 9

(Normalization by a reference configuration)
.

Fix any admissible reference potential
S_{0}
.
Let
C:=E_{\mathrm{can}}(S_{0})-\log\tau(S_{0})
.
Then for all admissible
S
,

E_{\mathrm{can}}(S)=\log\tau(S)+C.

Choosing the normalization
\log\tau(S_{0})=E_{\mathrm{can}}(S_{0})
removes the constant, so that
E_{\mathrm{can}}\equiv\log\tau
on the admissible class.

Example 9

(Deterministic and stochastic evaluation of
\log\tau
and its first variation)
.

This example provides two rigorously justified computational schemes that implement the identities of Theorem
11
.
Throughout,
P:=I-\Pi
denotes the orthogonal projector onto
(\ker\Delta)^{\perp}
.

(A) First variation via trace estimation.
The identity (
133
) reads
\delta\log\tau(S)=\tfrac{1}{2}\,\operatorname{Tr}(\Delta^{-1}\delta\Delta P)+\delta Q_{\mathrm{CS}}(S)
.
Let
z_{1},\dots,z_{N}
be i.i.d. random vectors supported on
P\mathcal{H}
with
\mathbb{E}[z_{i}z_{i}^{\top}]=P
(e.g. Rademacher or Gaussian probes projected onto
P\mathcal{H}
).
For each
i
, solve
\Delta y_{i}=Pz_{i}
by a preconditioned conjugate gradient method to tolerance
\varepsilon>0
; denote the computed solution by
y_{i}^{\varepsilon}
.
Define
\widehat{t}_{i}:=\langle z_{i},\delta\Delta\,y_{i}^{\varepsilon}\rangle
.
Then the estimator

\widehat{\delta\log\tau}=\frac{1}{2N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\widehat{t}_{i}+\delta Q_{\mathrm{CS}}(S)

satisfies the error decomposition

\displaystyle\big|\widehat{\delta\log\tau}-\delta\log\tau\big|\ \leq\

\displaystyle\underbrace{\frac{1}{2}\Big|\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\langle z_{i},\delta\Delta\,\Delta^{-1}Pz_{i}\rangle-\operatorname{Tr}(\Delta^{-1}\delta\Delta P)\Big|}_{\text{stochastic error}}

\displaystyle+\ \underbrace{\frac{1}{2N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\|\delta\Delta\|\,\|y_{i}^{\varepsilon}-\Delta^{-1}Pz_{i}\|}_{\text{solver error}}.

The stochastic term has variance
\mathcal{O}\big(\|\Delta^{-1}\delta\Delta P\|_{F}^{2}/N\big)
; the solver term is
\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon)
by stability of
\delta\Delta
on
P\mathcal{H}
.

(B)
\tfrac{1}{2}\log\det{}^{\prime}\Delta
via stochastic Lanczos quadrature (SLQ).
On
P\mathcal{H}
one has
\tfrac{1}{2}\log\det{}^{\prime}\Delta=\tfrac{1}{2}\operatorname{Tr}(\log\Delta\cdot P)
.
Let
z_{1},\dots,z_{N}
be as above.
For each
i
, run
m
steps of Lanczos on
(\Delta,Pz_{i})
to obtain the tridiagonal matrix
T_{m}^{(i)}
.
Set
\theta_{i}:=\|Pz_{i}\|^{2}e_{1}^{\top}(\tfrac{1}{2}\log T_{m}^{(i)})e_{1}
.
Then

\widehat{\tfrac{1}{2}\log\det{}^{\prime}\Delta}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\theta_{i}

converges to
\tfrac{1}{2}\operatorname{Tr}(\log\Delta\cdot P)
with bias
\mathcal{O}(m^{-2})
under standard spectral regularity (e.g.
\log
-Hölder continuity of the density of states) and variance
\mathcal{O}(N^{-1})
.
Adding
Q_{\mathrm{CS}}(S)
yields
\log\tau(S)
according to (
11
).
Both (A) and (B) preserve the orthogonality to
\ker\Delta
explicitly via
P
; this enforces the prime on the determinant and is mandatory for correctness.

Corollary 10

(
\tau
–stationarity as a practical stopping rule)
.

Let
S_{k}
be iterates produced by any admissible descent scheme for the Monge–Ampère residual with fixed boundary values.
If an estimator
\widehat{\delta\log\tau}(S_{k})
constructed by (A) satisfies
|\widehat{\delta\log\tau}(S_{k})|\leq\eta_{k}
with
\eta_{k}\to 0
while solver tolerances
\varepsilon_{k}\to 0
and probe counts
N_{k}\to\infty
so that
\eta_{k}
dominates both stochastic and solver errors, then
\delta\log\tau(S_{k})\to 0
and, by Corollary
8
, any accumulation point of
\{S_{k}\}
solves the Monge–Ampère equation.

Example 10

(Monotone ascent for
\log\tau
with elliptic regularization)
.

Let
R(S):=\det\nabla^{2}S-we^{-cS}
be the Monge–Ampère residual, and let
\mathcal{A}
be a fixed symmetric strictly elliptic isomorphism on
H_{0}^{2}(\Omega)
(for instance
(I-\alpha\Delta_{x})^{m}
with homogeneous Dirichlet data,
\alpha>0
,
m\in\mathbb{N}
).
Define the direction
\delta S:=\mathcal{A}^{-1}R(S)
by solving
\mathcal{A}(\delta S)=R(S)
in
H_{0}^{2}(\Omega)
.
Consider the update
S^{+}=S-\eta\,\delta S
with
\eta>0
chosen by backtracking.
Then, using Theorem
11
(i) together with the identity that the first variation of
\log\tau
equals minus the
L^{2}
–pairing with the residual, one has

\displaystyle\frac{d}{d\varepsilon}\,\log\tau\big(S-\varepsilon\delta S\big)\Big|_{\varepsilon=0}

\displaystyle=\ -\,\langle R(S),\delta S\rangle_{L^{2}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle=\ -\,\langle R(S),\mathcal{A}^{-1}R(S)\rangle=\ -\,\|\mathcal{A}^{-1/2}R(S)\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\ <\ 0.

Hence for all sufficiently small
\eta
,

\log\tau(S^{+})\ \geq\ \log\tau(S)\ +\ \sigma\,\eta\,\|\mathcal{A}^{-1/2}R(S)\|_{L^{2}}^{2}

for some universal
\sigma\in(0,1)
determined by a second–order Taylor bound based on the local Lipschitz continuity of
\delta\log\tau
along admissible rays.
Each iteration requires one elliptic solve with
\mathcal{A}
, one assembly of
R(S)
, and (optionally) one evaluation of
\log\tau
via Example
9
(B).
Any limit point satisfying
\|\mathcal{A}^{-1/2}R(S)\|_{L^{2}}\to 0
is a
\tau
–stationary point and, by Corollary
8
, solves the Monge–Ampère equation.

Theorem 12

(Minimax
\Longleftrightarrow

\tau
–stationarity
\Longleftrightarrow
MA–invariance)
.

Work under the standing hypotheses of §7.1–§7.2 (domain
\Omega
, regularity and convexity of
S
, fixed boundary values, positive weight
w
, fixed
c\in\mathbb{R}
, admissible variations vanishing on
\partial\Omega
). Let
E_{\mathrm{can}}
and
\tau
be as in §7.2, with

E_{\mathrm{can}}(S)\;=\;\log\tau(S)\;+\;C,

where
C
does not depend on
S
. Let
T(x)=x-\nabla S(x)
and
\rho_{\star}=w\,e^{-cS}
.
Then the following are equivalent:

(i)

S
is a minimax point of
E_{\mathrm{can}}
in the sense of §6 (saddle curvature along the admissible class).

(ii)

\delta\log\tau(S)=0
for all admissible first–order variations.

(iii)

T
preserves
\rho_{\star}
and
S
satisfies the Monge–Ampère equation
\det\nabla^{2}S=w\,e^{-cS}
a.e. on
\Omega
.

Proof.

All variational statements below are taken with respect to admissible perturbations
\delta S\in C^{2}(\Omega)
vanishing on
\partial\Omega
; by the standing regularity, differentiations under the integral sign are justified by dominated convergence on compact parameter domains.

Step 1: (i)
\Rightarrow
(ii).
By definition in §6, a minimax point of a
C^{2}
–functional
F
is stationary:
\delta F(S)=0
for all admissible
\delta S
. Apply this with
F=E_{\mathrm{can}}
to get
\delta E_{\mathrm{can}}(S)=0
. Using the identity
E_{\mathrm{can}}=\log\tau+C
from §7.2, one has
\delta\log\tau(S)=\delta E_{\mathrm{can}}(S)=0
. Hence (ii).

Step 2: (ii)
\Rightarrow
(iii).
From §7.2 we have the exact Euler–Lagrange identity

\delta\log\tau(S)\;=\;-\int_{\Omega}\Big(\det\nabla^{2}S-w\,e^{-cS}\Big)\,\delta S\,dx.

(139)

If
\delta\log\tau(S)=0
for all admissible
\delta S
, then by the fundamental lemma of the calculus of variations the integrand vanishes a.e., that is

\det\nabla^{2}S\;=\;w\,e^{-cS}\quad\text{a.e. on }\Omega.

By Theorem 7.1, this equality is equivalent to the invariance of
\rho_{\star}
under
T(x)=x-\nabla S(x)
. Hence (iii).

Step 3: (iii)
\Rightarrow
(ii).
If
\det\nabla^{2}S=w\,e^{-cS}
a.e., then the right–hand side of (
139
) is zero for all admissible
\delta S
, i.e.
\delta\log\tau(S)=0
. Hence (ii).

Step 4: (ii)
\Rightarrow
(i).
By §6 (stationary
\Rightarrow
minimax under the saddle–curvature hypotheses stated there) it suffices to identify the second variation of
E_{\mathrm{can}}
at a stationary
S
and to check the saddle structure. Using
E_{\mathrm{can}}=\log\tau+C
, we compute
\delta^{2}E_{\mathrm{can}}(S)=\delta^{2}\log\tau(S)
. Differentiating (
139
) at a stationary
S
in two admissible directions
U,V
gives the symmetric bilinear form

\delta^{2}\log\tau(S)[U,V]\;=\;-\int_{\Omega}\Big(\det\nabla^{2}S\ \operatorname{Tr}\big((\nabla^{2}S)^{-1}\nabla^{2}U\big)\;+\;c\,w\,e^{-cS}\,U\Big)\,V\,dx,

(140)

where we used Jacobi’s formula for the derivative of the determinant and the fact that
w
is fixed. At a stationary point we have
\det\nabla^{2}S=w\,e^{-cS}
a.e., hence (
140
) becomes

\delta^{2}\log\tau(S)[U,V]\;=\;-\int_{\Omega}\Big(w\,e^{-cS}\ \operatorname{Tr}\big((\nabla^{2}S)^{-1}\nabla^{2}U\big)\;+\;c\,w\,e^{-cS}\,U\Big)\,V\,dx.

(141)

Define
H:=\nabla^{2}S
and
A:=\det(H)\,H^{-1}
(the cofactor of
H
). Since
S
is strictly convex,
H
and
A
are a.e. positive definite. For
U=V
and using the identity
\det(H)\ \operatorname{Tr}(H^{-1}\nabla^{2}U)=\langle A,\nabla^{2}U\rangle
, (
141
) reads

\delta^{2}\log\tau(S)[U,U]\;=\;-\int_{\Omega}\langle A,\nabla^{2}U\rangle\,dx\;-\;c\int_{\Omega}w\,e^{-cS}\,U^{2}\,dx.

(142)

By the divergence identity for cofactors of Hessians established in §7.1 (and used there in the weak formulation),
\partial_{i}A_{ij}=0
in the distributional sense; with
U|_{\partial\Omega}=0
we may integrate by parts twice to obtain

-\int_{\Omega}\langle A,\nabla^{2}U\rangle\,dx\;=\;\int_{\Omega}\langle A\,\nabla U,\nabla U\rangle\,dx,

(143)

and consequently

\delta^{2}\log\tau(S)[U,U]\;=\;\int_{\Omega}\langle A\,\nabla U,\nabla U\rangle\,dx\;-\;c\int_{\Omega}w\,e^{-cS}\,U^{2}\,dx.

(144)

The first term in (
144
) is nonnegative and controls
\|\nabla U\|_{L^{2}}^{2}
from below by the essential infimum of the smallest eigenvalue of
A
; the second term is nonpositive and controls
\|U\|_{L^{2}}^{2}
from above by the essential supremum of
w\,e^{-cS}
. Therefore the quadratic form (
144
) has mixed sign directions: (a) for highly oscillatory
U
with
\|\nabla U\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\gg\|U\|_{L^{2}}^{2}
, the first term dominates and
\delta^{2}\log\tau(S)[U,U]>0
; (b) for functions
U
with small Dirichlet energy relative to mass (e.g., low modes in
H_{0}^{1}(\Omega)
), the second term dominates and
\delta^{2}\log\tau(S)[U,U]<0
. The existence of both kinds of directions follows rigorously by the standard spectral comparison on
H_{0}^{1}(\Omega)
: fix any strictly positive lower bound
a_{\min}
on the smallest eigenvalue of
A
and any finite upper bound
m_{\max}
on
w\,e^{-cS}
; then for a sequence of eigenfunctions
\{\varphi_{k}\}
of the Dirichlet Laplacian with eigenvalues
\lambda_{k}\to\infty
one has
\int\langle A\nabla\varphi_{k},\nabla\varphi_{k}\rangle\geq a_{\min}\int|\nabla\varphi_{k}|^{2}=a_{\min}\lambda_{k}\int\varphi_{k}^{2}
, so for all sufficiently large
k
,
\delta^{2}\log\tau(S)[\varphi_{k},\varphi_{k}]>0
; on the other hand, by the Poincaré inequality with the first eigenvalue
\lambda_{1}>0
and by choosing
U
as any fixed nonzero function in
H_{0}^{1}(\Omega)
sufficiently close to the first eigenfunction, the ratio
\int\langle A\nabla U,\nabla U\rangle/\int U^{2}
can be made arbitrarily close to
a_{\min}\lambda_{1}
, hence if
c\,m_{\max}>a_{\min}\lambda_{1}
the form is negative on that
U
. This furnishes the saddle curvature required by §6 at the stationary
S
. Therefore, by the minimax characterization stated in §6,
S
is a minimax point of
E_{\mathrm{can}}
, i.e. (i) holds.

Combining Steps 1–4 establishes the equivalence (i)
\Leftrightarrow
(ii)
\Leftrightarrow
(iii).
∎

Corollary 11

(MA–preconditioning of the step)
.

Let
S
satisfy the hypotheses of Theorem
12
and let
R(S):=\det\nabla^{2}S-w\,e^{-cS}
denote the Monge–Ampère residual. Consider one iteration of a descent method for
E_{\mathrm{can}}
of the form
S^{+}=S-\eta\,\delta S
, where
\delta S
is obtained by applying a symmetric positive definite preconditioner
\mathcal{P}^{-1}
to the
L^{2}
–gradient of
E_{\mathrm{can}}
:

\delta S\;=\;\mathcal{P}^{-1}\Big(\frac{\delta E_{\mathrm{can}}}{\delta S}\Big)\;=\;-\mathcal{P}^{-1}R(S).

Among all local, uniformly elliptic, coefficient–frozen preconditioners of the form

\mathcal{P}\;\in\;\Big\{\,-\operatorname{div}(B\,\nabla\cdot)\;+\;\mu\,I\ \Big|\ B(x)\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}\ \text{sym.\ pos.\ def.},\ \mu\geq 0\ \text{constants on each block}\,\Big\},

the choice
B=A=\det(\nabla^{2}S)\,(\nabla^{2}S)^{-1}
(optionally block– or diagonal–restricted) and
\mu=c\,w\,e^{-cS}
(frozen per block) minimizes the local condition number of the Hessian
\delta^{2}E_{\mathrm{can}}(S)
in the sense of the Rayleigh quotient. Precisely, for every admissible
U\not\equiv 0
,

\frac{\big\langle U,\ \delta^{2}E_{\mathrm{can}}(S)\,U\big\rangle}{\big\langle U,\ \mathcal{P}\,U\big\rangle}\;=\;\frac{\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\langle A\,\nabla U,\nabla U\rangle\,dx\;-\;c\int_{\Omega}w\,e^{-cS}\,U^{2}\,dx}{\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\langle B\,\nabla U,\nabla U\rangle\,dx\;+\;\mu\int_{\Omega}U^{2}\,dx},

and the numerator/denominator are simultaneously diagonalized (up to lower–order commutators) when
B=A
and
\mu=c\,w\,e^{-cS}
are locally frozen, which yields the smallest extremal spread of the quotient over
U
within that class.

Proof.

By Theorem
12
,
\delta^{2}E_{\mathrm{can}}(S)=\delta^{2}\log\tau(S)
, and by (
144
) its quadratic form is

\mathcal{H}_{S}[U]\;=\;\int_{\Omega}\langle A\,\nabla U,\nabla U\rangle\,dx\;-\;c\int_{\Omega}w\,e^{-cS}\,U^{2}\,dx.

For a preconditioner
\mathcal{P}=-\operatorname{div}(B\nabla\cdot)+\mu I
with
U|_{\partial\Omega}=0
,

\langle U,\mathcal{P}U\rangle\;=\;\int_{\Omega}\langle B\,\nabla U,\nabla U\rangle\,dx\;+\;\mu\int_{\Omega}U^{2}\,dx.

The generalized Rayleigh quotient is the ratio displayed in the statement. Freezing
A
and
w\,e^{-cS}
locally (blockwise or pointwise) the quotient becomes a ratio of two positive quadratic forms sharing the same principal part if
B=A
and
\mu=c\,w\,e^{-cS}
, which makes the associated generalized eigenvalue problem closest to the identity and minimizes the spectral condition number in the standard sense (Courant–Fischer min–max). Any other admissible
(B,\mu)
deviates from
(A,cw\,e^{-cS})
by a positive–definite perturbation in either the gradient or the mass channel and thus enlarges the spread of extremal values over
U
. This proves the claim.
∎

Example 11

(Stochastic MA–correction with MA–preconditioning)
.

Fix a quadrature rule
\mathcal{Q}
on
\Omega
and a family of minibatches
B\subset\mathcal{Q}
drawn i.i.d. across iterations. Given an iterate
S_{k}
with
S_{k}|_{\partial\Omega}=S_{0}|_{\partial\Omega}
and strictly convex, define the discrete residual on
B
by

R_{k}(x)\;:=\;\det\nabla^{2}S_{k}(x)\;-\;w(x)\,e^{-cS_{k}(x)},\qquad x\in B.

Let
A_{k}(x):=\det(\nabla^{2}S_{k}(x))\,(\nabla^{2}S_{k}(x))^{-1}
and
m_{k}(x):=c\,w(x)\,e^{-cS_{k}(x)}
. Form the preconditioner

\mathcal{P}_{k}\;=\;-\operatorname{div}\!\big(B_{k}\nabla\cdot\big)\;+\;\mu_{k}I,

where
B_{k}
and
\mu_{k}
are blockwise–frozen approximations of
A_{k}
and
m_{k}
assembled on the computational grid (e.g.,
B_{k}
diagonal with entries of
A_{k}
, or block–
2\times 2
along coordinate planes;
\mu_{k}
piecewise–constant on blocks). Compute the search direction
\delta S_{k}
as the solution of the regularized normal equation

\mathcal{P}_{k}\,\delta S_{k}\;=\;-\,\Pi_{B}^{\ast}R_{k}\;-\;\lambda_{\mathrm{tik}}\,\delta S_{k}\;-\;\lambda_{\mathrm{tv}}\,\operatorname{div}\Big(\frac{\nabla\delta S_{k}}{\sqrt{|\nabla\delta S_{k}|^{2}+\epsilon^{2}}}\Big),

with homogeneous Dirichlet boundary conditions. Here
\Pi_{B}^{\ast}
places the sampled residual as a weighted source on the full grid,
\lambda_{\mathrm{tik}}\geq 0
and
\lambda_{\mathrm{tv}}\geq 0
are Tikhonov and total–variation regularization weights, and
\epsilon>0
is a Huber parameter. Solve this elliptic problem by preconditioned CG to a fixed relative tolerance, yielding
\delta S_{k}
.

Update the potential by

S_{k+1}\;=\;S_{k}\;-\;\eta_{k}\,\delta S_{k},

with
\eta_{k}\in(0,\bar{\eta}]
chosen by backtracking on a stochastic Armijo condition evaluated on an independent minibatch
\widetilde{B}
:

\log\tau_{\widetilde{B}}(S_{k+1})\ \geq\ \log\tau_{\widetilde{B}}(S_{k})\ +\ \sigma\,\eta_{k}\,\langle\delta S_{k},\ \mathcal{P}_{k}\,\delta S_{k}\rangle,\quad\sigma\in(0,1).

Finally, perform the map step
x\mapsto x-\nabla S_{k+1}(x)
in the downstream application.

Correctness and monotonicity in expectation.
By Theorem
12
and (
144
),

\frac{d}{d\varepsilon}\log\tau(S_{k}-\varepsilon\,\delta S_{k})\Big|_{\varepsilon=0}\;=\;-\langle R(S_{k}),\,\delta S_{k}\rangle\;=\;\langle\mathcal{P}_{k}\,\delta S_{k},\,\delta S_{k}\rangle\;+\;\mathcal{R}_{k},

where
\mathcal{R}_{k}
collects the (nonnegative) TV/Tikhonov regularization contributions moved to the left–hand side. With unbiased sampling of residuals and an independent Armijo batch, taking conditional expectation given
S_{k}
yields

\mathbb{E}\big[\log\tau(S_{k+1})-\log\tau(S_{k})\,\big|\,S_{k}\big]\ \geq\ \eta_{k}\Big(\sigma\,\mathbb{E}\big[\langle\delta S_{k},\ \mathcal{P}_{k}\,\delta S_{k}\rangle\,\big|\,S_{k}\big]\ -\ \tfrac{L}{2}\,\eta_{k}\,\mathbb{E}\big[\|\delta S_{k}\|_{\mathcal{P}_{k}}^{2}\,\big|\,S_{k}\big]\Big),

for some Lipschitz constant
L
of the preconditioned gradient on the admissible set (finite under the freezing/regularity hypotheses). Choosing
\eta_{k}\leq 2\sigma/L
ensures nonnegative drift. Since
\mathcal{P}_{k}
is spectrally matched to the Hessian by Corollary
11
, the norm
\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{P}_{k}}
controls the curvature of
\log\tau
, which yields a variance–controlled ascent of
\log\tau
in expectation. Under standard Robbins–Monro steps (square–summable, not summable) and uniform convexity of
S_{k}
preserved by the line search, one gets
\|R(S_{k})\|_{H^{-1}}\to 0
, hence
S_{k}\to S_{\star}
solving
\det\nabla^{2}S_{\star}=w\,e^{-cS_{\star}}
; invariance of
\rho_{\star}
then follows from Theorem 7.1.

Computational cost.
Per iteration: (a) assemble
R_{k}
and
A_{k}
on
B
(determinant and inverse of a
d\times d
Hessian per sample); (b) one elliptic solve with
\mathcal{P}_{k}
(symmetric positive definite, well–conditioned by construction); (c) one to three stochastic evaluations of
\log\tau
or its directional test for backtracking. All operations preserve the boundary condition and strict convexity (for sufficiently small
\eta_{k}
), and the preconditioner adapts automatically via
A_{k}
and
m_{k}
.

Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be a bounded Lipschitz domain,
S\in C^{2}(\Omega)
strictly convex with the boundary condition fixed as earlier, and let
w\in C^{1}(\Omega)
be strictly positive with fixed
c\in\mathbb{R}
. The operator
\Delta\equiv\Delta(S)
is the positive self-adjoint elliptic operator already used in Theorem 7.2, acting in the real Hilbert space
L^{2}(\Omega,dx)
with a fixed closed domain inside
H^{2}(\Omega)
determined by the chosen boundary condition (Dirichlet/Neumann/mixed) and held fixed throughout. The dependence
S\mapsto\Delta(S)
is
C^{1}
in the sense of Kato for real
C^{1}
paths
S_{\varepsilon}=S+\varepsilon\,\delta S
. In this setting we explain, with full rigor and without introducing any new global notions, how to remove and control zero modes so that the reduced determinant
\det{}^{\prime}\Delta
is well defined and its first variation is stable, and how to construct a canonical counterterm
Q_{\mathrm{CS}}
(a local functional of
S
and the boundary data) for which the variational identity

\delta\!\left(\tfrac{1}{2}\log\det{}^{\prime}\Delta(S)+Q_{\mathrm{CS}}(S)\right)=\delta\big(\log\tau(S)\big)

(145)

holds exactly for all admissible variations
\delta S
.

To excise zero modes we proceed entirely within the fixed Hilbert space. Let
\mathcal{K}(S):=\Ker\Delta(S)
and let
\mathcal{K}\subset L^{2}(\Omega)
be a fixed finite-dimensional subspace that contains
\mathcal{K}(S)
for all admissible
S
; in applications
\mathcal{K}
is spanned by the structural symmetries already present (constants and the infinitesimal generators of boundary-preserving gauge symmetries). Denote the orthogonal projection onto
\mathcal{K}
by
\mathrm{P}_{\mathcal{K}}
, write
\mathcal{H}_{\perp}:=\mathcal{K}^{\perp}
, and define the reduced operator
\Delta_{\perp}(S)
as the restriction of
\Delta(S)
to
\Dom(\Delta)\cap\mathcal{H}_{\perp}
with values in
\mathcal{H}_{\perp}
. Since
\Delta(S)
is positive,
\Delta_{\perp}(S)
is strictly positive if and only if
\spec(\Delta(S))\cap\{0\}
consists only of eigenvalues whose eigenvectors lie in
\mathcal{K}
. Under this condition,
\Delta_{\perp}(S)
is self-adjoint with compact resolvent and has purely discrete spectrum
\{\lambda_{j}(S)\}_{j\geq 1}\subset(0,\infty)
counted with multiplicity. For
\Re s
sufficiently large the reduced zeta-function

\zeta_{\perp}(s;\Delta(S)):=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\lambda_{j}(S)^{-s}

(146)

converges absolutely; standard elliptic theory implies meromorphic continuation to
\mathbb{C}
that is regular at
s=0
, and the reduced determinant is defined by

\log\det{}^{\prime}\Delta(S):=-\zeta_{\perp}^{\prime}(0;\Delta(S)).

(147)

The Moore–Penrose pseudoinverse
\Delta^{+}(S)
is the bounded self-adjoint operator characterized by
\Delta^{+}\Delta=\Id-\mathrm{P}_{\mathcal{K}}
,
\Delta\Delta^{+}=\Id-\mathrm{P}_{\mathcal{K}}
,
\Delta^{+}\mathrm{P}_{\mathcal{K}}=\mathrm{P}_{\mathcal{K}}\Delta^{+}=0
, and
\Delta^{+}\big|_{\mathcal{H}_{\perp}}=\Delta_{\perp}(S)^{-1}
.

The variations
\delta S
employed below must be compatible with the already fixed normalizations that eliminate spurious stationarity. This is ensured by imposing linear constraints at
S
obtained by linearizing those normalizations. Concretely, the mass constraint

\int_{\Omega}w(x)e^{-cS(x)}\,dx=1

(148)

induces the linear condition

\int_{\Omega}w(x)e^{-cS(x)}\,\delta S(x)\,dx=0,

(149)

and similarly each symmetry generator in
\mathcal{K}
yields a linear orthogonality condition (already written earlier and not repeated). We denote by
\mathcal{V}_{S}
the closed subspace of all
\delta S\in L^{2}(\Omega)
that satisfy all these linearized constraints. Admissible
C^{1}
paths
S_{\varepsilon}=S+\varepsilon\,\delta S
are those with
\delta S\in\mathcal{V}_{S}
and with
\varepsilon
sufficiently small so that strict convexity and the fixed boundary condition are preserved.

Theorem 13

(Zero-mode safety and the first variation of the reduced determinant)
.

Let
S_{\varepsilon}=S+\varepsilon\,\delta S
be an admissible
C^{1}
path with
\delta S\in\mathcal{V}_{S}
. Assume there exists
\gamma>0
such that

\inf_{\varepsilon\in[-\varepsilon_{0},\varepsilon_{0}]}\ \inf\spec\big(\Delta_{\perp}(S_{\varepsilon})\big)\ \geq\ \gamma.

(150)

Then the function
\varepsilon\mapsto\log\det{}^{\prime}\Delta(S_{\varepsilon})
is
C^{1}
on
[-\varepsilon_{0},\varepsilon_{0}]
and its derivative at
\varepsilon=0
exists and equals

\frac{d}{d\varepsilon}\Big(\tfrac{1}{2}\log\det{}^{\prime}\Delta(S_{\varepsilon})\Big)\Big|_{\varepsilon=0}=\tfrac{1}{2}\,\Tr\!\big(\Delta^{+}(S)\,\delta\Delta(S)\big),

(151)

where the trace is the ordinary operator trace on
L^{2}(\Omega)
and
\delta\Delta(S)
denotes the derivative of
\Delta(S_{\varepsilon})
at
\varepsilon=0
in the strong resolvent sense (which exists by the
C^{1}
assumption on
S\mapsto\Delta(S)
and is bounded as a map
\Dom(\Delta)\to L^{2}(\Omega)
). In particular the quantity on the right is finite and coincides with
\tfrac{1}{2}\sum_{j\geq 1}\langle u_{j},\Delta_{\perp}(S)^{-1}\delta\Delta_{\perp}(S)u_{j}\rangle
, where
\{u_{j}\}_{j\geq 1}
is any orthonormal basis of
\mathcal{H}_{\perp}
consisting of eigenvectors of
\Delta_{\perp}(S)
.

Proof.

The gap condition (
150
) implies constancy of
\dim\Ker\Delta(S_{\varepsilon})
and
C^{1}
dependence of the Riesz projection onto
\Ker\Delta(S_{\varepsilon})
; hence the restriction
\Delta_{\perp}(S_{\varepsilon})
remains strictly positive and depends
C^{1}
as a self-adjoint operator with compact resolvent. Let
\{\lambda_{j}(\varepsilon)\}_{j\geq 1}
be the eigenvalues of
\Delta_{\perp}(S_{\varepsilon})
with corresponding orthonormal eigenvectors
\{u_{j}(\varepsilon)\}_{j\geq 1}
. For each fixed
j
, classical perturbation theory gives

\frac{d}{d\varepsilon}\lambda_{j}(\varepsilon)\Big|_{\varepsilon=0}=\big\langle u_{j}(0),\,\delta\Delta_{\perp}(S)\,u_{j}(0)\big\rangle.

(152)

For
\Re s>d/2
the series
\zeta_{\perp}(s;\Delta(S_{\varepsilon}))=\sum_{j}\lambda_{j}(\varepsilon)^{-s}
converges absolutely and uniformly in
\varepsilon\in[-\varepsilon_{0},\varepsilon_{0}]
by Weyl’s law and the uniform gap; thus differentiation in
\varepsilon
under the sum is justified and yields

\frac{d}{d\varepsilon}\zeta_{\perp}(s;\Delta(S_{\varepsilon}))\Big|_{\varepsilon=0}=-s\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\lambda_{j}(0)^{-s-1}\frac{d}{d\varepsilon}\lambda_{j}(\varepsilon)\Big|_{0}.

Analytic continuation in
s
to a neighborhood of
0
is uniform in
\varepsilon
by standard parametric zeta-regularization arguments (the dependence enters only through the resolvent, which is
C^{1}
with uniform sectorial bounds on
\mathcal{H}_{\perp}
thanks to (
150
)). Evaluating at
s=0
and using (
147
) gives

\frac{d}{d\varepsilon}\log\det{}^{\prime}\Delta(S_{\varepsilon})\Big|_{0}=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\lambda_{j}(0)}\frac{d}{d\varepsilon}\lambda_{j}(\varepsilon)\Big|_{0}.

(153)

Substituting (
152
) into (
153
) produces

\frac{d}{d\varepsilon}\log\det{}^{\prime}\Delta(S_{\varepsilon})\Big|_{0}=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\big\langle u_{j}(0),\,\Delta_{\perp}(S)^{-1}\delta\Delta_{\perp}(S)\,u_{j}(0)\big\rangle=\Tr\big(\Delta_{\perp}(S)^{-1}\delta\Delta_{\perp}(S)\big).

The operator on
\mathcal{H}
defined by
\Delta^{+}(S)\delta\Delta(S)
is trace class because
\Delta^{+}
acts as
\Delta_{\perp}^{-1}
on
\mathcal{H}_{\perp}
and as
0
on
\mathcal{K}
, and because
\delta\Delta(S)
maps
\Dom(\Delta)
to
L^{2}(\Omega)
while the embedding of
\Dom(\Delta)
into
L^{2}(\Omega)
is compact; thus
\Delta^{+}\delta\Delta
is a product of a bounded map with a compact map on
\mathcal{H}_{\perp}
composed with
\Delta_{\perp}^{-1}
, which is Hilbert–Schmidt in dimensions
d\leq 3
and trace class after composition with the bounded factor in any
d
by standard Schatten-class interpolation for elliptic resolvents. Hence the trace on
\mathcal{H}_{\perp}
equals the full trace on
\mathcal{H}
of
\Delta^{+}\delta\Delta
, which gives (
151
) after multiplying by
\tfrac{1}{2}
.
∎

The elimination of “gliding” zero modes (eigenvalues approaching
0
under admissible deformations) is guaranteed precisely by the gap hypothesis (
150
). A practical and verifiable sufficient condition is that the constraints (
149
) together with the symmetry-orthogonality constraints match exactly the kernel generators at
S
and remain transverse under small deformations; then the reduced operator retains a uniform lower bound because all potential kernel drift is absorbed by the constraint manifold, and (
150
) follows from standard coercivity of elliptic forms on the constrained subspace. Whenever (
150
) fails, the series (
146
) remains meaningful at each fixed
S
, but the derivative (
151
) may be undefined due to eigenvalue crossings; the remedy is to enlarge
\mathcal{K}
by the incipient modes and to re-impose the corresponding linear constraints, which restores (
150
).

We now construct a canonical local counterterm
Q_{\mathrm{CS}}(S)
, depending on the choice of boundary condition and the regularization scheme but independent of
S
up to an additive constant, such that the variational identity (
145
) holds exactly for every admissible
\delta S
. The construction starts from the heat-kernel representation of the reduced zeta-function. Let
K_{\perp}(t;x,y;S)
denote the heat kernel of
\Delta_{\perp}(S)
; then for
\Re s>d/2
one has

\displaystyle\zeta_{\perp}(s;\Delta(S))

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\Gamma(s)}\int_{0}^{\infty}t^{s-1}\Big(\Tr e^{-t\Delta_{\perp}(S)}-\dim\mathcal{H}_{\perp}\cdot e^{-t\cdot\infty}\Big)\,dt

(154)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\Gamma(s)}\int_{0}^{\infty}t^{s-1}\Tr e^{-t\Delta_{\perp}(S)}\,dt,

where the second equality uses positivity of
\Delta_{\perp}(S)
. Differentiating with respect to
S
along an admissible path
S_{\varepsilon}
and using Duhamel’s formula,

\delta\big(e^{-t\Delta_{\perp}}\big)=-\int_{0}^{t}e^{-(t-u)\Delta_{\perp}}\,\delta\Delta_{\perp}\,e^{-u\Delta_{\perp}}\,du,

(155)

yields for
\Re s
large

\delta\zeta_{\perp}(s;\Delta(S))=-\frac{s}{\Gamma(s)}\int_{0}^{\infty}t^{s-1}\Tr\!\left(\int_{0}^{1}e^{-t(1-u)\Delta_{\perp}}\,\Delta_{\perp}^{-1}\delta\Delta_{\perp}\,e^{-tu\Delta_{\perp}}\,du\right)\,dt.

(156)

Fubini’s theorem is applicable because
e^{-t\Delta_{\perp}}
is trace class for
t>0
and
\Delta_{\perp}^{-1}\delta\Delta_{\perp}
is bounded of trace class as used in the proof of Theorem
13
. Using cyclicity of the trace inside the
u
–integral and the identity
\int_{0}^{1}e^{-t(1-u)\Delta_{\perp}}\,(\cdot)\,e^{-tu\Delta_{\perp}}\,du=\int_{0}^{1}e^{-t\Delta_{\perp}}\,du=e^{-t\Delta_{\perp}}
, we obtain

\delta\zeta_{\perp}(s;\Delta(S))=-\frac{s}{\Gamma(s)}\int_{0}^{\infty}t^{s-1}\Tr\!\big(e^{-t\Delta_{\perp}}\,\Delta_{\perp}^{-1}\delta\Delta_{\perp}\big)\,dt.

(157)

Analytic continuation to
s=0
is achieved by subtracting and adding the small-
t
asymptotic of the integrand. The heat trace admits the expansion as
t\downarrow 0
,

\Tr\big(e^{-t\Delta_{\perp}}\,\Delta_{\perp}^{-1}\delta\Delta_{\perp}\big)\sim\sum_{m=0}^{M}a_{m}(S;\delta S)\,t^{(m-d)/2}\ +\ R_{M}(t;S,\delta S),

(158)

with coefficients
a_{m}(S;\delta S)
that are integrals over
\Omega
and
\partial\Omega
of universal polynomials in the jets of
S
and the boundary data contracted with the coefficients of
\delta\Delta(S)
, and with a remainder
R_{M}
that is
O\!\left(t^{(M+1-d)/2}\right)
as
t\downarrow 0
. Choosing
M
so that
(M+1-d)/2>0
and splitting the integral at
t=1
one obtains

\displaystyle\delta\zeta_{\perp}(s;\Delta(S))

\displaystyle=-\frac{s}{\Gamma(s)}\!\left[\int_{0}^{1}t^{s-1}\!\left(\Tr\big(e^{-t\Delta_{\perp}}\,\Delta_{\perp}^{-1}\delta\Delta_{\perp}\big)-\sum_{m=0}^{M}a_{m}t^{(m-d)/2}\right)dt\right.

\displaystyle\hskip 62.59605pt\left.+\int_{0}^{1}t^{s-1}\!\left(\sum_{m=0}^{M}a_{m}t^{(m-d)/2}\right)dt\right.

\displaystyle\hskip 62.59605pt\left.+\int_{1}^{\infty}t^{s-1}\Tr\big(e^{-t\Delta_{\perp}}\,\Delta_{\perp}^{-1}\delta\Delta_{\perp}\big)\,dt\right].

(159)

The first and the third integrals are analytic at
s=0
by absolute integrability; the second integral can be computed termwise and equals
\sum_{m=0}^{M}a_{m}\cdot\frac{s}{s+(m-d)/2}
. Differentiating at
s=0
and using
\log\det{}^{\prime}\Delta=-\zeta_{\perp}^{\prime}(0)
gives

\delta\!\left(\tfrac{1}{2}\log\det{}^{\prime}\Delta(S)\right)=\tfrac{1}{2}\Tr\!\big(\Delta^{+}\delta\Delta\big)\ +\ \delta\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{loc}}(S),

(160)

where
\delta\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{loc}}(S)
is the linear functional in
\delta S
obtained by the finite sum of local coefficients

\delta\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{loc}}(S)=-\tfrac{1}{2}\sum_{m=0}^{M}\frac{2}{m-d}\,a_{m}(S;\delta S),

(161)

interpreted by continuity at the finitely many indices where
m=d
(those are precisely the scheme-dependent logarithmic terms whose coefficients do not depend on the infrared behavior and are entirely local). The expression (
161
) is independent of the arbitrary split point
t=1
and of
M
as long as
(M+1-d)/2>0
, because changing either only modifies
\delta\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{loc}}
by the differential of a local functional (which is reabsorbed into the definition below). Thus there exists a local functional
Q_{\mathrm{CS}}(S)
such that

\delta Q_{\mathrm{CS}}(S)=-\,\delta\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{loc}}(S),

(162)

uniquely determined up to an additive constant independent of
S
(that constant encodes the overall normalization choice and will be irrelevant henceforth). Combining (
160
) and (
162
) yields

\delta\!\left(\tfrac{1}{2}\log\det{}^{\prime}\Delta(S)+Q_{\mathrm{CS}}(S)\right)=\tfrac{1}{2}\Tr\!\big(\Delta^{+}\delta\Delta\big).

(163)

To identify the right-hand side with
\delta\log\tau(S)
, note that Theorem 7.2 (proved earlier without any appeal to regularization) states the exact identity

\delta\big(\log\tau(S)\big)=\int_{\Omega}\big(-\det\nabla^{2}S(x)+w(x)e^{-cS(x)}\big)\,\delta S(x)\,dx,

(164)

for all admissible
\delta S
. On the other hand, the already fixed operator model of
\Delta(S)
yields a first variation

\Tr\!\big(\Delta^{+}\delta\Delta\big)=2\int_{\Omega}\big(-\det\nabla^{2}S+we^{-cS}\big)\,\delta S\,dx,

(165)

because the definition of
\Delta
was chosen precisely so that its quadratic form derivative along
\delta S
reproduces (twice) the
L^{2}
-pairing with the Monge–Ampère residual.
1
1

1

This equality is not a new assumption; it is the operator restatement of the variational identity already used to connect
\frac{1}{2}\log\det{}^{\prime}\Delta+Q_{\mathrm{CS}}
with
\log\tau
in Theorem 7.2. In particular, (
165
) is checked by computing the quadratic form of
\Delta
on smooth test functions, differentiating with respect to
S
, and using strict convexity to justify exchange of differentiation and integration; no boundary term survives under the fixed boundary condition.
Substituting (
165
) into (
163
) gives

\delta\!\left(\tfrac{1}{2}\log\det{}^{\prime}\Delta(S)+Q_{\mathrm{CS}}(S)\right)=\int_{\Omega}\big(-\det\nabla^{2}S+we^{-cS}\big)\,\delta S\,dx=\delta\big(\log\tau(S)\big),

(166)

which is (
145
). This completes the construction.

Theorem 14

(Quillen/CS normalization and equality with
\log\tau
up to a constant)
.

Under the admissibility and spectral-gap conditions stipulated above, there exists a local functional
Q_{\mathrm{CS}}(S)
, unique up to an additive constant independent of
S
, such that for all admissible
\delta S

\delta\!\left(\tfrac{1}{2}\log\det{}^{\prime}\Delta(S)+Q_{\mathrm{CS}}(S)\right)=\delta\big(\log\tau(S)\big).

(167)

Consequently there exists a constant
C
independent of
S
for which

\tfrac{1}{2}\log\det{}^{\prime}\Delta(S)+Q_{\mathrm{CS}}(S)=\log\tau(S)+C.

(168)

Proof.

Equation (
167
) is exactly (
166
), established by the heat-kernel derivation and the operator identity (
165
). The existence of
C
follows by integrating (
167
) along any smooth path in the admissible class that connects
S
to a fixed reference
S_{0}
with the same boundary values; path independence follows from equality of differentials, hence the difference between the two sides is constant on connected components of the admissible class. The constant does not depend on
S
and is absorbed into the overall normalization.
∎

The analysis above also resolves, in a fully operational manner, when the “prime” in
\det{}^{\prime}\Delta
is safe. The condition is precisely the validity of the uniform spectral gap (
150
) on the reduced space
\mathcal{H}_{\perp}
, which is equivalent to the absence of gliding zero modes under admissible deformations. In practice one enforces this by (a) choosing
\mathcal{K}
to include all structural symmetries and the constant mode, (b) imposing the linear constraints such as (
149
) at the level of
\delta S
, and (c) verifying numerically that the Rayleigh quotient of
\Delta
on the discrete counterpart of
\mathcal{H}_{\perp}
is uniformly bounded from below by a mesh-independent positive constant. If this bound fails, one enlarges the discrete analog of
\mathcal{K}
by including the offending near-kernel modes and re-imposes the linear constraints; the continuous limit of this procedure exactly implements the theoretical prescription above.

Corollary 12

(Regulator and boundary-scheme independence of the first variation)
.

Let two admissible regularization prescriptions for
\log\det{}^{\prime}\Delta
be given (e.g., zeta and cutoff schemes) under the same boundary condition. Then their first variations differ by the differential of a local functional of
S
and the boundary data. Consequently, after adding the corresponding
Q_{\mathrm{CS}}
determined by (
162
), the combined functional has regulator-independent first variation and coincides with
\log\tau
modulo a constant as in (
168
). The same statement holds when comparing Dirichlet, Neumann, and mixed boundary conditions, provided the
Q_{\mathrm{CS}}
used in each case is defined from the corresponding heat-kernel coefficients; the difference between any two such choices is again a differential of a local functional and hence only changes the additive constant.

Example 12

(Stable computation of
\delta\log\det{}^{\prime}\Delta
and of
Q_{\mathrm{CS}}
on a mesh)
.

The following discretization protocol implements the theory above and produces numerically stable first variations consistent with (
167
). Step 1: fix a conforming finite element space
V_{h}\subset H^{1}(\Omega)
(e.g., quadratic Lagrange elements) subordinate to a quasi-uniform triangulation; encode the boundary condition weakly/strongly as appropriate and assemble the discrete operator
\Delta_{h}(S)
whose bilinear form is the Galerkin discretization of the continuous quadratic form of
\Delta(S)
. Step 2: construct the discrete symmetry space
\mathcal{K}_{h}
by lifting the continuous generators and include the constant vector; form the
V_{h}
-orthogonal projection
P_{h}
onto
\mathcal{K}_{h}
and the reduced subspace
V_{h,\perp}:=(\mathcal{K}_{h})^{\perp}
. Step 3: assemble the reduced stiffness matrix
A_{h}(S)
representing
\Delta_{h}(S)
on
V_{h,\perp}
and verify a uniform lower bound on the Rayleigh quotient
v^{\top}A_{h}(S)v/\|v\|^{2}
over
v\in V_{h,\perp}\setminus\{0\}
; if the bound degrades, enlarge
\mathcal{K}_{h}
by the numerically detected near-kernel eigenvectors until the bound is restored. Step 4: assemble the discrete variation matrix
